Tokens, lexemes & patterns · 22
CD-U1-Q01MCQeasy

Separating lexical analysis from syntax analysis is standard compiler practice. Which of the following is NOT one of the standard reasons for the separation?

  1. It simplifies the overall design of the compiler.
  2. It allows specialised, efficient techniques for buffering and scanning the source text.
  3. It improves portability, because the peculiarities of the input alphabet are isolated in one place.
  4. It removes all ambiguity from the grammar before the parser sees it.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The three classical reasons for a separate lexical phase are simplicity of design, compiler efficiency (specialised buffering and scanning) and compiler portability (the input-alphabet peculiarities are confined to the lexer).

Step by step.

  1. Options (A), (B) and (C) are exactly those three reasons.
  2. Ambiguity is a property of the grammar, decided by the parser; no scanner can change the set of parse trees a grammar admits.
  3. A lexer can reject an ill-formed lexeme, but it cannot decide whether the grammar is ambiguous.

Final answer. Option (D) — removing ambiguity is not a reason for separating lexical analysis.

Common misconception. Believing that the lexer "resolves" grammar-level conflicts. The lexer classifies characters into tokens; ambiguity of the grammar survives it untouched.

CD-U1-Q02MCQeasy

A scanner uses the maximal-munch (longest-match) rule and has token classes for identifiers, ++, + and =. For the input a+++b, which token sequence does it return?

  1. a  ++  +  b
  2. a  +  ++  b
  3. a  +  +  +  b
  4. a  +++  b
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Maximal munch: at every position the scanner takes the longest prefix that is a valid token; only when several rules match the same longest prefix does rule order break the tie.

Step by step.

  1. Position 1: a starts an identifier; an identifier cannot contain +, so the token is a.
  2. Position 2: the characters are +++. The longest valid token here is ++ (two characters), so it is returned.
  3. Position 4: the single + remains and is returned as +.
  4. Position 5: b is an identifier.
  5. Result: a, ++, +, b.

Final answer. Option (A): a ++ + b.

Common misconception. Splitting greedily as + + + (option C) ignores maximal munch, and treating +++ as one token (option D) invents a token class that does not exist.

CD-U1-Q03MCQeasy

For the source line int sum = 0; a conventional lexer inserts how many new entries into the symbol table while scanning it?

  1. 0
  2. 1
  3. 2
  4. 3
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The symbol table holds information about identifiers. Keywords are recognised by the lexer as fixed token classes and are not entered as identifiers.

Step by step.

  1. int — a keyword: returned as the token class INT, no symbol-table entry.
  2. sum — an identifier: one entry is created (its lexeme, type, later its scope and address).
  3. = and ; — punctuation tokens, no entry.
  4. 0 — a constant; constants are usually kept in a separate literal/constant table, not the symbol table.
  5. Total new symbol-table entries: 1.

Final answer. Option (B) — exactly one entry, for sum.

Common misconception. Counting the keyword int (giving 2) or the constant 0 (giving 3). Keywords are not identifiers, and constants belong to a separate table.

CD-U1-Q04MCQmedium

Which of the following decisions cannot be made by the lexical analyser alone?

  1. Whether a sequence of characters is a well-formed identifier of the language.
  2. Whether an identifier used in an expression has been declared earlier in the program.
  3. Whether a sequence of characters forms a valid numeric literal of the language.
  4. Whether a comment opened near the end of the file is still unterminated.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A lexer classifies characters into tokens using regular expressions. Anything that needs information gathered across the whole program — such as which identifiers are declared, and in which scope — is outside the regular-power of a scanner and belongs to semantic analysis with a symbol table.

Step by step.

  1. Well-formedness of an identifier (A) is exactly a regular-expression test, so the lexer decides it.
  2. A valid numeric literal (C) is again a regular language; the lexer decides it.
  3. An unterminated comment (D) is detected while matching the comment pattern at end of file — a lexical error.
  4. Declaration-before-use (B) needs a symbol table filled in program order and a notion of scope; a scanner cannot answer it, and a one-token lookahead certainly cannot.

Final answer. Option (B) — declaration checking is a semantic (parser/semantic-analyser) task, not a lexical one.

Common misconception. Assuming that because the lexer fills a symbol table it can also check declarations. The lexer records identifiers; it does not reason about their declarations.

CD-U1-Q13MCQhard

A scanner lists its rules in this order: (1) [a-z]+ → return ID; (2) if → return IF. In a Lex/Flex-style scanner, what happens on the input if?

  1. IF is returned, because keyword rules automatically outrank identifier rules.
  2. ID is returned, because both rules match the same two-character lexeme and the earlier rule wins the tie, so rule (2) never fires.
  3. A lexical error is reported, because two rules match the same text.
  4. ID is returned for if but IF for a longer word such as iffy.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Rule selection is two-stage: (i) choose the longest match among all rules; (ii) if several rules match that same longest lexeme, the rule written earliest wins.

Step by step.

  1. Both rules match exactly two characters of if — neither match is longer.
  2. The tie is therefore broken by rule order, and rule (1) is earlier.
  3. So the scanner returns ID and rule (2) is dead code: no input can ever make it fire.
  4. The correct specification lists the keyword rule before the identifier rule.

Final answer. Option (B).

Common misconception. Assuming that keyword recognition is automatic, or that a "more specific" pattern outranks a more general one. Lex has no specificity rule — only longest match, then earliest rule. Option (D) is also wrong because iffy matches only [a-z]+ (longer than if).

CD-U1-Q16MSQeasy

Which of the following statements about lexemes and tokens are true?

  1. Two different lexemes can belong to the same token class.
  2. A token is a pair: a token name (an abstract class) and an optional attribute value; a lexeme is the concrete string matched.
  3. Every token returned for a keyword must carry an attribute value.
  4. A correct scanner assigns each lexeme to exactly one token class, because longest-match and rule-priority resolve any overlap.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A token class is a set of lexemes; the lexer returns a token name plus, when needed, an attribute that distinguishes the members of that class.

Step by step.

  1. (A) True: count1 and total are different lexemes of the same class id.
  2. (B) True: that is the standard definition — e.g. <id, pointer to symbol-table entry>.
  3. (C) False: keywords and punctuation need no attribute; the token name alone identifies them.
  4. (D) True: the two conventions (longest match, then earliest rule) make the scanner deterministic, so a lexeme such as if cannot be both a keyword and an identifier.

Final answer. Options (A), (B) and (D).

Common misconception. Statement (C) is the trap: attributes exist to distinguish members of a class (which identifier? which constant?). A keyword has no members to distinguish.

CD-U1-Q20MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about the lexer–parser interface are true?

  1. A lexer must produce the complete token stream for a file before the parser can begin.
  2. A token may carry an attribute value, for example a pointer to the symbol-table entry of an identifier.
  3. Deciding whether an identifier has been declared before its use is part of lexical analysis.
  4. In a single-pass design the parser may keep one token of lookahead, which amounts to pushing a token back to the lexer.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The parser normally pulls tokens on demand through a procedure such as getNextToken(), and the two phases communicate through tokens with attributes.

Step by step.

  1. (A) False: production compilers are demand-driven; buffering the whole file as tokens wastes memory and delays error reporting.
  2. (B) True: <id, p>, where p points at the identifier's symbol-table entry, is the standard example.
  3. (C) False: declaration checking needs the symbol table built across statements — a semantic task.
  4. (D) True: a one-token pushback is a common way to give the parser the lookahead it needs.

Final answer. Options (B) and (D).

Common misconception. Statement (A) is the trap: it describes a batch design nobody uses, and it would make single-pass compilation impossible.

CD-U1-Q21NATeasy

How many tokens does a C lexer return for the input line total = count1 + 42;? Count keywords, identifiers, operators, constants and punctuation as one token each, and ignore whitespace.

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Every lexeme that is not whitespace or a comment becomes exactly one token; whitespace between tokens is discarded by the scanner.

Step by step.

  1. total — identifier (1)
  2. = — assignment operator (2)
  3. count1 — identifier (3)
  4. + — additive operator (4)
  5. 42 — integer constant (5)
  6. ; — punctuation (6)
  7. Whitespace contributes no token.

Final answer. 6 tokens.

Common misconception. Counting the four spaces as tokens, or merging count1 into one token with +. Whitespace is skipped, and each maximal lexeme is a separate token.

CD-U1-Q26MCQeasy

Which one of the following is not passed from the lexical analyser to the syntax analyser as a token?

  1. A keyword such as while
  2. An identifier such as total
  3. A comment such as /* note */
  4. A relational operator such as <=
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A token is the pair (token name, attribute value). White space and comments are recognised by the scanner only so that they can be discarded; they never reach the parser.

Step by step.

  1. The scanner reads characters and groups them into lexemes.
  2. Each lexeme that matches a token pattern is returned as a token name with an optional attribute value.
  3. Comments and blank space have patterns too, but their only purpose is to be skipped.
  4. So a comment is consumed by the lexer and is not a token.

Final answer. Option (C) — a comment is not a token.

Common misconception. A comment is made of characters and is matched by a regular expression, so it feels like a token. Being recognised by the lexer and being handed to the parser are different things.

CD-U1-Q27MCQeasy

Three ideas are involved in scanning: a token, a pattern and a lexeme. A lexeme is

  1. a regular expression that describes every string of a token class
  2. the actual sequence of characters in the source text that matches the pattern of a token
  3. the internal integer code that a compiler assigns to a token class
  4. the list of attributes kept for an identifier in the symbol table
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Pattern, lexeme and token form a three-level hierarchy: the pattern is the description, the lexeme is the concrete string, and the token is the abstract class handed to the parser.

Step by step.

  1. Pattern: the form that the lexemes of a token may take, written as a regular expression.
  2. Lexeme: a sequence of characters in the source program that matches a pattern.
  3. Token: the abstract class returned to the parser.
  4. Hence a lexeme is the actual character sequence.

Final answer. Option (B).

Common misconception. Option (A) is the pattern and option (C) is the token code. Collapsing the three levels is the usual mistake.

CD-U1-Q28MCQmedium

The scanner returns a pair (token name, attribute value). For an identifier token the attribute value is normally

  1. the token name repeated, so that the parser can compare names
  2. a pointer to the entry for that lexeme in the symbol table
  3. the line number on which the identifier occurs
  4. empty, because identifiers carry no information
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Attribute values distinguish different lexemes that share one token name. Every identifier maps to the token name id, so the attribute must identify which identifier it is.

Step by step.

  1. All identifiers share the token name id.
  2. Without an attribute, count and total would be indistinguishable to the parser.
  3. The scanner therefore returns a pointer to the symbol-table entry holding the lexeme and its attributes.
  4. The line number is used for diagnostics and is not the attribute of the identifier.

Final answer. Option (B).

Common misconception. Thinking the attribute is the character string itself. Implementations may store the string in the symbol table and pass a pointer; the attribute is the symbol-table entry, not the token name.

CD-U1-Q29MCQmedium

The C statement x = -1; is scanned with maximal munch. How many tokens does the scanner produce? Treat -= as a valid C operator, and ignore white space.

  1. 3
  2. 4
  3. 5
  4. 6
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Maximal munch takes the longest prefix that matches some pattern. A compound operator exists only if it really is a token of the language, so =- cannot be formed.

Step by step.

  1. At x the identifier pattern matches just x, giving token 1.
  2. At = the scanner tries the two-character candidate =-; no such operator exists, so the one-character = is taken. Token 2.
  3. The - is then the unary minus operator. Token 3.
  4. The constant 1 is token 4 and the ; is token 5.
  5. Total: x, =, -, 1, ; — five tokens.

Final answer. Option (C) — 5 tokens.

Common misconception. Answering 4 by treating =- as one operator, or 3 by assuming the unary minus belongs to the constant. The minus sign is a token in its own right.

CD-U1-Q30MSQeasy

Which of the following are tokens of the C language? (Select all that apply.)

  1. int
  2. 42
  3. /* note */
  4. &&
  5. a newline character
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The token classes of a language are keywords, identifiers, constants, string literals, operators and punctuators. White space and comments are not token classes.

Step by step.

  1. int is a keyword, hence a token.
  2. 42 is an integer constant, hence a token.
  3. /* note */ is a comment: recognised only so that it can be discarded.
  4. && is a logical operator, hence a token.
  5. A newline is white space: skipped, not a token.

Final answer. Options (A), (B) and (D).

Common misconception. Counting comments or white space as tokens, which inflates every token-count answer by one or two.

CD-U1-Q31MSQhard

Which of the following tasks belong to the lexical analyser? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Stripping comments and white space before the tokens reach the parser.
  2. Entering each new identifier into the symbol table.
  3. Deciding whether the grammar of the language is ambiguous.
  4. Recording the line number so that later phases can report positions.
  5. Performing type checking on expressions.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The scanner does character-level work: it groups characters into lexemes, discards noise, records positions and maintains the symbol table. Grammar-level and type-level decisions belong to later phases.

Step by step.

  1. Stripping comments and white space: a scanner task, since only the scanner sees raw characters.
  2. Inserting identifiers into the symbol table: done while scanning, so each identifier is entered once.
  3. Deciding whether the grammar is ambiguous: a property of the grammar, decided by the parser designer — no scanner can affect it.
  4. Recording line numbers: the scanner tracks yylineno for error messages.
  5. Type checking: a semantic-analysis task, performed on the syntax tree.

Final answer. Options (A), (B) and (D).

Common misconception. Assuming the scanner 'resolves' ambiguity. Ambiguity is a property of the grammar; separating phases cannot remove it.

CD-U1-Q32NATeasy

How many tokens does a C lexer return for the statement a[i] = b + 1;? Count keywords, identifiers, operators, constants and punctuation as one token each, and ignore white space. (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Token counting: every keyword, identifier, constant, operator and punctuator counts once; white space and comments count zero.

Step by step.

  1. Scan left to right under maximal munch.
  2. Tokens: a, [, i, ], =, b, +, 1, ;.
  3. No two adjacent characters here combine into a longer token.
  4. Count: 9.

Final answer. 9

Common misconception. Merging the brackets into a single 'bracket' token, or counting the spaces. Each punctuator is its own token.

CD-U1-Q51MCQeasy

Which one of the following is a lexical error rather than a syntactic or a semantic error?

  1. use of an identifier that was never declared
  2. a string literal that is still open when the end of the line is reached
  3. a missing semicolon at the end of an expression statement
  4. an else with no matching if
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The scanner only groups characters into tokens; a defect in the spelling of a single token, so that no token pattern can match it, is lexical. Errors in how tokens are arranged belong to the parser, and errors about meaning belong to later phases.

Step by step.

  1. A string that is never closed cannot match the string-literal pattern, so the scanner fails inside one lexeme: lexical error.
  2. An undeclared identifier is a well-formed token; only the symbol table can reject it: semantic error.
  3. A missing semicolon is a defect in the arrangement of tokens: the parser rejects it.
  4. An else with no if is also a defect in the arrangement of tokens: the parser rejects it.

Final answer. Option (B) — an unterminated string literal is a lexical error.

Common misconception. Assuming that any error reported early in compilation is lexical. The phase that owns the construct decides: token spelling is the scanner's business, token arrangement the parser's, and meaning everybody else's.

CD-U1-Q52MCQeasy

In a scanner generated from regular definitions, reserved words such as while are normally recognised

  1. by giving every keyword its own separate branch of the DFA
  2. by matching the identifier pattern first and then looking the matched lexeme up in a table of reserved words
  3. by leaving them to the parser, which treats any identifier spelt while as a keyword
  4. by listing the keyword rules after the identifier rule, so that the later rule wins on a tie
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A keyword has exactly the same shape as an identifier, so one automaton can recognise both. The standard implementation matches the identifier pattern and then consults a reserved-word table; the keyword is simply an identifier whose entry in that table already exists.

Step by step.

  1. The pattern for an identifier already covers every keyword, so no extra automaton branch is needed (and one branch per keyword would grow the DFA for no gain).
  2. After the lexeme is matched, a table lookup decides whether the token name is ID or WHILE.
  3. Leaving keywords to the parser would force the grammar to know every reserved word and would break the clean separation of phases.
  4. On a tie in length the earlier rule wins, so placing keyword rules after the identifier rule would make them unreachable.

Final answer. Option (B) — match as an identifier, then look the lexeme up in the reserved-word table.

Common misconception. Believing that a keyword needs its own DFA path, or that a later rule can override an earlier one of the same length. Rule order only breaks ties, and it breaks them in favour of the rule written first.

CD-U1-Q53MCQmedium

A scanner for a simplified C knows exactly two patterns for multi-character tokens, NUM = [0-9]+ and ID = [A-Za-z_][A-Za-z0-9_]*, treats int as a keyword, and applies maximal munch. Which token sequence does it return for int a = 0x1F; ?

  1. int · a · = · NUM(0x1F) · ;
  2. int · a · = · NUM(0) · ID(x1F) · ;
  3. int · a · = · NUM(0) · ID(x) · NUM(1) · ID(F) · ;
  4. ID(int) · ID(a) · = · NUM(0) · ID(x1F) · ;
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Maximal munch takes the longest prefix that matches some pattern, and the scanner has no hexadecimal pattern: a number is only [0-9]+.

Step by step.

  1. At the character 0 the longest numeric match is 0 itself, because x is not a digit.
  2. Scanning restarts at x; maximal munch then takes the whole identifier x1F, since letters and digits may follow the first letter.
  3. So the sequence is int, a, =, NUM(0), ID(x1F), ; — six tokens.
  4. Option (A) assumes a hexadecimal pattern that this scanner does not have; (C) breaks x1F into three tokens against maximal munch; (D) forgets that int is a keyword, so it would be returned as a keyword token, not as ID.

Final answer. Option (B) — NUM(0) followed by ID(x1F).

Common misconception. Reading 0x1F as one number because C allows hexadecimal. The question is about the patterns this scanner actually has, and a scanner can only recognise what its patterns describe.

CD-U1-Q54MCQmedium

Why is it the lexical analyser, and not the parser, that removes blanks, tabs, newlines and comments?

  1. Because these strings are not tokens of the language, and a parser that saw them would need a production for them in every syntactic construct
  2. Because removing them changes the meaning of the operators that surround them
  3. Because comments may nest, and only a scanner can count nesting depth
  4. Because removing them reduces the number of token classes that the parser must know
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Blanks and comments carry no syntactic information. The scanner discards them so that the token stream handed to the parser contains only the constructs the grammar describes.

Step by step.

  1. A grammar written for the token stream has no production for whitespace or comments; if they reached the parser, every production would have to tolerate them, which bloats the grammar for no benefit.
  2. Whitespace never changes the meaning of an operator (that is decided by maximal munch and by rule order), so (B) is false.
  3. Nesting is not the reason — indeed a nesting comment cannot be handled by a regular-expression scanner at all, so (C) is false.
  4. The parser sees token classes either way; discarding whitespace removes token instances, not classes, so (D) is false.

Final answer. Option (A) — whitespace and comments are not tokens, so the scanner strips them and keeps the grammar clean.

Common misconception. Thinking that the parser could simply skip blanks itself. It could, but only at the cost of writing whitespace into every production; that is exactly the design the separation avoids.

CD-U1-Q55MSQeasy

Which of the following are lexical errors? (Select all that apply.)

  1. a block comment that is opened with /* and never closed
  2. a character such as $ that matches no token pattern of the language
  3. a string literal that contains an escaped quote, written \"
  4. an assignment to a variable that was declared const
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A lexical error is a defect the scanner can detect while forming tokens: no pattern matches the input at the current position, or a token is left unfinished.

Step by step.

  1. (A) An unclosed comment runs to end of file without ever matching the comment pattern, so the scanner cannot finish the token: lexical error.
  2. (B) No pattern matches $, so the scanner has no token to return: lexical error.
  3. (C) An escaped quote is part of a well-formed string literal — the pattern "([^"\\]|\\.)*" accepts it: not an error.
  4. (D) Assigning to a const object is perfectly well formed at the token level; it is rejected during semantic analysis: not a lexical error.

Final answer. Options (A) and (B).

Common misconception. Treating every complaint about a single character as lexical. The test is whether a token pattern fails, not whether the character is unusual.

CD-U1-Q56MCQhard

A language allows block comments to nest: (* ... (* ... *) ... *). A scanner built only from regular expressions, as in Lex/Flex, cannot recognise such comments because

  1. a regular expression cannot express the requirement that the number of closing delimiters equal the number of opening ones, which needs unbounded counting
  2. the delimiters contain parentheses, which are metacharacters that a regular expression cannot match
  3. the DFA would need more than one final state, and a DFA may have only one
  4. maximal munch would consume the rest of the file after the first opening delimiter
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The set of correctly nested bracket strings is not regular: by the pumping lemma a finite automaton cannot keep an unbounded count of unmatched openers. Regular expressions denote exactly the regular languages, so no regular expression describes nested comments.

Step by step.

  1. Write the nesting depth as a number. A DFA has finitely many states, so it cannot distinguish every depth; two depths must collapse into one state, and then a string with one bracket too many is wrongly accepted or rejected.
  2. That is the pumping-lemma argument: (*^n *) requires n to be counted without bound.
  3. Practical scanners handle it with a start condition plus a depth counter kept in C code inside the rule actions — the counter, not the regular expression, does the work.
  4. Options (B), (C) and (D) are false: parentheses can be escaped (\(), a DFA may have any number of final states, and maximal munch stops as soon as no transition exists.

Final answer. Option (A) — balanced nesting needs unbounded counting, so the language is not regular.

Common misconception. Believing that "regular expressions can match anything you can write down". Nested, matched delimiters are the standard example of a construct that a finite automaton cannot handle.

CD-U1-Q57NATeasy

A scanner returns a token name and an attribute value for every lexeme. For the source line total = total + 1; how many distinct token names does the scanner return? The line yields six tokens; count how many different token names appear. (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A token name (token class) is the abstract kind; a lexeme is the concrete text. Two occurrences of the same identifier produce two tokens but one token name.

Step by step.

  1. The six tokens are: total, =, total, +, 1, ;.
  2. Their token names are ID, ASSIGN, ID, PLUS, NUM, SEMI.
  3. The name ID appears twice, so the distinct names are ID, ASSIGN, PLUS, NUM and SEMI: five.
  4. The attribute value distinguishes the two occurrences of total (both point at the same symbol-table entry) and records 1 for the number.

Final answer. Five distinct token names (ID, ASSIGN, PLUS, NUM, SEMI) for six tokens.

Common misconception. Counting tokens when the question asks for token names, or counting attribute values as part of the name. The name is the class; the attribute is the extra information attached to an instance.

Regular expressions to lexers · 21
CD-U1-Q05MCQeasy

Which regular expression denotes exactly the set of identifiers that begin with a letter and continue with letters or digits (one or more characters in all)?

  1. [A-Za-z][A-Za-z0-9]*
  2. [A-Za-z]*[A-Za-z0-9]*
  3. [A-Za-z0-9]+
  4. [A-Za-z][A-Za-z]*
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The first character must come from the letter class and the remaining characters from the letter-or-digit class, with zero or more remaining characters.

Step by step.

  1. (A) forces one letter, then any number of letters or digits — exactly the required language.
  2. (B) lets the first part match the empty string, so the digit part can supply a leading digit (9x is matched); it also matches the empty string.
  3. (C) allows an identifier to start with a digit (1abc).
  4. (D) forbids digits anywhere after the first character.

Final answer. Option (A).

Common misconception. Option (B) is the classic trap: writing two stars in sequence does not impose a constraint on the first character, because the first star may match nothing.

CD-U1-Q06MCQmedium

How many states does the minimal DFA accepting the language denoted by (a|b)*abb have?

  1. 3
  2. 4
  3. 5
  4. 8
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A DFA for "ends with abb" only has to remember how much of the pattern abb is currently matched as a suffix of the input read so far.

Step by step.

  1. State 0: nothing matched (no suffix of abb).
  2. State 1: the input ends in a.
  3. State 2: the input ends in ab.
  4. State 3 (accepting): the input ends in abb.
  5. Transitions: on b state 0 stays in 0; on a state 0 goes to 1; state 2 on b goes to 3, on a back to 1; state 3 on b stays in 3, on a goes to 1.
  6. All four states are reachable and pairwise distinguishable, so the minimal DFA has 4 states.

Final answer. Option (B) — 4 states.

Common misconception. Answering 8 by applying the 2ⁿ subset-construction bound. That bound is a worst case for the NFA of the expression; the minimal DFA for this language needs only 4 states.

CD-U1-Q07MCQhard

Which of the following regular expressions does not denote the set of all strings over {a, b}?

  1. (a + b)*
  2. (a* b*)*
  3. (b* a*)*
  4. (a b*)*
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Two expressions denote the same language only if each can generate every string of the other. Test a string that starts with the "wrong" symbol.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is Σ* by definition.
  2. (B) (a*b*)*: any string can be cut into maximal blocks of a's and b's, so it generates all of Σ*.
  3. (C) (b*a*)*: similarly, cut the string into maximal blocks of b's followed by a's.
  4. (D) (ab*)*: every iteration starts with an a, so every non-empty generated string begins with a. The string ba is not generated.
  5. Hence (D) is not Σ*.

Final answer. Option (D).

Common misconception. Assuming that any star applied to a concatenation equals Σ*. Always test a short string that begins with the symbol the first factor does not allow.

CD-U1-Q15MCQhard

A scanner has the token classes INT = [0-9]+, FLOAT = [0-9]+\.[0-9]+ and DOT = \.. On the input 3.14 the scanner returns

  1. INT 3, then DOT ., then INT 14
  2. FLOAT 3.14
  3. FLOAT 3.1, then INT 4
  4. a lexical error, because 3.14 matches two rules
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Rule priority is decided by match length first. INT can match only the first character; FLOAT can match all four; the longest match therefore wins, and rule order is irrelevant here.

Step by step.

  1. At position 1, INT matches 3 (length 1).
  2. At the same position FLOAT matches 3.14 (length 4).
  3. Longest match wins: FLOAT 3.14 is returned as one token.
  4. The DOT rule is never consulted, because a longer match already exists.

Final answer. Option (B) — a single FLOAT token.

Common misconception. Assuming the first matching rule wins (option A). Order only breaks ties between matches of equal length; here the lengths differ, so longest match decides.

CD-U1-Q17MSQmedium

Which of the following languages can be recognised by a DFA, and hence by a Lex/Flex-generated lexer?

  1. The set of strings over {a, b} that end in ab.
  2. The set of strings over {a, b} that contain an equal number of a's and b's.
  3. The set of strings over {a, b} of the form aⁿbⁿ, n ≥ 0.
  4. The set of valid C integer literals (decimal, octal and hexadecimal).
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A scanner can recognise exactly the regular languages. A language is regular iff some finite automaton accepts it; languages needing unbounded counting are not regular (pumping lemma).

Step by step.

  1. (A) Regular: a three-state automaton remembers whether the last two symbols were ab.
  2. (B) Not regular: it needs to count the difference between the number of a's and b's, which is unbounded.
  3. (C) Not regular: it needs to count the a's and compare with the b's.
  4. (D) Regular: the set of syntactically valid literals is described by a regular expression such as 0[xX][0-9a-fA-F]+ | 0[0-7]* | [1-9][0-9]*.

Final answer. Options (A) and (D).

Common misconception. Believing a larger buffer or a bigger automaton can count. No finite automaton can compare two unbounded counts — that is exactly what the pumping lemma rules out.

CD-U1-Q22NATeasy

The minimal DFA accepting the language denoted by (a|b)*abb has how many states? (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The automaton only needs to remember the longest prefix of abb that is a suffix of the input read so far.

Step by step.

  1. State 0 — no suffix of the pattern (start state).
  2. State 1 — input ends in a.
  3. State 2 — input ends in ab.
  4. State 3 — input ends in abb (accepting).
  5. Transitions: 0 -a-> 1, 0 -b-> 0, 1 -a-> 1, 1 -b-> 2, 2 -a-> 1, 2 -b-> 3, 3 -a-> 1, 3 -b-> 3. All four states are reachable and pairwise distinguishable.

Final answer. 4 states.

Common misconception. Reporting 8 from the 2ⁿ subset-construction bound, or 3 by forgetting that the start state is distinct from the "ends in a" state.

CD-U1-Q25NAThard

How many states does the minimal DFA for the language of all strings over {a, b} whose third symbol from the end is a have — that is, for the language of (a|b)*a(a|b)(a|b)? (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. To decide a property of the last three symbols the automaton must remember those three symbols, and nothing older. There are 2³ = 8 possible three-symbol suffixes.

Step by step.

  1. Let the states be the 8 strings of length ≤ 3 recording the last three symbols read (with shorter strings for inputs of length below 3).
  2. On reading a symbol, drop the oldest symbol and append the new one — a sliding window of width 3.
  3. A state is accepting exactly when its recorded suffix has a in the third position from the end, i.e. suffixes a??.
  4. All 8 states are reachable, and any two distinct suffixes are distinguished by appending the length-3 string that makes one accepting and the other not, so the DFA is minimal.

Final answer. 8 states.

Common misconception. Answering 4 (remembering only the last symbol) or 16 (remembering four). The window must be exactly three symbols wide: fewer cannot decide the property, more is redundant.

CD-U1-Q33MCQeasy

Which regular expression denotes exactly the C unsigned decimal integer constants that have no leading zeros?

  1. [0-9]+
  2. [1-9][0-9]*
  3. 0|[1-9][0-9]*
  4. 0?[1-9][0-9]*
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A no-leading-zero literal is either the single digit 0, or a non-zero digit followed by any number of digits. The two cases must be unioned.

Step by step.

  1. [0-9]+ allows leading zeros such as 007.
  2. [1-9][0-9]* excludes the single digit 0.
  3. 0|[1-9][0-9]* is exactly the union of the two valid shapes.
  4. 0?[1-9][0-9]* still generates 05, because the optional 0 may precede a non-zero digit.

Final answer. Option (C).

Common misconception. Forgetting that 0 itself is legal. The 'first digit non-zero' rule has to be written as a union, not as an optional prefix.

CD-U1-Q34MCQeasy

The pattern [0-9]+(\.[0-9]+)?([eE][+-]?[0-9]+)? describes

  1. integer constants only
  2. decimal floating-point constants with an optional fractional part and an optional exponent
  3. identifiers that begin with a digit
  4. hexadecimal constants
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Each bracketed group is optional, so the pattern is the concatenation of a mandatory digit run with an optional fraction and an optional exponent.

Step by step.

  1. [0-9]+ is the mandatory integer part.
  2. (\.[0-9]+)? adds an optional fraction, so 3.25 is accepted.
  3. ([eE][+-]?[0-9]+)? adds an optional exponent with an optional sign, so 1e-9 is accepted.
  4. Together they describe decimal floating-point constants; hex forms need 0x and hex digits.

Final answer. Option (B).

Common misconception. Reading the ? as 'one or more'. It means zero or one occurrence, so both groups may be absent and plain integers are accepted too.

CD-U1-Q35MCQmedium

A scanner has exactly two patterns: P1 = a+ returning token A, and P2 = ab returning token B. Using maximal munch, what does it produce for the input aab?

  1. A(aa), then a lexical error at the b
  2. A(a), then A(ab)
  3. A(a), then A(a), then a lexical error at the b
  4. B(ab), preceded by a lexical error at the first a
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Maximal munch compares, at the current position, the longest prefix that each pattern can match, and commits to the longest one. There is no backtracking into a shorter match to suit a different pattern.

Step by step.

  1. At position 1 the remaining input is aab.
  2. a+ matches aa — two characters.
  3. ab matches nothing at position 1, because the second character is a, not b.
  4. The longer match wins: token A with lexeme aa.
  5. At position 3 the input is b: neither pattern matches, so a lexical error is reported.
  6. The scanner never re-splits aa into a single a in order to make ab possible.

Final answer. Option (A).

Common misconception. Expecting the scanner to back up and return B(ab). A DFA-based scanner commits to the longest match found and does not search for a globally consistent tokenisation.

CD-U1-Q36MCQhard

Which one of the following strings does not belong to the language denoted by (ab|ba)*?

  1. abab
  2. abba
  3. baab
  4. aabb
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Every string of (ab|ba)* is a concatenation of two-character blocks, each of which is ab or ba. So the length is even and, inside each block, the two characters differ.

Step by step.

  1. abab = ab · ab, so it is in the language.
  2. abba = ab · ba, so it is in the language.
  3. baab = ba · ab, so it is in the language.
  4. aabb would have to split as aa · bb or a · abb …, and neither aa nor bb is ab or ba. Not in the language.

Final answer. Option (D) — aabb.

Common misconception. Checking only the length or only the counts of a and b. Both aabb and abba have two a's and two b's, yet only the second is in the language.

CD-U1-Q37MCQhard

Over the alphabet {a, b}, which regular expression denotes exactly the strings containing an even number of a's?

  1. (b*ab*a)*b*
  2. (a|b)*a(a|b)*
  3. a*b*
  4. (ab|ba)*
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Write the string as a sequence of b-runs separated by single a's. The number of a's is even exactly when the a's can be grouped in pairs, each pair being a with any number of b's before and after it.

Step by step.

  1. A string with an even number of a's can be split as b*, then repeated blocks a b* a, then a final b*.
  2. That is exactly (b*ab*a)*b*.
  3. Check the distractors: (a|b)*a(a|b)* admits aba (two a's, fine) but also ab, which has one a.
  4. a*b* has at most one run of a's and admits a (odd).
  5. (ab|ba)* admits only even-length alternating strings, and misses bbaabb.

Final answer. Option (A).

Common misconception. Reaching for (b*ab*)*, which is also correct, or for (aa)*, which forbids b's. The correct form must allow b's anywhere.

CD-U1-Q38MSQmedium

Which of the following token classes are specified by regular expressions and can therefore be handled by a DFA-based scanner? (Select all that apply.)

  1. C identifiers
  2. Integer constants with an optional decimal exponent
  3. Balanced parentheses
  4. Comments delimited by /* and */, without nesting
  5. String literals with escaped quotes
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A token class is lexer-friendly exactly when the set of its strings is regular, that is, recognisable by a finite automaton with no auxiliary storage.

Step by step.

  1. Identifiers: a start set followed by a starred set — regular.
  2. Integer constants with an optional exponent: a finite union of concatenations — regular.
  3. Balanced parentheses: needs an unbounded count of openers, so not regular.
  4. Non-nesting comments: \/\*([^*]|\*+[^*/])*\*+\/ — regular.
  5. Escaped string literals: "([^"\\]|\\.)*" — regular, since an escape is a fixed two-character unit.

Final answer. Options (A), (B), (D) and (E).

Common misconception. Rejecting escaped string literals because they look recursive. A backslash escape is not a nested construct, so a star over two alternatives suffices.

CD-U1-Q39NATmedium

A scanner uses the three patterns below over the alphabet of digits, . and +, and applies maximal munch. [0-9]+\.[0-9]+ → FLOAT, [0-9]+ → NUM, \+ → PLUS. How many tokens does it produce for the input 12.5+3? (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Maximal munch may run across the boundary between two candidate patterns; the longer match wins even when the shorter pattern is listed first.

Step by step.

  1. At position 1 the candidates are 12 (NUM, two characters) and 12.5 (FLOAT, four characters).
  2. The longer match wins: FLOAT with lexeme 12.5.
  3. At position 5 the input is +3; + matches PLUS.
  4. At position 6, 3 matches NUM.
  5. Total: FLOAT, PLUS, NUM — three tokens.

Final answer. 3

Common misconception. Answering 4 by splitting 12.5 into 12, . and 5, or 5 by also counting a separate dot token.

CD-U1-Q40NATmedium

How many states does the minimal DFA for the language of all strings over {a, b} whose second symbol from the right is a have? (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A DFA that must inspect a fixed suffix of length k remembers the last k symbols, so it needs k + 1 states.

Step by step.

  1. State q0: fewer than two symbols have been read, or the last two symbols are not yet decided.
  2. State q1: the last symbol read is a.
  3. State q2: the last two symbols read are a followed by anything — accepting.
  4. On reading a new symbol x, the window slides: q0 goes to q1 if x = a else stays q0; q1 goes to q2 if the new symbol arrives after an a; q2 goes to q1 or q0 depending on x.
  5. All three states are reachable and pairwise distinguishable, so 3 is minimal.

Final answer. 3

Common misconception. Answering 4 by confusing this with 'third symbol from the right is a', which needs 8 states, or with 'ends in ab', which needs 3 states but a different acceptance condition.

CD-U1-Q58MCQeasy

Over the alphabet of letters, digits and the underscore, which regular expression denotes exactly the identifiers that end with a digit?

  1. [A-Za-z_][A-Za-z0-9_]*[0-9]
  2. [A-Za-z_][A-Za-z0-9_]*
  3. [A-Za-z0-9_]+[0-9]
  4. [A-Za-z_][0-9]*[A-Za-z0-9_]*
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. An identifier starts with a letter or underscore and continues with letters, digits or underscores. Requiring the last character to be a digit means the middle may be anything in that set, but the final symbol is pinned to [0-9].

Step by step.

  1. Option (A): first symbol from [A-Za-z_], then any run from the full set, then one digit — exactly the identifiers whose last character is a digit.
  2. Option (B) puts no constraint on the last character, so it also accepts abc.
  3. Option (C) allows the first character to be a digit, so it accepts 1x2, which is not an identifier.
  4. Option (D) allows a trailing letter, so it accepts a1b, which does not end in a digit.

Final answer. Option (A).

Common misconception. Reading [A-Za-z0-9_]* as "ends with a digit". A star can match the empty string, so it constrains nothing about the last symbol.

CD-U1-Q59MSQmedium

Over Σ = {a, b}, which of the following regular expressions denote the same language as (a|b)* ? (Select all that apply.)

  1. (a*b*)*
  2. (a*|b*)*
  3. (ε|a|b)*
  4. (ab|ba)*
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. All three of the first expressions can generate every string over {a, b}, and only such strings. A useful test is whether each one can generate an arbitrary string such as aab and the single symbol a.

Step by step.

  1. (A) Any string can be cut into blocks of the form a*b* (one block per descent from a-run to b-run), so the star over those blocks gives every string.
  2. (B) A star over (a*|b*) can pick one block per symbol, so it also gives every string.
  3. (C) The star ranges over the empty string and the two one-symbol strings, so concatenations of those are exactly all strings.
  4. (D) (ab|ba)* generates only even-length strings made of the pairs ab and ba; it cannot generate a, b or aab.

Final answer. Options (A), (B) and (C).

Common misconception. Assuming that a star over a smaller alphabet piece must give a smaller language. A star repeats without bound, and several different expressions denote the same language — regular expressions are not unique.

CD-U1-Q60MCQmedium

Which regular expression denotes exactly the C-style block comments /* ... */, with no nesting, where the body may contain newlines and runs of * ?

  1. /\*([^*]|\*+[^*/])*\*+/
  2. /\*.*\*/
  3. /\*[^*]*\*/
  4. /\*([^*]|\*[^/])*\*/
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A comment body is any run of characters in which every * that is followed by / is the closing delimiter. A run of stars not followed by a slash must be consumed as ordinary text, and the closing delimiter itself is a run of one or more stars followed by a slash.

Step by step.

  1. Take the standard body expression ([^*]|\*+[^*/])*. Either a non-star, or a run of stars followed by something that is neither star nor slash, is consumed as text.
  2. The comment then closes with \*+/ — one or more stars and a slash. This accepts /***/, because the middle stars belong to the closing run.
  3. (B) fails across newlines in most notations, because . does not match a newline.
  4. (C) cannot consume a star inside the body, so it rejects /* a * b */. (D) rejects /***/: after the opening delimiter the group cannot consume the middle stars, and a single closing \*/ is not enough.

Final answer. Option (A).

Common misconception. Writing the body as [^*]* and forgetting that stars do occur inside comments. The subtlety is that the closing delimiter is a run of stars, not one star.

CD-U1-Q61MCQhard

The language L = { w ∈ {a, b}* : w has an even number of a's and an odd number of b's } is recognised by a DFA. After all equivalent states are merged, how many states does that DFA have?

  1. 2
  2. 3
  3. 4
  4. 6
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The only thing a state has to remember is the parity of the number of a's and the parity of the number of b's seen so far — four combinations. Each combination is reachable and each has a different right language, so all four survive minimisation.

Step by step.

  1. Write a state as a pair (parity of a's, parity of b's), each parity being 0 (even) or 1 (odd).
  2. The start state is (0, 0) and is not final, because the empty string has no odd number of b's.
  3. All four pairs are reachable: (0,0) by ε, (1,0) by a, (0,1) by b, (1,1) by ab.
  4. They are pairwise distinguishable — from (0,1) the empty suffix is accepted, from (0,0) and (1,0) it is not, and (1,0) and (1,1) differ on the suffix b — so no two merge. The minimal DFA has exactly 4 states, with (0,1) final.

Final answer. Option (C) — 4 states.

Common misconception. Counting states as "one per condition plus a start state" and answering 3. Parity conditions compose: two independent binary facts give 2 × 2 = 4 states, and none of them is redundant here.

CD-U1-Q62NATmedium

A scanner's patterns are combined into one automaton in the standard way: each pattern gets its own NFA with its own start state, and a new start state is added with an ε-transition to each of those start states. Four patterns have NFAs with 3, 5, 4 and 6 states respectively. How many states does the combined NFA have, counting the new start state? (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The union construction adds exactly one new start state; every state of every component NFA is kept, and the only new edges are the ε-edges from that new start state.

Step by step.

  1. States contributed by the four pattern NFAs: 3 + 5 + 4 + 6 = 18.
  2. The new start state adds 1.
  3. Total: 18 + 1 = 19 states, with four new ε-edges.
  4. No states are merged: merging would change the language, and the DFA that is built afterwards is what removes redundancy.

Final answer. 19 states.

Common misconception. Adding one start state per pattern instead of one for the union, or forgetting the new start state altogether and answering 18.

CD-U1-Q63MCQeasy

Which statement about the automata from which a scanner is built is TRUE?

  1. Two different NFAs for the same regular language always have the same number of states
  2. The minimal DFA for a regular language is unique up to renaming of its states
  3. Minimising a DFA can change the language it accepts
  4. Every NFA can be made minimal by merging states that have the same outgoing transitions
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Minimal DFAs are canonical: for a regular language there is exactly one minimal DFA, and any two minimal DFAs differ only in the names of their states. NFAs enjoy no such uniqueness.

Step by step.

  1. The Myhill–Nerode theorem says a language's minimal DFA has one state per distinct right language (residual), and that set of residuals is a property of the language alone.
  2. So the minimal DFA is unique up to isomorphism: option (B) is true.
  3. (A) is false: an NFA for a* may have one state or many. (C) is false: minimisation merges equivalent states and preserves the language by construction. (D) is false: merging states with the same outgoing transitions ignores the states they reach and generally changes the language.

Final answer. Option (B).

Common misconception. Transferring the uniqueness of the minimal DFA to NFAs. NFAs have no unique minimal form, which is one reason scanners are run on DFAs.

Lex input structure · 16
CD-U1-Q08MCQeasy

A Lex/Flex specification has three sections separated by %% lines. In which section are the pattern–action rules written?

  1. The first section (definitions)
  2. The second section (rules)
  3. The third section (user subroutines)
  4. Any of the three; order does not matter
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A Lex program is declarations %% translation rules %% auxiliary procedures.

Step by step.

  1. Section 1 (definitions): optional C declarations in %{ %}, plus named regular definitions such as DIGIT [0-9].
  2. Section 2 (rules): one line per rule, pattern followed by the action to run when the pattern matches.
  3. Section 3 (user subroutines): auxiliary C code, typically main() and error routines.

Final answer. Option (B) — the second section holds the rules.

Common misconception. Placing helper functions in the rules section. Only pattern–action pairs belong there; any other C code must go in the third section (or in %{ %} for declarations).

CD-U1-Q09MCQmedium

A Lex/Flex scanner contains the rule [0-9]+. While scanning the line total = 100; that rule fires. What are yytext and yyleng for this match?

  1. yytext = "100", yyleng = 3
  2. yytext = "100", yyleng = 2
  3. yytext = " 100", yyleng = 4
  4. yytext = "total = 100", yyleng = 11
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. yytext points at the characters of the lexeme that the rule actually matched; yyleng is the number of characters in that lexeme.

Step by step.

  1. The rule [0-9]+ matches the maximal run of digits, which is 100.
  2. The match contains exactly three characters: 1, 0, 0.
  3. So yytext = "100" and yyleng = 3.
  4. The leading space is not part of the match; it would have been consumed by a separate whitespace rule.

Final answer. Option (A).

Common misconception. Two errors are common: counting the length from zero (giving 2) and including the preceding blank in the lexeme (giving 4). yytext never contains characters the rule did not match.

CD-U1-Q10MCQmedium

Which statement about a DFA-based scanner produced by Lex/Flex is true?

  1. The automaton is built when the scanner is generated, so scanning n characters takes time proportional to n, with at most one character of lookahead past the current token.
  2. A fresh automaton is built for each token while the scanner runs, which is why scanning is slow.
  3. The scanner backtracks over the input to find the longest match, so the worst case is quadratic.
  4. The scanner needs a pushdown stack, because regular expressions can nest.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The patterns of all rules are compiled into one automaton (regular expressions → NFA → DFA) at scanner-generation time. At run time the scanner just simulates that DFA on the input.

Step by step.

  1. Simulation performs one transition per input character, so the work is O(n) for n characters.
  2. The only lookahead is the single character that fails to extend the current token; it is pushed back and starts the next token.
  3. There is no backtracking: a DFA is deterministic, so the last accepting state seen is the end of the token.
  4. Nesting is beyond regular languages; a scanner has no stack — that is exactly why balanced constructs are left to the parser.

Final answer. Option (A).

Common misconception. Confusing the NFA (which may branch) with the DFA that is actually simulated, and therefore expecting backtracking or exponential behaviour.

CD-U1-Q18MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about Lex/Flex are true?

  1. When several rules match, the longest match wins; among rules matching the same longest lexeme, the rule written first wins.
  2. The finite automaton is built when the scanner is generated, so scanning n characters takes O(n) time.
  3. In the rules section, a rule whose action is the single character | uses the action of the next rule.
  4. The definitions section must be the last section of a Lex specification.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Lex fixes both the rule-selection policy and the three-section layout; the generated scanner is a table-driven DFA.

Step by step.

  1. (A) True: longest match first, then earliest rule — the same two-stage rule as in any maximal-munch scanner.
  2. (B) True: patterns are compiled to one DFA at generation time, and simulation costs one transition per character.
  3. (C) True: | as an action means "same action as the following rule".
  4. (D) False: the order is definitions, rules, user subroutines — definitions come first.

Final answer. Options (A), (B) and (C).

Common misconception. Statement (D) is the trap, and its mirror image — putting declarations after the rules — is the most common practical mistake when writing a Lex file.

CD-U1-Q24NATeasy

A complete Lex/Flex specification has three sections. How many %% separator lines are needed to delimit them? (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Three sections in a linear file need two delimiters.

Step by step.

  1. Section 1 (definitions)   %%   Section 2 (rules)   %%   Section 3 (user subroutines).
  2. Separators required: 2.

Final answer. 2 separator lines.

Common misconception. Writing three separators (which produces an empty fourth section) or none at all (in which case the whole file is treated as the rules section).

CD-U1-Q41MCQeasy

In a Lex/Flex specification, the text between %{ and %} is

  1. the pattern–action rules
  2. C declarations that are copied verbatim into the generated scanner
  3. the user's main function, which is mandatory
  4. a comment block that Lex ignores
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The first section of a Lex file holds definitions. C code inside %{ %} is copied into the generated scanner, while macro definitions outside those delimiters are local shorthands.

Step by step.

  1. %{ %} encloses raw C: #include lines, global variables, typedefs.
  2. That text is emitted verbatim at the top of the generated scanner.
  3. Pattern–action rules belong to the second section.
  4. The third section holds auxiliary functions, which may include main, but main is not mandatory when a library driver is linked.

Final answer. Option (B).

Common misconception. Confusing the two kinds of content in the first section: C code inside %{ %}, and regex macros such as DIGIT [0-9] outside them.

CD-U1-Q42MCQeasy

Inside a Lex action, the variable yylineno holds

  1. the number of the input line currently being scanned
  2. the length of the matched lexeme
  3. the text of the matched lexeme
  4. the number of rules that have fired so far
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Lex exposes several auxiliaries to the action code: yytext (the lexeme), yyleng (its length) and yylineno (the current line number).

Step by step.

  1. yytext is the matched character string.
  2. yyleng is the number of matched characters.
  3. yylineno counts newlines consumed so far, so it is the current input line.
  4. It is normally used to prefix error messages with a line number.

Final answer. Option (A).

Common misconception. Mixing up yylineno and yyleng. The 'len' in the name is the only thing that makes it a length.

CD-U1-Q43MCQmedium

Which statement about yywrap() in a Lex/Flex scanner is correct?

  1. It is called after every lexeme to validate the token.
  2. It is called when the scanner reaches end of input; returning 1 tells the scanner to stop.
  3. It must return 0 for the scanner to terminate.
  4. It replaces yylex() when the input comes from a file.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. When yylex() exhausts its input it calls yywrap(). A non-zero return value signals that there is no further input, so scanning ends; returning 0 tells the scanner that a new input file has been set up in yyin.

Step by step.

  1. The scanner hits the end of yyin and calls yywrap().
  2. If yywrap() returns 1, yylex() returns 0 and the scan is finished.
  3. If it returns 0, the scanner continues reading from whatever yyin now points at — this is how multiple input files are chained.
  4. It is not called per lexeme, and it never replaces yylex().

Final answer. Option (B).

Common misconception. Reversing the convention: 1 means 'we are done'. Without %option noyywrap, you must supply your own yywrap().

CD-U1-Q44MSQmedium

Which of the following names are standard Lex/Flex auxiliary entities usable inside a rule action? (Select all that apply.)

  1. yytext
  2. yyleng
  3. yylineno
  4. yytoken
  5. yylex
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Flex exposes yytext, yyleng, yylineno, yyin, yyout and the entry point yylex. yytoken is not part of the interface.

Step by step.

  1. yytext — the matched lexeme: valid.
  2. yyleng — its length: valid.
  3. yylineno — the current line: valid.
  4. yytoken — does not exist in Lex or Flex.
  5. yylex — the scanner function itself, callable recursively: valid.

Final answer. Options (A), (B), (C) and (E).

Common misconception. Assuming every identifier beginning with 'yy' is real. yytoken, yypos and yytextlen are common inventions.

CD-U1-Q45NATeasy

A Lex rule [A-Za-z][A-Za-z0-9]* fires on the input line temp1 = 7;. What is the value of yyleng when the rule's action runs? (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The scanner matches the longest prefix that the rule accepts, and yyleng is the length of that matched lexeme.

Step by step.

  1. The rule starts matching at the first character, t.
  2. It accepts letters and digits, so it continues through e, m, p and 1.
  3. The next character is a space, which is not in the character class, so matching stops.
  4. The lexeme is temp1, five characters, so yyleng is 5.

Final answer. 5

Common misconception. Counting the following space (6) or the whole statement. yyleng covers the matched lexeme only.

CD-U1-Q64MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about a Lex/Flex specification are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. When two rules match lexemes of the same length, the rule written earlier in the rules section is used
  2. The user-code section after the second %% is copied verbatim into the generated scanner
  3. %option noyywrap removes the need to supply a yywrap() function
  4. A named definition declared in the definitions section cannot be used inside a pattern in the rules section
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A Lex/Flex file has three sections separated by %%: definitions, rules, and user code. The rules section supplies patterns and actions; the definitions section supplies named regular definitions and C declarations; the user-code section is copied into the output unchanged.

Step by step.

  1. (A) True: maximal munch decides by length first, and only equal-length matches are settled by the order of the rules — the earlier rule wins.
  2. (B) True: the text after the second %% is emitted verbatim, which is where main() and helper functions usually live.
  3. (C) True: %option noyywrap tells Flex to treat end of input as final, so no yywrap() is required.
  4. (D) False: a definition such as DIGIT [0-9] is used inside a pattern as {DIGIT}+, which is the main reason the definitions section exists.

Final answer. Options (A), (B) and (C).

Common misconception. Thinking that rule order beats length. Length is decided first by maximal munch; order only breaks ties.

CD-U1-Q65MCQeasy

A Lex/Flex scanner's yylex() returns the value 0. What does this mean?

  1. a lexical error has occurred
  2. the end of the input has been reached, and the parser should stop asking for tokens
  3. the scanner's buffer needs to be refilled before scanning can continue
  4. the token just returned is the last one on the current line
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. By convention yylex() returns 0 at end of input, which is the value the parser's end-marker uses. Buffering and line structure are invisible to the caller.

Step by step.

  1. The generated scanner reads until no more input remains, then calls yywrap().
  2. If yywrap() returns 1 (or noyywrap is in force), yylex() returns 0.
  3. The parser sees 0 as the end-of-input marker and stops.
  4. A lexical error is normally reported by printing a message and skipping the offending character, not by returning 0; buffer refills happen inside the scanner and are never visible; newlines are ordinary input.

Final answer. Option (B).

Common misconception. Reading 0 as "success" or as an error code. In this interface 0 is the end-of-input token value.

CD-U1-Q66NATmedium

A Flex scanner lists these rules in this order: (1) ab → return T1; (2) a → return T2; (3) abc → return T3. On the input abc, which rule number fires? (Enter 1, 2 or 3.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Flex applies maximal munch: among all rules that match at the current position it takes the one with the longest match. Rule order is consulted only when two matches are of the same length.

Step by step.

  1. At the start of abc the rule ab matches two characters and a matches one.
  2. The rule abc matches three characters, which is longer than either.
  3. Longest match wins, so rule 3 fires and the whole lexeme abc is consumed.
  4. Rule order would only matter if, say, rules 1 and 3 both matched three characters.

Final answer. 3 — the rule for abc.

Common misconception. Applying "first matching rule wins" as in a hand-written if-else chain. In Flex the longest match is chosen first; the earliest rule only breaks ties.

CD-U1-Q67MCQeasy

In Flex, a start condition such as %x COMMENT is used to

  1. let the scanner apply different sets of rules in different states of the scan, for example while handling a comment or a string literal
  2. remove unreachable states from the generated DFA
  3. set the initial value of yylineno
  4. declare the start state of the parser that will consume the tokens
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A start condition names a mode of the scanner. Rules prefixed with are active only in that mode, so the same scanner can behave differently inside a comment, a string, or ordinary code.

Step by step.

  1. Entering the mode is done in an action with BEGIN(COMMENT).
  2. While the mode is active, only rules tagged with that start condition (plus untagged rules, if %s is used rather than %x) can fire.
  3. Leaving the mode is another BEGIN(INITIAL). This is how string literals, nested comments and other context-sensitive lexemes are handled without leaving the scanner.
  4. Start conditions do not touch DFA minimisation, do not set yylineno, and have nothing to do with the parser's start symbol.

Final answer. Option (A).

Common misconception. Confusing a Flex start condition with the parser's start symbol. The two live in different phases and mean different things.

CD-U1-Q68MSQhard

Which of the following statements about how a Lex/Flex scanner works are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. All the token patterns are compiled into a single automaton, so one pass over the input recognises every token class
  2. A trailing-context rule r/s matches r only when r is immediately followed by s, and the text matched by s is not consumed
  3. Among matches of the same length, the rule that appears earlier in the rules section is the one that fires
  4. A rule's action may not call yylex() recursively
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The scanner is one automaton over the whole set of patterns, with each accepting state tagged by the rule that owns it. That is what makes a single left-to-right pass enough, and it is why trailing context and rule order are needed to disambiguate.

Step by step.

  1. (A) True: the patterns are combined and the resulting DFA tags each accepting state with a rule number.
  2. (B) True: in r/s the left part is matched and returned, the right part is only looked at; yyleng covers r alone.
  3. (C) True: this is the tie-break after maximal munch, and it is the reason a keyword rule must be written before the identifier rule only when the two matches are of equal length.
  4. (D) False: an action is ordinary C code and may call yylex(), which is occasionally used to pull the next token while handling a construct such as a nested block.

Final answer. Options (A), (B) and (C).

Common misconception. Believing that a scanner must make several passes, one per token class. A single combined automaton handles all classes in one pass; that is the whole point of the construction.

CD-U1-Q69MCQmedium

In the rules section of a Lex/Flex specification, a line of C code that is not part of a pattern must be

  1. indented, or enclosed in %{ and %}
  2. prefixed with #
  3. placed only after the second %%
  4. written on the same line as its pattern, after the action
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The rules section is a sequence of pattern-action pairs, one per line starting in the first column. Anything starting in column one is read as a pattern, so C code has to be distinguishable — indentation or %{ %} does that.

Step by step.

  1. A line beginning in column 1 of the rules section is parsed as a pattern.
  2. Indented text, or text between %{ and %}, is copied into the generated scanner as C code instead.
  3. An action that is a block in braces may span several lines without indentation, because the braces delimit it.
  4. Prefixing with # is a C preprocessor convention, not a Lex one; code placed only after the second %% is legal but is not "in the rules section"; and putting code on the pattern's line confuses it with the action itself.

Final answer. Option (A).

Common misconception. Assuming that the rules section is free-form C. Column one is reserved for patterns, which is why an accidental unindented statement becomes a mysterious "unrecognised rule" error.

Input buffering & lookahead · 16
CD-U1-Q11MCQeasy

In the two-buffer input scheme, a special sentinel character is placed at the end of each half-buffer. Its purpose is to

  1. mark the end of the token currently being scanned.
  2. remove the need to test for the end of the buffer on every character, so that only one test per character is needed.
  3. allow the scanner to match tokens of unbounded length.
  4. terminate the program once the input file is exhausted.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Without a sentinel the inner loop must check two conditions per character: "is the buffer exhausted?" and "which character is this?". A sentinel — a character that cannot occur in the source, such as end-of-file — merges the two tests into one.

Step by step.

  1. Each half-buffer ends with the sentinel.
  2. The loop advances while the current character is not the sentinel; a single comparison suffices.
  3. On reaching the sentinel, the scanner knows the half is exhausted and refills it.

Final answer. Option (B).

Common misconception. Thinking the sentinel marks the end of a token (it marks the end of a buffer) or that it lifts the bound on lexeme length (it does not — a lexeme must still fit in the buffer).

CD-U1-Q12MCQmedium

A DFA-based lexer is scanning a token and, after reading character c, the automaton enters a non-accepting state from which no accepting state is reachable. Assuming it had reached an accepting state earlier, what does the lexer do?

  1. It reports a lexical error immediately, because c cannot extend any token.
  2. It returns the last token it accepted and treats c as the first character of the next token.
  3. It restarts the automaton at the initial state and rescans the whole buffer from the beginning.
  4. It discards the accepted token and reports an error.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The lexer must return the longest prefix of the input that is a token. It therefore remembers the position of the last accepting state, together with the input position where that state was reached.

Step by step.

  1. The automaton accepted a prefix of length k and then read further characters that lead to a dead state.
  2. The token is the accepted prefix; the extra characters read after position k are returned to the input (only one character here, c).
  3. Scanning restarts from the initial state at that pushed-back position.
  4. An error is reported only if no accepting state was reached at all.

Final answer. Option (B).

Common misconception. Declaring an error as soon as the automaton leaves the accepting set. A token has already been found; the dead state only says the token cannot be extended.

CD-U1-Q14MCQmedium

In a two-buffer scheme the two halves hold N characters each and both halves are filled by one read operation. A lexeme begins near the end of the first half and continues into the second. Which statement is true?

  1. The scanner reports an error, because a lexeme may not cross a buffer boundary.
  2. Scanning simply continues into the second half; before the first half is next refilled, the part of the lexeme that lies in it must be preserved (moved to the start of the buffer).
  3. The scanner must restart the lexeme from the beginning of the file.
  4. The buffer must be doubled for every lexeme that crosses the boundary.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The two halves are contiguous, so a lexeme that crosses the midpoint needs no special action while it is being read. The danger is the later refill, which would overwrite the lexeme's first characters.

Step by step.

  1. Characters 1 … N live in the first half and N+1 … 2N in the second; the scan pointer moves through them as one buffer.
  2. A lexeme starting at N−3 and ending at N+4 is read normally.
  3. When the scan pointer reaches the end of the second half, the first half is refilled — which would destroy the lexeme's head.
  4. The standard fix is to move the lexeme's characters to the front of the buffer (or, equivalently, to enlarge the buffer) before refilling.

Final answer. Option (B).

Common misconception. Believing a lexeme cannot cross the boundary. It can; what cannot happen is refilling a half that still holds part of the current lexeme.

CD-U1-Q19MSQhard

Which of the following statements about the two-buffer input scheme and its sentinels are true?

  1. The sentinel must be a character that cannot occur in the source text, so it can never be mistaken for real input.
  2. Even with the sentinel in place, the scanner still needs two tests per character: one for the end of the buffer and one for the character class.
  3. If a lexeme crosses from one half-buffer into the other, the part of the lexeme already read must be preserved before that half is refilled.
  4. Without enlarging the buffer or moving the lexeme, a single lexeme cannot be longer than the buffer.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The sentinel exists precisely to halve the per-character work, and the scheme keeps lexemes intact by moving them before a refill.

Step by step.

  1. (A) True: end-of-file is the usual sentinel; if it could appear in the text, a real occurrence would be read as the buffer end.
  2. (B) False: the sentinel removes the end-of-buffer test, so one test per character remains — that is the entire point of the trick.
  3. (C) True: otherwise the refill overwrites the lexeme's head.
  4. (D) True: a lexeme must fit in the buffer; longer lexemes force a bigger buffer or a move-to-front copy.

Final answer. Options (A), (C) and (D).

Common misconception. Statement (B) is the trap — the sentinel is not cosmetic. If two tests per character were still needed, the scheme would buy nothing.

CD-U1-Q23NATmedium

A scanner uses the two-buffer scheme with halves of 4096 characters each, and each read operation fills both halves. A source file of 10,000 characters is scanned. How many read operations are performed? (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. One read fills the whole buffer, i.e. both halves: 2 × 4096 = 8192 characters.

Step by step.

  1. Read 1 supplies characters 1 … 8192.
  2. Characters 8193 … 10,000 (1808 characters) remain, so read 2 supplies the rest and hits end of file.
  3. No third read is needed.

Final answer. 2 read operations.

Common misconception. Dividing 10,000 by 4096 and rounding up (giving 3). A read fills both halves, so the capacity per read is 8192, not 4096.

CD-U1-Q46MCQeasy

Why does a lexical analyser read its input into two buffers instead of one?

  1. So that a lexeme crossing a buffer boundary can be handled without copying it to another place.
  2. So that two tokens can be scanned at the same time.
  3. So that the number of disk reads becomes exactly zero.
  4. So that the symbol table can be kept in the second buffer.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. In the two-buffer scheme the halves are adjacent in memory, so a lexeme that runs off the end of the first half continues directly into the second half.

Step by step.

  1. Fill the first half; when the scanner reaches its end, fill the second half.
  2. Because the halves are contiguous, the tail in the first half and the head in the second form one continuous string.
  3. No copying or shifting of the lexeme is needed.
  4. Scanning is sequential and single-threaded, so parallel scanning plays no part.

Final answer. Option (A).

Common misconception. Confusing the two-buffer scheme with double buffering for I/O overlap. Its purpose is contiguous storage across a boundary.

CD-U1-Q47MCQmedium

A DFA-based scanner has read up to position p in a non-accepting state, but it passed through an accepting state at position q, where q < p. It must fall back to q + 1. In the two-buffer scheme this fall-back costs

  1. re-reading the characters from q + 1 to p from secondary storage
  2. a constant-time pointer assignment, because those characters are still in the buffer
  3. copying the lexeme into a separate array and restarting the DFA
  4. restarting the DFA from the beginning of the file
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The scanner keeps a pointer to the character after the last accepting state. Retraction is a pointer move; the characters have not been discarded.

Step by step.

  1. The DFA records the furthest position at which some pattern accepted, say q.
  2. Scanning continues to p looking for a longer match.
  3. If no longer match appears, the lexeme is q characters wide and scanning resumes at q + 1.
  4. All characters between q and p are still in the buffer, so the cost is one assignment.

Final answer. Option (B).

Common misconception. Assuming retraction means backtracking over the input. Only the pointer moves; the characters are neither re-read nor re-examined.

CD-U1-Q48MCQhard

In the two-buffer scheme each half holds N characters and the two halves are adjacent in memory. If the scanner never moves a lexeme, the longest lexeme it can hold is

  1. N characters
  2. 2N characters
  3. unbounded
  4. N/2 characters
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Both halves together form one contiguous region of 2N characters. A lexeme may begin anywhere in the first half and run on into the second, so its maximum length is the size of the whole region.

Step by step.

  1. The two halves are adjacent, so the buffer is one array of 2N characters.
  2. A lexeme may start at the first character of the first half.
  3. It may then run to the last character of the second half.
  4. Maximum length without moving anything: 2N characters.
  5. A longer lexeme forces the scanner to move the part already read, or to enlarge the buffer.

Final answer. Option (B) — 2N characters.

Common misconception. Answering N because each half is N long. The halves are contiguous, which is exactly why the scheme exists.

CD-U1-Q49MSQhard

Which of the following statements about lookahead and retraction in a DFA-based scanner are true? (Select all that apply.)

  1. The scanner remembers the furthest position at which some pattern was accepted.
  2. Retraction means the characters are read again from the file.
  3. One character of lookahead past the end of a token may be needed to decide that the token has ended.
  4. Retraction is a pointer move; the characters stay in the buffer.
  5. A DFA-based scanner never needs any lookahead.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A deterministic scanner advances while the DFA keeps accepting, and keeps a record of the last accepting position so that it can retreat cheaply when the automaton dies.

Step by step.

  1. The last accepting position is stored: true, and it is what makes retraction cheap.
  2. Re-reading from the file: false; the characters are already in the buffer.
  3. One character of lookahead may be needed: true — for example, [0-9]+ cannot know the number has ended until it sees a non-digit.
  4. Retraction is a pointer move: true.
  5. No lookahead ever: false; the scanner must see the character that terminates the token.

Final answer. Options (A), (C) and (D).

Common misconception. Thinking lookahead means unbounded backtracking. For a DFA scanner the lookahead is bounded by the longest token and retraction is a single pointer assignment.

CD-U1-Q50NATmedium
