A DFA-based scanner has consumed 6 characters of the input. The last accepting state it passed through was reached after 4 characters, and from the current state no accepting state is reachable. How many characters must it retract before the next token starts? (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Retraction distance = (characters consumed) − (position of the last accepting state). The scanner gives back everything past the accepted lexeme.

Step by step.

  1. Characters consumed: 6.
  2. Position of the last accepting state: 4, so the token is 4 characters wide.
  3. Characters read past the token: 6 − 4 = 2.
  4. The scanner resets its pointer to position 5, so it retracts 2 characters.

Final answer. 2

Common misconception. Answering 6 or 4. The question asks for the number of characters handed back, not the length of the token or of the read-ahead.

CD-U1-Q70NATeasy

A scanner reads its input in blocks of 4096 bytes, using the two-buffer scheme in which one read operation fills both halves. The input file is 10000 bytes long. How many bytes does the final read operation transfer? (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Each read fills both halves, that is 2 × 4096 = 8192 bytes. The last read transfers whatever is left of the file.

Step by step.

  1. First read: bytes 1 to 8192 — a full 8192 bytes.
  2. Bytes still unread: 10000 − 8192 = 1808.
  3. Second read: those 1808 bytes, followed by the end-of-file marker.
  4. No third read is needed, because the end-of-file marker was already seen.

Final answer. 1808 bytes.

Common misconception. Dividing 10000 by 4096 and reading the remainder as 10000 mod 4096 = 1808 — the same number, but by luck: the block is 8192 because one read fills both halves. With halves of 1024 the remainder argument would be wrong.

CD-U1-Q71MCQeasy

Why does a scanner read its input in large blocks rather than one character at a time?

  1. it replaces many relatively expensive I/O operations with a few, so the cost of obtaining input is amortised over many characters
  2. it removes the need for a lookahead buffer
  3. it lets the scanner work without a finite automaton
  4. it guarantees that the scanner never has to retract a character
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A system call or a device read costs orders of magnitude more than a comparison. Reading a block moves the per-character cost down to an array access.

Step by step.

  1. Scanning is character-by-character work done in the CPU; fetching a character from a device or a system call is slow.
  2. Reading 4096 characters in one call amortises that fixed cost over 4096 characters.
  3. The block is then scanned from memory, where each step is a table lookup in the DFA.
  4. Lookahead, the need for an automaton, and retraction are all unchanged by block size — they are consequences of how tokens are recognised, not of how input arrives.

Final answer. Option (A).

Common misconception. Thinking that buffering is about token recognition. It is purely about the cost of getting bytes; the automaton is the same either way.

CD-U1-Q72MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about the Flex input primitives are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. yyless(n) keeps the first n characters of yytext and returns the rest to the input stream
  2. yymore() makes the text of the next match be appended to the current yytext
  3. unput(c) pushes the character c back onto the input stream
  4. yyless(n) discards the first n characters of yytext and keeps the remainder
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. These three primitives let an action undo or extend a match without leaving the scanner: they manipulate the boundary between what has been consumed and what is still to be read.

Step by step.

  1. (A) True: yyless(n) truncates yytext to its first n characters and pushes the remainder back, so the scanner will see it again.
  2. (B) True: yymore() keeps the current text and appends the next lexeme to it — the usual way to build a long string across several rules.
  3. (C) True: unput(c) puts one character back; several calls push back several characters, in reverse order.
  4. (D) False: it is the first n characters that are kept, not the last.

Final answer. Options (A), (B) and (C).

Common misconception. Reading yyless(n) as "keep all but the first n". The name means "use less of the match" — the prefix is what survives.

CD-U1-Q73MCQmedium

A DFA-based scanner reads ahead until no transition is possible and then falls back to the last accepting state it passed. The maximum number of characters it can ever have to retract is bounded by

  1. the length of the longest token pattern, minus one
  2. the number of states in the DFA
  3. the number of token classes the scanner knows
  4. the size of the input buffer
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Retraction is only needed because the automaton may run past the end of a token before discovering that no longer match exists. The run can never exceed the longest token the patterns can describe.

Step by step.

  1. Let L be the length of the longest token pattern. No token is longer than L characters.
  2. The scanner reads at most L characters from the start of a token, since beyond that no pattern can match.
  3. At least one of those characters belongs to the token actually returned, so at most L − 1 characters are pushed back.
  4. The DFA size, the number of token classes and the buffer size limit how much work the scanner does, but not how far it can overshoot a token.

Final answer. Option (A) — at most the longest pattern length minus one.

Common misconception. Supposing that retraction can grow with the input size. It cannot: it is bounded by the longest token the patterns can express, which is a property of the language, not of the file.

CD-U1-Q74MCQhard

In the two-buffer scheme a token begins near the end of the first half-buffer and continues past it. Which statement is TRUE?

  1. the token continues into the other half-buffer after a refill: the sentinel marks the end of a buffer, not the end of a token, so a token may straddle two halves
  2. the scanner must report the token as truncated at the sentinel and rescan it from the beginning of the next half
  3. the scanner must copy the input so that every token fits inside one half, because a token may never cross a half boundary
  4. the token is an error, because a lexeme must be contiguous inside one buffer
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The sentinel is a signal to the scanning loop, not a character of the language. When the forward pointer reaches it the other half is refilled and scanning simply continues, so the current lexeme spans the boundary without any special treatment.

Step by step.

  1. The scanner reaches the sentinel at the end of the half it is in.
  2. It refills the other half with the next block of input and switches to it.
  3. The DFA state is unchanged and the token simply keeps growing across the join.
  4. If the token is longer than the whole buffer, Flex copies the partial text into a separate growing buffer, but the token is never truncated or treated as an error.

Final answer. Option (A).

Common misconception. Thinking the sentinel ends the current token. It only ends the current buffer; the automaton, not the buffer, decides where tokens end.

CD-U1-Q75NAThard

Halves of 1024 characters are refilled alternately, each refill being triggered when the forward pointer reaches the sentinel of the half in use. A token begins at offset 1000 of the first half and the longest accepted prefix is 1500 characters long. How many times does the scanner switch halves while reading this token? (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Count the buffer boundaries the token has to cross. The token occupies offsets 1000 to 1000 + 1500 − 1 = 2499, and the halves are 0–1023, 1024–2047, 2048–3071, and so on.

Step by step.

  1. The token starts at offset 1000, inside the first half (0–1023).
  2. The sentinel at the end of the first half is reached at offset 1024: first switch, into the second half (1024–2047).
  3. The sentinel at the end of the second half is reached at offset 2048: second switch, back to the first half, refilled with 2048–3071.
  4. The token ends at offset 2499, which is inside that third block, so no further switch occurs. Total: 2 switches.

Final answer. 2 buffer switches.

Common misconception. Counting the halves the token touches (three blocks) instead of the boundaries between them (two). A token that lies in three blocks crosses two sentinels.

Review the 75 solved questions from Unit 2 — Syntax Analysis: Top-Down Parsing
FIRST and FOLLOW sets · 21
CD-U2-Q01MCQeasy

Consider the grammar S → a S b | ε over the alphabet {a, b}. Which one of the following is FIRST(S)?

  1. {a}
  2. {a, ε}
  3. {a, b}
  4. {a, b, ε}
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. FIRST(α) is the set of terminals that can begin a string derived from α, plus ε when α derives the empty string. It is computed production by production: a terminal contributes itself, and a nonterminal contributes the FIRST sets of its own alternatives.

Step by step.

  1. The alternative a S b begins with the terminal a, so a ∈ FIRST(S).
  2. The alternative ε derives the empty string, so ε ∈ FIRST(S).
  3. b is never the first symbol of any right side, so it is not in FIRST(S) — it only appears after S has already produced something.
  4. Hence FIRST(S) = {a, ε}.

Answer: {a, ε} — option B.

Common misconception. Adding b because it occurs in the grammar. b follows S, which makes it a member of FOLLOW(S), not of FIRST(S).

CD-U2-Q04MCQmedium

For the grammar S → A B, A → a | ε, B → b, the set FOLLOW(A) is

  1. {a}
  2. {b}
  3. {b, $}
  4. {$}
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. FOLLOW(A) is the set of terminals that can appear immediately to the right of A in some sentential form, plus $ if A can end a sentential form. It is read off from where A occurs on right sides.

Step by step.

  1. A occurs in S → A B, followed by B.
  2. So everything in FIRST(B) belongs to FOLLOW(A). FIRST(B) = {b}.
  3. B is not nullable, so the symbols that follow S do not flow into FOLLOW(A); $ stays out.
  4. Hence FOLLOW(A) = {b}.

Answer: {b} — option B.

Common misconception. Adding $ to every FOLLOW set. $ reaches FOLLOW(A) only when the rest of the right side after A can vanish entirely — here it cannot.

CD-U2-Q08MCQmedium

For the grammar S → A B, A → a | ε, B → b | ε, the set FIRST(S) is

  1. {a, b}
  2. {a, b, ε}
  3. {a, ε}
  4. {a, b, $}
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. For a right side X1 X2 … Xn, add FIRST(X1) − {ε}; add FIRST(X2) − {ε} as well only if X1 is nullable; and add ε only if every Xi is nullable.

Step by step.

  1. FIRST(A) = {a, ε}, so a ∈ FIRST(S).
  2. A is nullable, so FIRST(B) − {ε} = {b} also enters FIRST(S).
  3. Both A and B are nullable, so A B derives ε and ε ∈ FIRST(S).
  4. Hence FIRST(S) = {a, b, ε}.

Answer: {a, b, ε} — option B.

Common misconception. Stopping at the first symbol that has a non-empty FIRST set. The second symbol contributes only because the first is nullable — but it does contribute.

CD-U2-Q14MCQeasy

For the grammar S → A B, A → ε | a, B → b | ε, the set FIRST(B) is

  1. {b}
  2. {b, ε}
  3. {ε}
  4. {a, b, ε}
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. FIRST of a nonterminal is the union of the FIRST sets of its alternatives, taken one production at a time.

Step by step.

  1. B → b contributes b.
  2. B → ε contributes ε.
  3. No other production has B on the left.
  4. Hence FIRST(B) = {b, ε}.

Answer: {b, ε} — option B.

Common misconception. Reading the alternatives of A into FIRST(B), or writing {ε} alone because B is nullable. Nullability adds ε; it does not remove the terminals.

CD-U2-Q16NATeasy

In the grammar F → ( E ) | id, how many terminals belong to FIRST(F)? (Enter an integer. Do not count ε, since F is not nullable.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. FIRST(F) is the set of terminals that can begin a string derived from F.

Step by step.

  1. F → ( E ) begins with the terminal (.
  2. F → id begins with the terminal id.
  3. Neither alternative is nullable, so ε ∉ FIRST(F).
  4. Hence FIRST(F) = {(, id} — two terminals.

Answer: 2 terminals.

Common misconception. Counting ) or E. ) comes after E and never begins a string of F, and E is a nonterminal, not a terminal.

CD-U2-Q18MCQhard

Which one of the following statements about FIRST and FOLLOW sets is FALSE?

  1. FOLLOW(A) can contain $ only if A can occur as the rightmost symbol of a sentential form.
  2. If A is nullable and occurs at the end of a production, then FOLLOW(A) contains the FOLLOW of the head of that production.
  3. FIRST(A) contains ε exactly when A is nullable.
  4. FOLLOW(A) contains ε whenever A is nullable.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. FIRST describes what a string can begin with, and ε is a legitimate "beginning" of a nullable string. FOLLOW describes what can come after a nonterminal in a sentential form; its symbols are always terminals or the end marker $, never ε.

Step by step.

  1. (A) true: $ enters FOLLOW(A) only through the propagation rule for a symbol at the right end of a production, which ultimately traces back to S.
  2. (B) true: this is the standard propagation rule, including the chain through nullable symbols.
  3. (C) true: it is the definition of nullability expressed through FIRST.
  4. (D) false: FOLLOW(A) is a set of terminals and $. Nullability puts ε in FIRST(A) and is handled in the table by copying the ε-production across FOLLOW(A), not by putting ε inside it.

Answer: option D is false.

Common misconception. Symmetry. FIRST and FOLLOW look similar but are asymmetric: FIRST may hold ε, FOLLOW never does; FOLLOW holds $, FIRST never does.

CD-U2-Q20NATmedium

For the grammar S → A B C, A → a | ε, B → b | ε, C → c | ε, how many symbols does FOLLOW(A) contain? Count $ as a symbol. (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. When A is followed by a string of nullable symbols, every terminal that can begin one of them follows A, and if all of them can vanish, the FOLLOW of the head propagates as well.

Step by step.

  1. A occurs in S → A B C, followed by B C.
  2. FIRST(B) − {ε} = {b}, so b ∈ FOLLOW(A).
  3. B is nullable, so FIRST(C) − {ε} = {c} also enters: c ∈ FOLLOW(A).
  4. C is nullable too, so the rest of the right side can vanish completely and FOLLOW(S) = {$} propagates: $ ∈ FOLLOW(A).
  5. FOLLOW(A) = {b, c, $}, three symbols.

Answer: 3 symbols.

Common misconception. Answering 2 by stopping at B, or answering 4 by counting ε. Nullability is what carries the FOLLOW of the head into FOLLOW(A), and ε is never a member of a FOLLOW set.

CD-U2-Q26MCQeasy

Consider the grammar S → A b, A → c | ε. Which one of the following is FIRST(S)?

  1. {c}
  2. {c, b}
  3. {c, b, ε}
  4. {b}
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. FIRST of a string is read left to right: the first symbol contributes its own FIRST set without ε, and only when that symbol is nullable does the next symbol contribute.

Step by step.

  1. FIRST(A) = {c, ε}, because the alternative c gives the terminal c and the alternative ε makes A nullable.
  2. In S → A b, A comes first, so c ∈ FIRST(S); ε itself never enters FIRST(S).
  3. Because A is nullable, the scan moves on to b, so b ∈ FIRST(S).
  4. S itself derives no empty string, so ε is not in FIRST(S). Hence FIRST(S) = {c, b}.

Answer: {c, b} — option (B).

Common misconception. Writing {c, b, ε} because A is nullable. ε belongs to FIRST(A), not to FIRST(S); a nullable symbol passes the scan to the next symbol, it does not pass ε along.

CD-U2-Q28NATeasy

For the grammar S → a A | B c, A → b | ε, B → d, how many terminals belong to FIRST(S)? Do not count ε, since S is not nullable. (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. FIRST of a nonterminal is the union of the FIRST sets of its alternatives, and a nullable symbol that ends an alternative adds nothing to that alternative's FIRST set.

Step by step.

  1. FIRST(A) = {b, ε} and FIRST(B) = {d}.
  2. Alternative a A: it begins with the terminal a, so a is in FIRST(S); A is nullable but it is the last symbol, so the scan ends there and contributes no further terminal.
  3. Alternative B c: B contributes d, so d ∈ FIRST(S).
  4. S is not nullable, so ε is excluded: FIRST(S) = {a, d}, two terminals.

Answer: 2

Common misconception. Counting three terminals, by imagining that the nullable A at the end of a A lets the scan reach some further symbol. There is no symbol after A, so the scan stops.

CD-U2-Q34MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about FIRST and FOLLOW sets are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. If A ⇒* ε, then ε ∈ FIRST(A).
  2. If A is nullable, then FIRST(A B) = FIRST(A) ∪ FIRST(B) with ε removed from the result unless B is also nullable.
  3. If A is not nullable, FOLLOW(A) may still contain ε.
  4. For every nonterminal X of an LL(1) grammar, FIRST(X) ∩ FOLLOW(X) = ∅.
  5. For the start symbol S, $ ∈ FOLLOW(S).
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. FIRST is about what a string can begin with, FOLLOW about what can come immediately after a nonterminal. ε is a FIRST symbol only; FOLLOW holds terminals and the end marker $ and never ε.

Step by step.

  1. (A) True: A derives the empty string exactly when ε is in FIRST(A).
  2. (B) True: a nullable A lets the scan reach B, so B contributes its FIRST set, and the concatenation is nullable only if B is nullable too.
  3. (C) False: ε is never a member of any FOLLOW set; FOLLOW answers "which terminals may follow", and the empty string is not a terminal.
  4. (D) False: nothing forbids a terminal from both starting X and following X — the grammar S → a S | a has a in both FIRST(S) and FOLLOW(S) and is still LL(1).
  5. (E) True: the end marker is placed in FOLLOW(S) as the initialisation step of every FOLLOW computation.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (E).

Common misconception. Putting ε into a FOLLOW set when a nonterminal is nullable. FOLLOW is a set of terminals plus $; nullability affects which symbols are added, not whether ε is added.

CD-U2-Q36MCQeasy

For the grammar S → a S | b, which one of the following is FOLLOW(S)?

  1. {a}
  2. {b}
  3. {$}
  4. {a, b}
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. FOLLOW(S) starts with $, and grows only when S appears somewhere other than at the end of a right side.

Step by step.

  1. Initialise FOLLOW(S) = {$} because S is the start symbol.
  2. In S → a S the recursive S is the last symbol, so nothing new is added beyond FOLLOW(S) itself.
  3. In S → b there is no S at all.
  4. Hence FOLLOW(S) = {$}: nothing can follow a complete sentence.

Answer: {$} — option (C).

Common misconception. Adding b to FOLLOW because b ends a production. What matters is what follows S, and here S only ever appears at the very end of a right side.

CD-U2-Q39MCQmedium

For the grammar S → A B C, A → a, B → b, C → ε | c, which one of the following is FOLLOW(B)?

  1. {c}
  2. {c, $}
  3. {b, c}
  4. {$}
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. When B is followed by C in a right side, FOLLOW(B) takes FIRST(C) without ε, and if C is nullable it also takes FOLLOW of the left-side nonterminal.

Step by step.

  1. FIRST(C) = {c, ε}, because C → ε makes C nullable.
  2. B is followed by C in S → A B C, so c ∈ FOLLOW(B).
  3. C is nullable, so the scan runs past C to the end of the production, and FOLLOW(S) = {$} is added as well.
  4. Hence FOLLOW(B) = {c, $}.

Answer: {c, $} — option (B).

Common misconception. Answering {c} by treating C as non-nullable. Because C can vanish, whatever follows S can also follow B.

CD-U2-Q41NATmedium

For the grammar S → X Y Z, X → x | ε, Y → y | ε, Z → z | ε, how many symbols does FOLLOW(Y) contain? Count $ as a symbol. (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. FOLLOW of a symbol in the middle of a right side takes the FIRST set of everything to its right, and continues past any nullable suffix into FOLLOW of the left-side nonterminal.

Step by step.

  1. Y is followed by Z, so FIRST(Z) = {z, ε} contributes z.
  2. Z is nullable, so the scan reaches the end of S → X Y Z and adds FOLLOW(S) = {$}.
  3. ε is never a member of a FOLLOW set, so it is not counted.
  4. FOLLOW(Y) = {z, $}, two symbols.

Answer: 2

Common misconception. Counting ε as a third symbol, or stopping at z without noting that the nullable Z exposes the end of the sentence.

CD-U2-Q43MCQhard

For the grammar S → A B | B a, A → a | ε, B → b | ε, which one of the following is FOLLOW(B)?

  1. {$}
  2. {a, $}
  3. {a, b, $}
  4. {b, $}
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A nonterminal that occurs twice in the grammar collects contributions from every occurrence, so each occurrence must be examined separately.

Step by step.

  1. First occurrence, S → A B: B is last, so FOLLOW(S) = {$} is added.
  2. Second occurrence, S → B a: B is followed by the terminal a, so a is added.
  3. A being nullable does not matter here: A appears only in the first alternative, where it precedes B rather than following it.
  4. Hence FOLLOW(B) = {a, $}.

Answer: {a, $} — option (B).

Common misconception. Answering {$} from the first occurrence only. Every occurrence of the nonterminal contributes to its FOLLOW set.

CD-U2-Q50MSQeasy

Which of the following statements about FIRST and FOLLOW sets are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. For a terminal a, FIRST(a) = {a}.
  2. The FOLLOW set of the start symbol always contains $.
  3. If a nonterminal other than the start symbol never occurs on the right side of any production, its FOLLOW set is empty.
  4. A FOLLOW set may contain a nonterminal as a member.
  5. FIRST of a string is decided by its leftmost symbol alone, even when that symbol is a nullable nonterminal.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. FIRST sets describe what a string can begin with; FOLLOW sets describe which terminals may appear immediately after a nonterminal, plus the end marker $ for the start symbol.

Step by step.

  1. (A) True: a terminal derives only itself, so its FIRST set is the singleton {a}.
  2. (B) True: every FOLLOW computation is initialised with FOLLOW(S) = {$}.
  3. (C) True: FOLLOW of a nonterminal grows only from occurrences on right sides, so a nonterminal with no such occurrence keeps the empty set — only the start symbol is seeded with $.
  4. (D) False: FOLLOW holds terminals and $ only; a nonterminal on the right side is followed by whatever its own FOLLOW is, not by the nonterminal itself.
  5. (E) False: when the leftmost symbol is nullable, the scan continues to the next symbol, so the FIRST set of the whole string can include symbols that the leftmost symbol cannot start.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C).

Common misconception. Stopping the FIRST computation at the leftmost symbol even when it is nullable, and imagining that nonterminals can appear inside FOLLOW sets.

CD-U2-Q51MCQeasy

The set FIRST(A) is defined for a nonterminal A. Which one of the following states exactly what x ∈ FIRST(A) means, where x is a terminal?

  1. A ⇒* x γ for some (possibly empty) string γ of grammar symbols.
  2. A ⇒* γ x for some (possibly empty) string γ of grammar symbols.
  3. x ⇒* A γ for some (possibly empty) string γ of grammar symbols.
  4. A ⇒* x, and x must be the last symbol of the derived string.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. FIRST(A) collects the terminals that can begin a string derived from A, together with ε when A can derive the empty string. The quantifier is "some derivation, some suffix", never "every derivation".

Step by step.

  1. A ⇒* x γ says: from A one can derive a string whose first symbol is x. That is precisely the definition.
  2. Option (B) says x is the last symbol. That is the shape of a FOLLOW set, not a FIRST set.
  3. Option (C) reverses the derivation arrow, so it says x derives something containing A; that is not a statement about A at all.
  4. Option (D) demands that nothing follow x. That is too strong: for A → a B, a ∈ FIRST(A) even though B follows.

Final answer. Option (A).

Common misconception. Reading FIRST as "the symbols that can appear somewhere in a string derived from A". FIRST is only about the leftmost position; symbols in the middle or at the end belong to FIRST of other symbols or to FOLLOW.

CD-U2-Q52MCQeasy

For three nonterminals of a grammar, FIRST(A) = {a, ε}, FIRST(B) = {b, ε} and FIRST(C) = {c, d}. Which one of the following is FIRST(A B C)?

  1. {a, b, c, d, ε}
  2. {a, b, c}
  3. {a, b, c, d}
  4. {a, b, c, d, $}
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. FIRST of a string is read left to right: take FIRST of the first symbol, drop ε, and continue with the next symbol only if the previous one is nullable. Add ε to the result only if every symbol of the string is nullable.

Step by step.

  1. A contributes a. It is nullable, so the scan continues.
  2. B contributes b. It is nullable too, so the scan continues.
  3. C contributes c and d. It is not nullable, so the scan stops and ε is not added.
  4. Union: {a} ∪ {b} ∪ {c, d} = {a, b, c, d}.

Final answer. {a, b, c, d} — option (C).

Common misconception. Adding ε because some symbol of the string is nullable. The whole string is nullable only when every symbol in it is, and C is not.

CD-U2-Q53NATeasy

Consider the grammar S → A B | c, A → a | ε, B → b | ε. How many of the three nonterminals have $ in their FOLLOW set? (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. $ enters FOLLOW(S) because S is the start symbol, and it spreads to other nonterminals only through nullable suffixes: if A is followed in some right side by a string that can derive ε, then FOLLOW of the head flows into FOLLOW(A).

Step by step.

  1. FOLLOW(S) = {$} — the start symbol, and S appears on no right side.
  2. In S → A B, A is followed by B, so FIRST(B) − {ε} = {b} ⊆ FOLLOW(A). Since B is nullable, FOLLOW(S) ⊆ FOLLOW(A) as well. Hence FOLLOW(A) = {b, $}.
  3. B ends the right side of S → A B, so FOLLOW(S) ⊆ FOLLOW(B): FOLLOW(B) = {$}.
  4. All three sets contain $: S, A and B.

Final answer. 3.

Common misconception. Assuming $ belongs only to FOLLOW(S). Whenever the start symbol can be followed by a nullable run of symbols, $ reaches every nonterminal on that run.

CD-U2-Q54MCQmedium

FIRST and FOLLOW sets are computed by starting from empty sets and repeatedly applying the set rules until nothing changes. Which one of the following statements about that iteration is TRUE?

  1. A symbol added in one round can be removed in a later round once a production is found that does not derive it.
  2. The final sets depend on the order in which the nonterminals are visited, so two different visiting orders can produce two different FIRST sets.
  3. The iteration is guaranteed to stop only when the grammar is LL(1).
  4. Every symbol added is justified by a rule applied to symbols already present, so the sets only grow, and because each set is finite the iteration must stop.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. FIRST and FOLLOW are the least fixed point of a monotone system: each rule has the form "if these symbols are present, then add that one". Adding symbols can only enable more additions, never disable them.

Step by step.

  1. No rule removes a symbol, so option (A) is false: the sets are monotone non-decreasing.
  2. Because the rules are monotone, any fair order of application reaches the same least fixed point. Order changes only how many rounds are needed, not the answer, so option (B) is false.
  3. The sets are subsets of a finite universe — the grammar's terminals plus $, plus ε for FIRST — so a strictly growing chain cannot continue for ever. Termination does not depend on LL(1)-ness, so option (C) is false.
  4. That is exactly the fixed-point argument: finite universe plus monotone rules gives termination at a unique least fixed point.

Final answer. Option (D).

Common misconception. Believing that a grammar must be well behaved for FIRST and FOLLOW to be well defined. They are defined, and computable, for every context-free grammar, LL(1) or not.

CD-U2-Q55MCQhard

Consider the grammar S → A S | b, A → a, where S is the start symbol. Which one of the following is FOLLOW(S)?

  1. {$}
  2. {a, b, $}
  3. {b, $}
  4. {a, b}
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. FOLLOW(X) holds the terminals that can appear immediately after X in some sentential form, plus $ when X is the start symbol. A nonterminal's own FIRST set is irrelevant to its FOLLOW set.

Step by step.

  1. FIRST(S) = {a, b}, because S ⇒ A S ⇒ a S and S ⇒ b. This is the trap, not the answer.
  2. The only right side containing S is A S, and there S is the last symbol. Nothing can follow it from that production.
  3. No production of this grammar places any symbol after S, so no terminal is added.
  4. S is the start symbol, so $ ∈ FOLLOW(S). Hence FOLLOW(S) = {$}.

Final answer. Option (A).

Common misconception. Copying FIRST(S) into FOLLOW(S) because S appears on a right side. Appearance on a right side matters only through what stands after the occurrence — here, nothing.

CD-U2-Q56MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about FIRST and FOLLOW sets are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. If A is nullable and B is not, then FIRST(A B) = (FIRST(A) − {ε}) ∪ FIRST(B).
  2. A nonterminal that is unreachable from the start symbol necessarily has an empty FOLLOW set.
  3. No FOLLOW set ever contains ε.
  4. FIRST sets are computed from the productions alone, without first checking which nonterminals are reachable from the start symbol.
  5. Every nonterminal that has ε in its FIRST set also has ε in its FOLLOW set.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. FIRST describes what a string can begin with and whether it can vanish; FOLLOW describes what can follow a nonterminal and always contains terminals and $ only. Both are purely syntactic — they are defined over the production list, not over reachability from the start symbol.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is the standard rule for a two-symbol string with a nullable first symbol: a can begin the string, and so can anything that begins B. The string is nullable only if B is, and B is not. TRUE.
  2. (B) is false. FOLLOW is computed from every production, reachable or not: an unreachable nonterminal can inherit FOLLOW from productions of other nonterminals.
  3. (C) is true by definition: FOLLOW contains terminals and $, and ε is a string, not a terminal.
  4. (D) is true: FIRST and FOLLOW are defined for the grammar as written. Deleting useless symbols is a separate, later simplification.
  5. (E) is false: nullable means ε ∈ FIRST; FOLLOW never holds ε at all.

Final answer. (A), (C) and (D).

Common misconception. Treating FIRST and FOLLOW as semantic notions that need the grammar to be cleaned up first. They are syntactic fixed points over the productions, and they are defined even for grammars with useless symbols.

LL(1) parsing tables · 18
CD-U2-Q05MSQeasy

Which of the following statements about LL(1) grammars are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. No left-recursive grammar is LL(1).
  2. For any nonterminal, the FIRST sets of two distinct alternatives are disjoint, and where one alternative is nullable its FOLLOW set is disjoint from the FIRST sets of the other alternatives.
  3. Every LL(1) grammar is unambiguous.
  4. Every unambiguous grammar is LL(1).
  5. An LL(1) parser sometimes has to backtrack over tokens it has already read.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A grammar is LL(1) when one token of lookahead always determines which alternative to expand. That forces the FIRST sets of the alternatives of a nonterminal to be disjoint, and, when an alternative can vanish, forces its FOLLOW set to be disjoint from the FIRST sets of the others.

Step by step.

  1. (A) true: in A → A α | β both alternatives can begin with the same string (namely β α…), so the FIRST sets collide and no finite lookahead separates them. Every left-recursive grammar has such a nonterminal somewhere on a cycle.
  2. (B) true: this is exactly the LL(1) condition; it is what makes each table cell hold at most one entry.
  3. (C) true: if the grammar were ambiguous, two distinct leftmost derivations would share a prefix, so the two productions applied at the divergence point would have to be told apart by the same lookahead — a table conflict.
  4. (D) false: the classic dangling-else grammar is unambiguous but is not LL(1). Unambiguity alone does not give one-token decisions.
  5. (E) false: an LL(1) parser never backtracks; the table decides every step.

Answer: options (A), (B) and (C).

Common misconception. Treating "unambiguous" and "LL(1)" as synonyms. LL(1) is a property of the grammar plus a one-token decision procedure; unambiguous grammars that need two tokens of lookahead, or that are left-recursive, fall outside it.

CD-U2-Q06MCQmedium

Consider the dangling-else grammar S → i E t S S′ | a, S′ → e S | ε, E → b, where i, t and e stand for if, then and else. In its LL(1) parsing table, which cell receives two entries?

  1. M[S, i]
  2. M[S′, e]
  3. M[E, b]
  4. None — the grammar is LL(1)
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A cell M[A, a] is filled by the alternative A → α when a ∈ FIRST(α), and by A → ε for every a ∈ FOLLOW(A). Two entries in one cell mean the grammar is not LL(1).

Step by step.

  1. S′ occurs at the very end of S → i E t S S′, so FOLLOW(S′) ⊇ FOLLOW(S) = {e, $}.
  2. FIRST(e S) = {e}, so M[S′, e] receives S′ → e S.
  3. Because S′ is nullable, S′ → ε is entered in every cell of FOLLOW(S′) — and e is one of them.
  4. So M[S′, e] holds both S′ → e S and S′ → ε: the parser cannot tell, from the token else, whether it belongs to the inner or the outer if.

Answer: M[S′, e] — option B.

Common misconception. Expecting the conflict at M[S, i] because the grammar is "about" the two if rules. The clash is on the token else: it is in FIRST(e S) and also in FOLLOW(S′), which is what an ε-production needs.

CD-U2-Q07NATmedium

For the grammar S → A B, A → a A | ε, B → b B | ε, how many cells of the LL(1) parsing table are non-empty? Count a cell once however many entries it holds. (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Fill M[A, a] from FIRST for every alternative that begins with a terminal, and place A → ε in every cell M[A, b] with b ∈ FOLLOW(A).

Step by step.

  1. Sets: FIRST(A) = {a, ε}, FIRST(B) = {b, ε}, FIRST(S) = {a, b, ε}.
  2. FOLLOW(S) = {$}; FOLLOW(A) = FIRST(B) − {ε} ∪ FOLLOW(S) = {b, $}; FOLLOW(B) = FOLLOW(S) = {$}.
  3. Row S: M[S, a] = S → A B (from FIRST(A)), M[S, b] = S → A B (A nullable, b ∈ FIRST(B)), M[S, $] = S → A B (both nullable). Three cells.
  4. Row A: M[A, a] = A → a A, M[A, b] = A → ε, M[A, $] = A → ε. Three cells.
  5. Row B: M[B, b] = B → b B, M[B, $] = B → ε. Two cells.
  6. Total 3 + 3 + 2 = 8. No cell holds two entries, so the grammar is LL(1).

Answer: 8 non-empty cells.

Common misconception. Writing A → ε in a single cell labelled ε. The ε-production is duplicated into every terminal of the FOLLOW set — that duplication is exactly what can create a conflict.

CD-U2-Q13MCQeasy

A grammar is LL(1) if and only if, for every pair of distinct alternatives α and β of a nonterminal A,

  1. FIRST(α) ∩ FIRST(β) = ∅, and if α ⇒* ε then FIRST(β) ∩ FOLLOW(A) = ∅ (and symmetrically for β).
  2. FIRST(α) ∩ FOLLOW(A) = ∅ for both alternatives.
  3. FIRST(α) ∩ FIRST(β) = ∅ and FOLLOW(A) = ∅.
  4. the grammar contains no ε-production.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The LL(1) condition is exactly the statement that no cell of the parsing table receives two entries. Two ways of colliding are possible: two alternatives can start with the same terminal, or a nullable alternative can vanish on a terminal that another alternative can start with.

Step by step.

  1. FIRST(α) ∩ FIRST(β) = ∅ rules out the first kind of collision: no terminal selects two alternatives by starting them.
  2. If α ⇒* ε, then A → α is entered in every cell M[A, b] with b ∈ FOLLOW(A), so FIRST(β) must avoid FOLLOW(A).
  3. Options B and C are too strong: FOLLOW(A) may be non-empty, it just must not overlap the FIRST sets of the other alternatives.
  4. Option D is false: S → a S b | ε contains an ε-production and is LL(1).

Answer: option A.

Common misconception. Thinking LL(1) forbids ε-productions. It only requires that the ε-alternative be distinguishable from the others by the lookahead, which is what the FOLLOW test checks.

CD-U2-Q19MSQmedium

Which of the following grammars are LL(1)? (Select all that apply.)

  1. S → a S b | ε
  2. S → A a | b, A → b | ε
  3. S → a A | b B, A → c | ε, B → c | ε
  4. E → E + T | T
  5. S → i E t S S′ | a, S′ → e S | ε, E → b
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Test each nonterminal: the FIRST sets of its alternatives must be disjoint, and where an alternative is nullable its FOLLOW set must not overlap the FIRST sets of the others. A grammar is LL(1) only if every nonterminal passes.

Step by step.

  1. S → a S b | ε: FIRST sets are {a} and {ε}; FOLLOW(S) = {b, $} does not contain a. LL(1).
  2. S → A a | b with A → b | ε: FIRST(A a) = {b, a} because A can be b or ε, and FIRST(b) = {b}. Both contain b, so M[S, b] gets two entries. Not LL(1).
  3. S → a A | b B, A → c | ε, B → c | ε: FOLLOW(A) = FOLLOW(B) = {$}, so M[A, c] = A → c, M[A, $] = A → ε and likewise for B. The two nonterminals never share a row, so no conflict. LL(1).
  4. E → E + T | T is left recursive: the two alternatives of E have the same FIRST set. Not LL(1).
  5. The dangling-else grammar has two entries in M[S′, e]. Not LL(1).

Answer: options (A) and (C).

Common misconception. Judging by the look of the rules. Option C looks suspicious because A and B have identical right sides, yet it is LL(1): conflicts are per row of the table, and A and B occupy different rows.

CD-U2-Q24MCQhard

For the grammar S → A a | b, A → b | ε, which statement about its LL(1) parsing table is TRUE?

  1. Exactly one cell, M[S, b], holds two entries, so the grammar is not LL(1).
  2. The grammar is LL(1), because the two alternatives of A begin differently.
  3. M[A, a] holds A → b.
  4. M[A, $] holds A → ε.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The FIRST set of a right side is computed left to right, and a nullable first symbol lets the following symbols contribute. It is FIRST(A a), not FIRST(A), that decides the row of S.

Step by step.

  1. FIRST(A) = {b, ε}, so FIRST(A a) = {b} ∪ {a} = {b, a}: A may be b, or it may vanish and let a begin the string.
  2. FIRST(b) = {b}, so M[S, b] receives both S → A a and S → b. That is the conflict.
  3. FOLLOW(A) = {a}, so M[A, b] = A → b and M[A, a] = A → ε; M[A, $] stays empty.
  4. So options C and D name cells that hold the wrong entry, and option B ignores the conflict in row S.

Answer: M[S, b] holds two entries — option A.

Common misconception. Checking only the alternatives of the "suspicious" nonterminal A. Here A is perfectly well behaved; the conflict is created one level up, by the nullability of A inside S → A a.

CD-U2-Q30MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about an LL(1) parsing table are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. A cell of the table may hold more than one production when the grammar is not LL(1).
  2. If a nonterminal A is nullable, the FOLLOW set of A decides which columns of row A receive A → ε.
  3. A nullable nonterminal contributes entries only in the columns of its own FIRST set.
  4. In the table of an LL(1) grammar no cell can hold two different productions.
  5. Deleting every ε-production from a grammar leaves its parsing table unchanged.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The table is filled by two rules: A → α goes into M[A, a] for each a ∈ FIRST(α), and A → ε goes into M[A, b] for each b ∈ FOLLOW(A). A grammar is LL(1) exactly when no cell receives two entries.

Step by step.

  1. (A) True: when two rules place different productions in one cell, that cell holds both, and the grammar is not LL(1).
  2. (B) True: A → ε can be applied only when the lookahead can follow A, which is precisely FOLLOW(A).
  3. (C) False: A → ε is placed using FOLLOW, not FIRST, so a nullable nonterminal also writes into columns outside its FIRST set.
  4. (D) True: that is the definition of LL(1) — a single entry per cell gives the parser a unique move for every lookahead.
  5. (E) False: ε-productions create the FOLLOW-driven entries, so deleting them removes cells and changes the language.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D).

Common misconception. Believing that a nullable nonterminal writes only into its FIRST columns. The ε-alternative is entered through FOLLOW, which is how a predictive parser knows the nonterminal may vanish here.

CD-U2-Q31MCQmedium

For the grammar S → a A | b, A → c | ε, which cell of the LL(1) parsing table holds the production A → ε?

  1. M[A, c]
  2. M[A, b]
  3. M[A, $]
  4. M[S, $]
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A production A → ε is entered in the columns given by FOLLOW(A): the parser may drop A exactly when the next input symbol is one that can legitimately follow A.

Step by step.

  1. FOLLOW(A) = FOLLOW(S) = {$}, because A appears only at the end of S → a A.
  2. So A → ε goes into M[A, $].
  3. The alternative A → c occupies M[A, c] instead, so no cell receives two entries and the grammar is LL(1).

Answer: M[A, $] — option (C).

Common misconception. Entering A → ε in M[A, c]. The column is the lookahead token that triggers the move, and ε is applied when the lookahead follows A, not when it starts A.

CD-U2-Q35MCQeasy

In an LL(1) parsing table, the rows are indexed by ______ and the columns by ______.

  1. terminals; nonterminals
  2. nonterminals; terminals together with $
  3. productions; input symbols
  4. nonterminals; productions
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The table answers one question: given the nonterminal on top of the stack and the current input symbol, which production should be applied?

Step by step.

  1. The parser looks up M[A, a] where A is the nonterminal on top of the stack.
  2. So rows are nonterminals and columns are the lookahead symbols — the terminals of the grammar plus the end marker $.
  3. Each entry is either empty (a syntax error) or a single production, which is what makes the parse deterministic.

Answer: Rows: nonterminals; columns: terminals plus $ — option (B).

Common misconception. Swapping the two axes. The row is the symbol being expanded, the column is the token being looked at.

CD-U2-Q37NAThard

For the grammar S → A B | c, A → a | ε, B → b, how many cells of the LL(1) parsing table are non-empty? Count a cell once however many entries it holds. (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Every production writes into one cell per symbol of its FIRST set, and an ε-production writes into one cell per symbol of FOLLOW. Rows that exist are only those of nonterminals that actually own productions.

Step by step.

  1. FIRST(A) = {a, ε}, FOLLOW(A) = FIRST(B) = {b}, FOLLOW(B) = FOLLOW(S) = {$}.
  2. Row S: S → A B covers a (from A) and b (A nullable, so B contributes); S → c covers c — three cells.
  3. Row A: A → a covers a; A → ε covers the column b from FOLLOW(A) — two cells.
  4. Row B: B → b covers b — one cell.
  5. No cell receives two entries, so the grammar is LL(1) and the total is 3 + 2 + 1 = 6.

Answer: 6

Common misconception. Answering 5 by stopping the row of S after the terminal a, forgetting that a nullable A lets the same production also cover the column b.

CD-U2-Q49MCQmedium

Which one of the following statements about LL(1) grammars is TRUE?

  1. LL(1)-ness is a property of the language, so every grammar for an LL(1) language is LL(1).
  2. LL(1)-ness is a property of the grammar, and a grammar that is not LL(1) may still generate a language that has an LL(1) grammar.
  3. Every unambiguous grammar is LL(1).
  4. Every deterministic context-free language has an LL(1) grammar.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. LL(1) is a property of a grammar — it is decided by inspecting FIRST and FOLLOW sets of productions — while the language is the set of strings the grammar generates.

Step by step.

  1. The LL(1) test is applied to productions and their FIRST/FOLLOW sets, so it classifies grammars, not languages.
  2. The same language can have several grammars: the left-recursive E → E + T | T is not LL(1), yet the factored version E → T E′, E′ → + T E′ | ε generates the same language and is LL(1).
  3. (C) is false: S → a S a | a is unambiguous but not LL(1), because both alternatives put a in FIRST.
  4. (D) is false: deterministic context-free languages need not be LL(1); {aⁿ bⁿ} ∪ {aⁿ cⁿ} is deterministic but has no LL(1) grammar.

Answer: Option (B) — LL(1)-ness belongs to the grammar, not the language.

Common misconception. Assuming that rewriting a language into a nicer grammar always rescues LL(1)-ness. Some deterministic languages have no LL(1) grammar at all, so the property is not a property of the language.

CD-U2-Q57MCQeasy

A grammar has 6 nonterminals and 9 terminals, and its LL(1) parsing table has one row per nonterminal and one column per terminal. How many cells does the table have?

  1. 54
  2. 60
  3. 55
  4. 15
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The LL(1) table M[A, a] is indexed by nonterminals on the rows and by terminals plus the end marker $ on the columns.

Step by step.

  1. Rows: one per nonterminal, so 6.
  2. Columns: one per terminal, 9, plus the column for $, which is needed for the ε-entries of nullable nonterminals and for matching the end of input. That is 10.
  3. 6 × 10 = 60 cells.

Final answer. Option (B), 60 cells.

Common misconception. Computing 6 × 9 = 54 by forgetting the $ column. Without it there is nowhere to record that a nullable nonterminal may vanish at the end of the input.

CD-U2-Q58MCQeasy

In a grammar whose start symbol is S, the production S → ε is entered into which cell of the LL(1) parsing table?

  1. M[S, ε], the column labelled ε.
  2. M[S, $], but only if some other nonterminal can follow S.
  3. Every cell of row S, because ε can begin nothing.
  4. M[S, $], because $ is the only symbol in FOLLOW(S).
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A production A → α goes into M[A, a] for every a ∈ FIRST(α), and, when α is nullable, also for every a ∈ FOLLOW(A).

Step by step.

  1. FIRST(ε) = {ε}, so the FIRST rule contributes no terminal column.
  2. The FOLLOW rule then places the production in every column named by FOLLOW(S).
  3. S is the start symbol and appears on no right side, so FOLLOW(S) = {$}.
  4. Hence the single entry is M[S, $] = S → ε.

Final answer. Option (D).

Common misconception. Looking for a column headed ε. There is no such column: ε is not a terminal, and a nullable production is reached through the FOLLOW set of its head.

CD-U2-Q59NATmedium

Consider the grammar S → A B, A → a | ε, B → b | ε. How many cells of its LL(1) parsing table contain an ε-production? (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. An ε-production for A is written into exactly the cells M[A, a] with a ∈ FOLLOW(A). So the count is the sum of |FOLLOW(A)| over the nullable nonterminals A.

Step by step.

  1. FOLLOW(S) = {$}.
  2. FOLLOW(A): A is followed by B, so {b} ⊆ FOLLOW(A); B is nullable, so FOLLOW(S) ⊆ FOLLOW(A). Hence {b, $}, giving two cells: M[A, b] and M[A, $].
  3. FOLLOW(B) = {$}, giving one cell: M[B, $].
  4. 2 + 1 = 3 cells.

Final answer. 3.

Common misconception. Counting one cell per ε-production, or placing the entries in the FIRST columns of the nonterminal. The FOLLOW set, not the FIRST set, decides where a nullable production lands.

CD-U2-Q60MCQmedium

The grammar S → a A | a B, A → b, B → c is not LL(1). Which one of the following is the correct reason?

  1. Because A and B each derive a single terminal, so no lookahead can tell them apart.
  2. Because FOLLOW(A) and FOLLOW(B) both contain $.
  3. Because both alternatives of S begin with a, so both are entered in the single cell M[S, a].
  4. Because the grammar has no ε-production, so row S is left empty.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. An LL(1) table cell may hold at most one production. A conflict means that the lookahead token alone does not decide which alternative to take.

Step by step.

  1. FIRST(a A) = {a} and FIRST(a B) = {a}.
  2. Both rules therefore write into M[S, a], which ends up holding S → a A and S → a B.
  3. The two alternatives share the one-symbol prefix a; the parser would have to read past a to choose, which is exactly what one token of lookahead cannot do.
  4. The conflict is removed by left factoring: S → a S′, S′ → A | B, which is LL(1).

Final answer. Option (C).

Common misconception. Blaming the shared FOLLOW sets. FOLLOW plays no part here: neither alternative is nullable, so the FOLLOW rule adds nothing and the whole conflict comes from FIRST.

CD-U2-Q61MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about the LL(1) parsing table are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. A cell may hold two entries only if the grammar is not LL(1).
  2. If some cell of the table holds two entries, then the grammar is ambiguous.
  3. The table is built from the FIRST and FOLLOW sets alone; no parse tree has to be constructed.
  4. In the table of an LL(1) grammar every cell of every row is non-empty.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The table is a two-dimensional lookup built mechanically from FIRST and FOLLOW. LL(1)-ness is exactly the absence of multi-entry cells.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is true by the definition of LL(1): at most one production per cell is the whole condition.
  2. (B) is false. A conflict means the grammar cannot be parsed with one token of lookahead, which is a weaker failure than ambiguity. The dangling-else grammar has a conflict in M[S′, e] and is still unambiguous.
  3. (C) is true: the construction walks the productions once, using FIRST(α) and, for nullable α, FOLLOW(A).
  4. (D) is false. Most cells are empty; for the grammar of the expression nonterminals, row E has entries only under id and (.

Final answer. (A) and (C).

Common misconception. Equating "not LL(1)" with "ambiguous". Not LL(1) only says one token of lookahead is insufficient; the grammar may still have exactly one parse tree per string.

CD-U2-Q62MCQhard

Consider the grammar S → A B, A → a | ε, B → b | ε, in which both A and B are nullable. Which one of the following statements is TRUE?

  1. It is not LL(1), because a nullable nonterminal always creates a conflict.
  2. It is not LL(1), because the cell M[S, b] receives both S → A B and B → b.
  3. It is LL(1): every cell of its table holds at most one entry, even though both A and B are nullable.
  4. It is LL(1), but only after the two ε-productions are deleted from the grammar.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Nullability is not a defect. A nullable nonterminal merely spreads its entries over its FOLLOW set, and the grammar stays LL(1) as long as no cell ends up with two entries.

Step by step.

  1. FOLLOW(S) = {$}; FOLLOW(A) = {b, $}; FOLLOW(B) = {$}.
  2. Row S: FIRST(A B) = {a, b, ε}, so S → A B goes into M[S, a], M[S, b] and M[S, $]. One entry in each.
  3. Row A: A → a in M[A, a]; A → ε in M[A, b] and M[A, $].
  4. Row B: B → b in M[B, b]; B → ε in M[B, $].
  5. Eight non-empty cells, none with two entries. In particular M[S, b] holds only S → A B: B → b is a production of B and can only ever be entered in row B.

Final answer. Option (C).

Common misconception. Believing that a nullable nonterminal forces a conflict, or that a production of B can be written into row S. Entries live in the row of the production's head.

CD-U2-Q63MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about LL(1) grammars are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. No grammar that is left recursive can be LL(1).
  2. Every LL(1) grammar is unambiguous.
  3. If a language has an LL(1) grammar, then every grammar for that language is LL(1).
  4. Every grammar that is not LL(1) can be rewritten into an LL(1) grammar for the same language.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. LL(1)-ness is a property of a grammar, not of a language. It implies unambiguity, but it is not implied by unambiguity, and it is not preserved by arbitrary re-grammaring.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is true. With A → A α | β, the alternative A α begins with a symbol that derives A again, so FIRST(A α) and FIRST(β) cannot be kept apart; a top-down parser would recurse on A for ever. Left recursion must be removed first.
  2. (B) is true: an LL(1) parser produces exactly one leftmost derivation for each accepted string, so two distinct parse trees are impossible.
  3. (C) is false. The classic expression language has the LL(1) grammar E → T E′, E′ → + T E′ | ε and the non-LL(1) grammar E → E + T | T.
  4. (D) is false. Not every context-free language has an LL(1) grammar — a language with an inherently ambiguous grammar, or one needing unbounded lookahead, is a counterexample.

Final answer. (A) and (B).

Common misconception. Swapping "LL(1) implies unambiguous" for its converse. Unambiguous grammars can still fail LL(1), for example when two alternatives share a prefix.

Recursive-descent parsers · 18
CD-U2-Q09MCQeasy

A recursive-descent parser for an LL(1) grammar must choose one alternative of A when the next input token is x. It chooses the alternative α such that

  1. x ∈ FIRST(α); if no alternative satisfies this and A is nullable, it takes A → ε whenever x ∈ FOLLOW(A).
  2. x ∈ FOLLOW(α) for every alternative α of A.
  3. x ∈ FIRST(A), taking the first alternative listed whenever several qualify.
  4. it tries every alternative in turn and backtracks when one fails.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. In predictive (LL(1)) parsing the lookahead token must identify the alternative uniquely: the token is either one that the alternative can start with, or — when the alternative is ε — one that may legitimately follow the nonterminal.

Step by step.

  1. If x ∈ FIRST(α) the alternative can begin with x, so expanding it is correct.
  2. If no alternative can begin with x but x ∈ FOLLOW(A), then A must produce nothing here and the parser takes A → ε.
  3. LL(1) guarantees these two tests never both apply and never leave the choice open, so no backtracking is needed (option D is the definition of a non-predictive parser).
  4. FOLLOW(α) (option B) is not defined — FOLLOW is a property of a nonterminal, not of a right side.

Answer: the FIRST test, with the FOLLOW test for the nullable case — option A.

Common misconception. Using FIRST(A) instead of FIRST(α). FIRST(A) is the union over all alternatives, so it cannot distinguish them — which is precisely the information the parser needs.

CD-U2-Q12NAThard

A predictive parser uses the grammar E → T E′, E′ → + T E′ | ε, T → F T′, T′ → * F T′ | ε, F → ( E ) | id. For the input id + id it applies a sequence of productions. How many productions are applied in total, counting each ε-production that is applied? (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A predictive parser expands nonterminals left to right, and each expansion is the application of exactly one production. The count is therefore the number of nonterminal expansions in the leftmost derivation of the input, including the expansions of the ′-nonterminals to ε.

Step by step.

  1. E → T E′ — 1.
  2. T → F T′ — 2.
  3. F → id, matching the first id — 3.
  4. T′ → ε, since the lookahead is + and + ∈ FOLLOW(T′) — 4.
  5. E′ → + T E′, consuming the + — 5.
  6. T → F T′ — 6.
  7. F → id, matching the second id — 7.
  8. T′ → ε, lookahead $ — 8.
  9. E′ → ε, lookahead $ — 9.

Answer: 9 productions.

Common misconception. Counting only the "visible" productions and dropping the two ε-expansions. Every nonterminal in the sentential form must be expanded, and a nonterminal that disappears by ε still costs one production.

CD-U2-Q15MSQhard

Which of the following statements about recursive-descent parsing are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Without memoisation, a backtracking recursive-descent parser can take time exponential in the input length on some grammars.
  2. For an LL(1) grammar one token of lookahead always selects the alternative, so no backtracking is needed.
  3. Recursive descent handles a left-recursive grammar directly, because the recursion in the parser mirrors the recursion in the grammar.
  4. Left recursion must be removed before a recursive-descent parser is written.
  5. A recursive-descent parser builds the parse tree bottom-up, from the leaves to the root.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A recursive-descent parser has one procedure per nonterminal, and a procedure calls the procedures of the symbols on the right side of the alternative it chooses. It is therefore a top-down, leftmost-derivation parser, and its control flow copies the shape of the grammar.

Step by step.

  1. (A) true: with backtracking, each failed alternative may re-parse the same suffix, and the number of partial parses can grow exponentially — the classical reason backtracking descent is avoided in production compilers.
  2. (B) true: LL(1) means the parsing table has at most one entry per cell, and a recursive-descent parser implements that table directly.
  3. (C) false: for A → A α | β the procedure for A calls itself before consuming any input, so it recurses forever without making progress.
  4. (D) true: that is why the elimination of Unit-2 left-recursion removal precedes the parser construction.
  5. (E) false: bottom-up parsing (shift-reduce) builds the tree from the leaves; recursive descent builds it from the root downwards.

Answer: options (A), (B) and (D).

Common misconception. Assuming the parser's recursion can "handle" the grammar's recursion. The parser mirrors the grammar, so a left-recursive rule becomes an infinite loop rather than a left-associative parse.

CD-U2-Q21MCQeasy

In a table-driven predictive parser the symbol on top of the stack is the nonterminal A, the current input symbol is a, and M[A, a] = A → X Y Z. The parser

  1. pops A and pushes Z, then Y, then X, so that X is on top.
  2. pops a from the input and pushes A.
  3. shifts a onto the stack.
  4. reduces the handle X Y Z to A.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A predictive parser keeps a stack of grammar symbols with the start symbol at the bottom. When the top is a nonterminal it expands it using the table; when the top is a terminal it must match the current input symbol and is popped.

Step by step.

  1. The stack is … A with A on top, so A is popped.
  2. The right side X Y Z is pushed in reverse order — Z first, then Y, then X — so that X, the leftmost symbol, ends up on top and is matched next.
  3. Nothing is consumed from the input at this step; the input pointer moves only when a terminal on top matches.
  4. Options B, C and D describe shift-reduce (bottom-up) behaviour, not predictive parsing.

Answer: option A.

Common misconception. Pushing the right side left to right. That would put Z on top and the parser would try to match the rightmost symbol first — a rightmost, not leftmost, derivation.

CD-U2-Q22MCQmedium

A backtracking recursive-descent parser is used on a grammar with no LL(1) guarantee. In the worst case, for an input of length n, the work it may do grows

  1. linearly in n
  2. as n log n
  3. exponentially in n
  4. as n²
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A predictive (LL(1)) parser is linear because the table fixes every decision. A backtracking parser instead explores alternatives, and when several alternatives can match the same prefix the number of partial parses can double with every token.

Step by step.

  1. On failure the parser restores the input position and tries the next alternative, re-parsing the same suffix.
  2. If the grammar allows k alternatives at each of n levels, the search tree has on the order of kn nodes.
  3. Classic examples, such as the ambiguous expression grammar, show this exponential blow-up in practice.
  4. Memoisation (packrat parsing) removes the repetition and restores linear time at the cost of memory — but that is no longer plain backtracking.

Answer: exponentially in n — option C.

Common misconception. Assuming any parser is linear in the input. Linearity is a property of the deterministic table-driven method, not of top-down parsing in general.

CD-U2-Q25MCQeasy

Which of the following is true of every top-down parser?

  1. It builds a leftmost derivation of the input string.
  2. It builds a rightmost derivation in reverse.
  3. It reduces handles at the top of the stack.
  4. It can parse every context-free grammar.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A top-down parser starts at the start symbol and expands nonterminals until the frontier matches the input. Expanding the leftmost nonterminal at every step produces a leftmost derivation, so the parse tree grows from the root towards the leaves.

Step by step.

  1. Recursive descent and LL(1) table-driven parsing both expand the leftmost nonterminal, giving a leftmost derivation — statement A.
  2. Statement B describes bottom-up (shift-reduce) parsing, which reconstructs a rightmost derivation in reverse.
  3. Statement C is the handle-reduction step of LR parsing, again bottom-up.
  4. Statement D is false: a top-down parser needs a grammar free of left recursion and FIRST/FIRST conflicts; many context-free grammars, including all ambiguous ones, are outside its reach.

Answer: option A.

Common misconception. Confusing "top-down" with "backtracking" or with "handles". Top-down means root-to-leaves and leftmost expansion; handles and rightmost derivations belong to the bottom-up family.

CD-U2-Q27MCQeasy

In a table-driven predictive parser the symbol on top of the stack is a terminal that equals the current input symbol. The parser must

  1. replace the stack top by the right side of a production.
  2. pop the terminal from the stack and advance the input to the next symbol.
  3. report a syntax error, because a terminal can never reach the top of the stack.
  4. push the current input symbol onto the stack and continue.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The parser has three actions only: expand a nonterminal using the table, match a terminal against the input, and report an error. A match consumes one symbol from each side.

Step by step.

  1. The stack top is a terminal and the input symbol equals it, so the two agree on the next symbol of the sentential form.
  2. The parser pops that terminal from the stack and advances the input pointer past the matching symbol.
  3. No production is applied: expansions are only triggered when the stack top is a nonterminal.

Answer: Pop the stack top and advance the input — option (B).

Common misconception. Choosing to expand a production here. The table M[A, a] is consulted only when the stack top A is a nonterminal; a terminal on top is matched, never expanded.

CD-U2-Q33MCQeasy

A recursive-descent procedure is written for the alternative A → a B c. It has matched a, called the procedure for B, and that call has returned successfully. What must it do next?

  1. match the token c and advance the input past it.
  2. call the procedure for A again, since A appears on the left side.
  3. push B onto an explicit stack.
  4. reduce a B c to A and return without reading further input.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A procedure for A walks the right side of its chosen production from left to right: a terminal is matched against the input, a nonterminal becomes a recursive call.

Step by step.

  1. The right side is a B c. The first symbol a was matched, and B was handled by its own procedure.
  2. The next symbol is the terminal c, so the procedure matches c against the current input symbol and advances.
  3. After the whole right side is consumed the procedure returns, and its caller continues with the symbol after A.

Answer: Match the terminal c and advance — option (A).

Common misconception. Expecting a recursive call for c. Only nonterminals have procedures; terminals are matched directly by consuming the input token.

CD-U2-Q38MCQeasy

In a predictive parser the stack top is the nonterminal A, the input symbol is a, and M[A, a] is empty. The parser must

  1. pop A and continue with the next symbol on the stack.
  2. skip the input symbol a and try the next one.
  3. report a syntax error.
  4. apply the production A → ε.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. An empty cell means no production of A can start with the lookahead a and A cannot vanish here either; the input is not a sentence of the grammar.

Step by step.

  1. The table holds a production in M[A, a] exactly when A can legitimately produce something beginning with a.
  2. The cell is empty, so no legal move exists at this point of the derivation.
  3. A predictive parser has no backtracking to fall back on, so it stops and reports a syntax error.

Answer: Report a syntax error — option (C).

Common misconception. Applying A → ε. That production is legal only when the lookahead lies in FOLLOW(A), which is exactly the condition under which the cell would not be empty.

CD-U2-Q42MCQeasy

In a recursive-descent parser, the recursion of the host language replaces which component of a table-driven predictive parser?

  1. the input buffer
  2. the explicit parse stack
  3. the parsing table
  4. the output stream
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Both parsers are predictive and consult the same FIRST/FOLLOW information; they differ only in how the pending sentential form is held.

Step by step.

  1. A table-driven parser keeps the pending symbols on an explicit stack.
  2. A recursive-descent parser keeps them implicitly: each pending nonterminal is an active procedure call, and the call stack records the symbols still to be matched.
  3. The production to apply is still chosen by the same FIRST/FOLLOW test, so the table is not what the recursion replaces.

Answer: The explicit parse stack — option (B).

Common misconception. Saying the recursion replaces the parsing table. The choice of alternative is still driven by FIRST/FOLLOW; only the storage of the pending form moves to the call stack.

CD-U2-Q44MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about recursive-descent and predictive parsing are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. A table-driven predictive parser keeps an explicit stack in place of the procedure calls of a recursive-descent parser.
  2. A recursive-descent parser for an LL(1) grammar needs no backtracking.
  3. The parse tree built by a top-down parser corresponds to a rightmost derivation.
  4. The procedure for a nonterminal returns after consuming exactly the input derived from that nonterminal.
  5. A hand-written recursive-descent parser for an LL(1) grammar may still need more than one token of lookahead.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A top-down parser builds a leftmost derivation: it expands the leftmost nonterminal first, and the frontier of the tree always matches the input read so far.

Step by step.

  1. (A) True: the two parsers are the same algorithm with the pending sentential form stored differently — on an explicit stack or on the call stack.
  2. (B) True: the FIRST/FOLLOW test picks a unique production for each lookahead, so no alternative ever has to be undone.
  3. (C) False: top-down parsing expands the leftmost nonterminal first, giving a leftmost derivation; a rightmost derivation in reverse is what a bottom-up parser builds.
  4. (D) True: that is the contract of the procedure — it consumes exactly the string derived from its nonterminal and leaves the rest of the input untouched.
  5. (E) False: for an LL(1) grammar one token of lookahead always determines the choice, which is what the "1" in LL(1) asserts.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D).

Common misconception. Believing a top-down parser produces a rightmost derivation. Leftmost expansion is the definition of top-down; the reverse of a rightmost derivation is the bottom-up (shift-reduce) order.

CD-U2-Q48NAThard

A predictive parser uses E → T E′, E′ → + T E′ | ε, T → F T′, T′ → * F T′ | ε, F → ( E ) | id. For the input ( id ), how many productions are applied in total, counting each application separately? (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Every nonterminal expansion costs one production application, including each ε-production used to close off E′ or T′. Terminals are matched, not expanded, so they cost nothing.

Step by step.

  1. E → T E′ (1), T → F T′ (2), F → ( E ) (3) — the opening parenthesis is matched.
  2. Inside the parentheses: E → T E′ (4), T → F T′ (5), F → id (6).
  3. T′ sees ), not *, so T′ → ε (7); E′ sees ), not +, so E′ → ε (8); the closing parenthesis is matched.
  4. Back in the outer T: T′ → ε (9) on the lookahead $; then E′ → ε (10).
  5. Total 10 productions; the two parentheses and the identifier are matched, not expanded.

Answer: 10

Common misconception. Answering 6 by counting only the productions that add terminals. Each ε-production is a real expansion and costs one application.

CD-U2-Q64MCQeasy

A table-driven predictive parser for a grammar with start symbol S begins with which stack contents?

  1. An empty stack.
  2. S with $ beneath it, so that S is on top.
  3. $ with S beneath it, so that $ is on top.
  4. The first token of the input.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The stack holds the unexpanded remainder of the current sentential form, with the leftmost symbol on top, and $ at the bottom to mark the end of the input.

Step by step.

  1. The parser must expand S first, so S has to be on top.
  2. Underneath it sits $, matching the end marker appended to the input string.
  3. Expanding a nonterminal pops it and pushes its right side in reverse, so the leftmost symbol of the alternative ends up on top. That is what keeps the derivation leftmost.
  4. Option (C) has the two symbols the wrong way round: the parser would try to match $ before expanding anything.

Final answer. Option (B).

Common misconception. Pushing the right side of a production in reading order. It must be pushed in reverse so that its first symbol is on top.

CD-U2-Q65MCQeasy

A recursive-descent parser has just returned successfully from the procedure for the start symbol, but input tokens remain. What should it do?

  1. Report a syntax error, because the input contains tokens that the start symbol does not derive.
  2. Call the procedure for the start symbol again on the remaining input.
  3. Accept, because the start symbol has been matched.
  4. Discard the remaining tokens and accept.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The start symbol must derive the whole input, so success of its procedure counts only when the input pointer has reached the end marker.

Step by step.

  1. The procedure for S has consumed the prefix that S derives.
  2. Any token still unread lies outside that prefix, so the input is not a string of the language.
  3. In a table-driven parser the same situation is detected by the final stack check: after matching $ both stack and input must be empty.

Final answer. Option (A).

Common misconception. Treating the start symbol's return as acceptance. A prefix of a sentence is not a sentence.

CD-U2-Q66NATmedium

A table-driven predictive parser uses E → T E′, E′ → + T E′ | ε, T → F T′, T′ → * F T′ | ε, F → ( E ) | id, starting with E on top of the stack and $ beneath it. For the input id * id + id, what is the largest number of symbols that are on the stack at the same moment at any point of the parse, counting $ as one of them? (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The stack height is the length of the unexpanded remainder of the sentential form, plus the end marker. Expanding a nonterminal pops one symbol and pushes the symbols of its right side.

Step by step.

  1. Start: [$ , E], height 2.
  2. E → T E′ gives [$ , E′ , T], height 3; then T → F T′ gives [$ , E′ , T′ , F], height 4.
  3. F → id matches id, leaving [$ , E′ , T′]. The lookahead is *, so T′ → * F T′ pushes three symbols and the stack becomes [$ , E′ , T′ , F , *] — height 5, the maximum.
  4. After the second id, T′ → ε drops the height to 2; E′ → + T E′ raises it to 4 again, and the rest of the parse stays at 4 or below.
  5. The parse ends with $ matching, height 1 and then 0.

Final answer. 5.

Common misconception. Measuring the height just after pushing a right side of three symbols while ignoring the symbols already on the stack beneath it. The height is the whole stack, not the production's right side.

CD-U2-Q67MCQeasy

A table-driven predictive parser for an LL(1) grammar is run on an input string that is not in the language. Which one of the following is guaranteed?

  1. It reports the error before applying any production.
  2. It always backtracks to the most recent point at which more than one alternative was possible.
  3. It reports the error only after the whole input has been consumed.
  4. It may apply several productions and then report the error at the first point where the table lookup fails or a terminal does not match the lookahead.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A predictive parser never backtracks, so it cannot "try again". It detects an error at the first step where the table gives no action or a terminal fails to match the lookahead.

Step by step.

  1. Productions that are consistent with the prefix read so far are applied normally; nothing marks them as wrong yet, so option (A) is false.
  2. There is no choice point to return to — the table gave one answer at every step — so option (B) is false.
  3. Error detection happens at the first offending token, not at the end, so option (C) is false.
  4. Option (D) describes the mechanism exactly: the failure is reported where M[top, lookahead] is empty, or where a stack terminal differs from the lookahead.

Final answer. Option (D).

Common misconception. Assuming a non-backtracking parser must catch the error immediately. It catches it as soon as the error becomes detectable, which may be several productions into the parse.

CD-U2-Q68MSQhard

Which of the following statements about recursive-descent parsing are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. A recursive-descent parser for a grammar that is not left factored may need backtracking even when the grammar is unambiguous.
  2. The call stack of the host language plays the role that the explicit stack plays in a table-driven parser.
  3. Removing left recursion from a grammar can change the language it generates.
  4. A table-driven parser and a recursive-descent parser for the same LL(1) grammar apply the same sequence of productions to a given input.
  5. A recursive-descent parser cannot handle a grammar with ε-productions.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Recursive descent is the same algorithm as table-driven predictive parsing with the explicit stack replaced by procedure calls. The two differ in representation, not in the derivation they build.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is true: without left factoring the alternatives of a nonterminal can share a prefix, so the procedure cannot choose from one token and must be allowed to retry. Unambiguity does not help.
  2. (B) is true: each recursive call records "which alternative am I in, and where did I get to", which is exactly what the explicit stack holds.
  3. (C) is false. Both the direct and the general elimination preserve the generated language; they only reshape the derivations.
  4. (D) is true: both consult the same FIRST/FOLLOW information, so both apply the unique LL(1) choice at every step.
  5. (E) is false. An ε-alternative becomes a procedure body that simply returns when the lookahead is in FOLLOW of the nonterminal.

Final answer. (A), (B) and (D).

Common misconception. Thinking recursive descent is a weaker technique than the table-driven parser. For the same LL(1) grammar the two are step-for-step identical.

CD-U2-Q69MCQeasy

A table-driven predictive parser for a fixed LL(1) grammar scans the input once and never backtracks. On an input of n tokens, how does its running time grow?

  1. O(n log n)
  2. O(n)
  3. O(n²)
  4. exponentially in n
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Each step of the parser either pops a stack symbol or shifts one input token, and each step is a constant-time table lookup. The total number of steps is proportional to the length of the parse.

Step by step.

  1. One production application pops one symbol and pushes at most k symbols, where k is the longest right side of the fixed grammar.
  2. The number of productions applied to derive a string of n terminals is O(n), because every application contributes at least one terminal eventually.
  3. Hence the total work is O(n) table lookups — linear, with a constant that depends on the grammar, not on the input.

Final answer. Option (B), O(n).

Common misconception. Confusing predictive parsing with general CFG parsing. The cubic and exponential bounds belong to algorithms for arbitrary grammars (CYK, Earley, backtracking descent), not to LL(1) table-driven parsing.

Left recursion & factoring · 18
CD-U2-Q02MCQeasy

For the grammar E → E + T | T, T → T * F | F, F → ( E ) | id, immediate left recursion is removed from E alone. The productions for E and for the new nonterminal E′ are then

  1. E → T E′, E′ → + T E′ | ε
  2. E → E′ T, E′ → T + E′ | ε
  3. E → T E′, E′ → T + E′ | ε
  4. E → T + E′ | T, E′ → E′ + T | ε
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A rule of the form A → A α | β generates the strings β α*. Replacing it by A → β A′ and A′ → α A′ | ε produces exactly the same language without the left recursion.

Step by step.

  1. Here A = E, α = + T and β = T.
  2. The recursive alternative E → E + T is rewritten so that the recursion moves to the right: E′ → + T E′.
  3. The base alternative becomes E → T E′, and E′ → ε terminates the repetition.
  4. Option C reverses the order inside E′ to T + E′, which generates T T + … — a different language. Option B moves E′ to the wrong side. Option D leaves the recursion in place.

Answer: E → T E′, E′ → + T E′ | ε — option A.

Common misconception. Swapping the order of the symbols while "moving" the recursion. The recursive tail must stay exactly as it was — + T, in that order — and only its head changes from E to E′.

CD-U2-Q03NATeasy

The grammar A → A a | A b | c is transformed by removing immediate left recursion, which yields A → c A′ together with the productions of A′. How many productions does the transformed grammar contain in total — the productions for A plus all the productions for A′? (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. For A → A α1 | … | A αm | β1 | … | βn the elimination gives A → β1 A′ | … | βn A′ and A′ → α1 A′ | … | αm A′ | ε.

Step by step.

  1. Two recursive alternatives, so m = 2: a and b.
  2. One base alternative, so n = 1: c.
