  3. A keeps one production: A → c A′.
  4. A′ gets A′ → a A′, A′ → b A′ and A′ → ε — three productions.
  5. Total 1 + 3 = 4.

Answer: 4 productions.

Common misconception. Forgetting the ε-production of A′, or merging the two recursive alternatives into a single A′ → a | b A′ line. Each alternative of the original rule stays a separate production of A′, and ε is one more.

CD-U2-Q10MCQmedium

The grammar A → a b c | a b d | a e is fully left factored. The result is

  1. A → a A′, A′ → b c | b d | e
  2. A → a A′, A′ → b A″ | e, A″ → c | d
  3. A → a A′, A′ → b A″ | e A″′, A″ → c | d
  4. A → A′ a, A′ → b c | b d | e
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Left factoring pushes the longest common prefix of two alternatives into a new nonterminal, so that one token of lookahead decides between the remainders. It is applied repeatedly until no two alternatives of the same nonterminal share a first symbol.

Step by step.

  1. All three alternatives begin with a, so factor it once: A → a A′ with A′ → b c | b d | e.
  2. A′ still has two alternatives beginning with b, so factor again: A′ → b A″ | e and A″ → c | d.
  3. Now every nonterminal has alternatives with distinct FIRST sets: FIRST(c) = {c}, FIRST(d) = {d}, FIRST(b A″) = {b}, FIRST(e) = {e}. Factoring is complete.
  4. Option A stops after one step and leaves a FIRST/FIRST conflict on b; option C introduces a needless nonterminal for e.

Answer: A → a A′, A′ → b A″ | e, A″ → c | d — option B.

Common misconception. Factoring only the outer common symbol. A single pass leaves the inner pair b c and b d still ambiguous on b; factoring is applied until no FIRST conflict remains.

CD-U2-Q11MSQmedium

Which of the following transformations leave the language generated by a grammar unchanged? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Replacing A → A α | β, where β does not begin with A, by A → β A′, A′ → α A′ | ε.
  2. Left factoring two alternatives that share a common prefix.
  3. Deleting every ε-production of the grammar.
  4. Deleting useless symbols, that is, non-generating and unreachable symbols.
  5. Reordering the alternatives of a non-LL(1) grammar until it becomes LL(1).
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A transformation is language-preserving when the set of terminal strings derivable from the start symbol is unchanged. Left-recursion elimination and left factoring are such transformations; deleting ε-productions and reordering alternatives are not, in general.

Step by step.

  1. (A) true: A → A α | β generates β αk for k ≥ 0, and so does A → β A′ with A′ → α A′ | ε.
  2. (B) true: factoring only regroups alternatives, it derives exactly the same strings.
  3. (C) false: dropping every ε-production removes ε from the language and breaks every derivation that relied on an optional part; a correct elimination must add compensating productions instead of merely deleting.
  4. (D) true: a symbol that generates no terminal string, or that can never be reached from the start symbol, contributes nothing to the language.
  5. (E) false: reordering alternatives cannot remove a FIRST/FIRST or FIRST/FOLLOW conflict; the ambiguity stays in the grammar. Only rewriting the rules helps.

Answer: options (A), (B) and (D).

Common misconception. Believing every "clean-up" step is safe. ε-elimination is language-preserving only when it is done properly — replicating the nullable symbols into the other right sides — and even then ε is lost unless the start symbol is nullable.

CD-U2-Q17MCQmedium

For the grammar S → A a | b, A → S d | c, indirect left recursion is removed by substituting the S-productions into A and then removing the immediate left recursion. The resulting productions for A are

  1. A → A a d | b d | c, with no further step needed
  2. A → b d A′ | c A′, A′ → a d A′ | ε
  3. A → b d | c, A′ → a d A′
  4. A → S d | c, unchanged, because the recursion is in S
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Indirect (mutual) left recursion is removed in two stages: substitute the earlier nonterminal's alternatives into the later one, which turns the indirect recursion into an immediate one, and then apply the standard immediate-elimination rule.

Step by step.

  1. Take the order S then A. In A → S d, replace S by its alternatives: A → A a d | b d, together with the existing A → c.
  2. Now A is immediately left recursive with α = a d and β ∈ {b d, c}.
  3. Apply the rule: A → b d A′ | c A′ and A′ → a d A′ | ε.
  4. S itself is not left recursive and stays as it is.

Answer: A → b d A′ | c A′, A′ → a d A′ | ε — option B.

Common misconception. Stopping after the substitution (option A) — the grammar is then still left recursive, only immediately so — or forgetting that the substituted alternative b d also needs the A′ suffix.

CD-U2-Q23MSQhard

Which of the following statements about left recursion and left factoring are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Left factoring can turn a grammar that is not LL(1) into an LL(1) grammar without changing the language.
  2. Removing left recursion always yields an LL(1) grammar.
  3. Indirect left recursion must be removed by substituting in a fixed order of nonterminals before the immediate left recursion is removed.
  4. A recursive-descent parser that meets a left-recursive alternative recurses forever.
  5. Left factoring introduces no new nonterminals.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Left recursion and FIRST/FIRST conflicts are the two obstacles to predictive parsing. Left-recursion elimination removes the first; left factoring removes the second. Neither is a complete LL(1) transformation by itself.

Step by step.

  1. (A) true: a grammar whose only defect is a shared prefix becomes LL(1) after factoring, and factoring preserves the language.
  2. (B) false: S → a A | b B, A → c | ε, B → c | ε is free of left recursion and is LL(1) — but S → A a | b, A → b | ε is also free of left recursion and is not LL(1), because of a FIRST/FIRST conflict.
  3. (C) true: the substitution step turns mutual recursion into immediate recursion, and it needs a fixed order (for example A1, …, An) or the process does not terminate.
  4. (D) true: the procedure for A calls itself before consuming input.
  5. (E) false: factoring creates a new nonterminal (here A′) for every common prefix it extracts.

Answer: options (A), (C) and (D).

Common misconception. Believing "no left recursion" implies "LL(1)". FIRST/FIRST and FIRST/FOLLOW conflicts survive left-recursion removal untouched.

CD-U2-Q29MCQmedium

The grammar A → x y z | x y w | x v is left factored as far as possible. Which one of the following is the result?

  1. A → x A′; A′ → y z | y w | v
  2. A → x A′; A′ → y A″ | v; A″ → z | w
  3. A → x A′; A′ → y A″ | v A‴; A″ → z | w
  4. A → A′ x; A′ → y z | y w | v
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Left factoring removes the longest common prefix of two or more alternatives and repeats on the remainder until no two alternatives of a nonterminal share a first symbol.

Step by step.

  1. All three alternatives begin with x, so A → x A′ with A′ → y z | y w | v.
  2. Two alternatives of A′ still share the prefix y, so factor again: A′ → y A″ | v.
  3. The remainder z | w has no common prefix: A″ → z | w.
  4. Factoring stops here because every nonterminal now has alternatives with distinct first symbols.

Answer: Option (B) — A → x A′; A′ → y A″ | v; A″ → z | w.

Common misconception. Stopping after one factoring step, which leaves A′ → y z | y w with the shared prefix y still in place, so FIRST sets of two alternatives still overlap.

CD-U2-Q32NATmedium

Immediate left recursion is removed from every nonterminal of the grammar E → E + T | T, T → T * F | F, F → ( E ) | id. How many productions does the resulting grammar contain in total? (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The rewrite for A → A α₁ | … | A αₘ | β₁ | … | βₙ is A → β₁ A′ | … | βₙ A′ together with A′ → α₁ A′ | … | αₘ A′ | ε: n productions for A, m + 1 for the new nonterminal.

Step by step.

  1. E has one β (namely T) and one α (+ T): E → T E′ and E′ → + T E′ | ε — 3 productions.
  2. T has one β (F) and one α (* F): T → F T′ and T′ → * F T′ | ε — 3 productions.
  3. F has no left recursion: F → ( E ) | id stays at 2 productions.
  4. Total 3 + 3 + 2 = 8 productions.

Answer: 8

Common misconception. Answering 6 by forgetting the new nonterminals, or 12 by counting each α-alternative as an extra production of A rather than of A′.

CD-U2-Q40MSQhard

Which of the following statements about the two preprocessing rewrites — eliminating left recursion and left factoring — are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. After immediate left recursion is removed from A → A α | β, no production of A begins with A.
  2. Left factoring never changes the language generated by the grammar.
  3. Removing indirect left recursion is enough to make any grammar LL(1).
  4. Left factoring always removes every LL(1) conflict a grammar has.
  5. A left-recursive grammar can be ambiguous.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Both transformations are language-preserving rewrites. Removing left recursion clears one obstacle to predictive parsing; left factoring clears another; neither is sufficient on its own.

Step by step.

  1. (A) True: the result is A → β A′ with A′ → α A′ | ε, and β does not begin with A by assumption, so no production of A starts with A.
  2. (B) True: factoring only regroups alternatives under a new nonterminal, so the set of derived strings is unchanged.
  3. (C) False: a grammar can be free of left recursion and still be non-LL(1), for instance when two alternatives share a common prefix or when FIRST/FOLLOW overlap at a nullable alternative.
  4. (D) False: left factoring removes conflicts caused by a shared prefix only; conflicts from nullable alternatives, or from a grammar that is inherently ambiguous, survive it.
  5. (E) True: E → E + E | E * E | id is left recursive and ambiguous, since id + id * id has two parse trees.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (E).

Common misconception. Treating the two rewrites as a cure for every LL(1) conflict. They are necessary repairs for two specific causes of conflict, not a guarantee of LL(1)-ness.

CD-U2-Q45MCQmedium

Immediate left recursion is removed from A → A a b | A c | d. Which one of the following is the result?

  1. A → d A′; A′ → a b A′ | c A′ | ε
  2. A → d A′; A′ → a A′ | b A′ | c A′ | ε
  3. A → A′ d; A′ → a b A′ | c A′ | ε
  4. A → d A′; A′ → b a A′ | c A′ | ε
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. With A → A α₁ | … | A αₘ | β, the rewrite is A → β A′ and A′ → α₁ A′ | … | αₘ A′ | ε, keeping each α intact as a whole string.

Step by step.

  1. The two recursive alternatives give α₁ = a b and α₂ = c; the non-recursive alternative gives β = d.
  2. So A → d A′.
  3. And A′ → a b A′ | c A′ | ε, with the two symbols of α₁ kept together and in order.
  4. A now generates d (ab | c)*, the same language as before.

Answer: Option (A) — A → d A′; A′ → a b A′ | c A′ | ε.

Common misconception. Splitting the multi-symbol α into separate alternatives, as option (B) does. The α must be carried across as one string; splitting it changes the language.

CD-U2-Q46MCQeasy

The left-recursive grammar S → S a | b generates which language?

  1. b a*
  2. a* b
  3. (a b)*
  4. b* a
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Reading the grammar semantically: the only way to stop the recursion is the base alternative b, and each recursive step appends one a to the right.

Step by step.

  1. Every derivation must begin with the base case: S ⇒ b.
  2. Each application of S → S a appends a single a at the right end.
  3. So the strings are b, b a, b a a, …, that is b a*.
  4. Left recursion changes how the strings are derived, not which strings exist.

Answer: b a* — option (A).

Common misconception. Reading the recursion as prefixing, giving a* b. The recursion S → S a puts the new a on the right of what S already produced.

CD-U2-Q47MCQhard

Consider A → B a | c, B → A b | d. Indirect left recursion is removed by substituting the A-productions into B and then removing the immediate left recursion that results. Which one of the following describes the outcome?

  1. B → B a b | c b | d, with no further step needed
  2. B → c b B′ | d B′; B′ → a b B′ | ε, with A → B a | c unchanged
  3. B → a b B′ | d B′, B′ → c b B′ | ε, with A → B a | c unchanged
  4. B → c b | d; B′ → a b B′, with A → B a | c unchanged
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Indirect recursion is removed in two steps: substitute so the recursion becomes immediate, then apply the immediate-left-recursion rewrite.

Step by step.

  1. Substitute A → B a | c into B → A b | d: B → B a b | c b | d.
  2. This is immediate left recursion with α = a b and β ∈ {c b, d}.
  3. Rewrite: B → c b B′ | d B′ and B′ → a b B′ | ε.
  4. A keeps its productions A → B a | c, which no longer involve a cycle through B.

Answer: Option (B) — B → c b B′ | d B′; B′ → a b B′ | ε, A unchanged.

Common misconception. Stopping after the substitution, as option (A) does. Substitution only exposes the left recursion; the rewrite still has to be applied to remove it.

CD-U2-Q70MCQeasy

Which one of the following grammars must be left factored before a predictive parser can be written for it?

  1. S → a S b | ε
  2. S → a b | a c
  3. S → S a | b
  4. S → a | b
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Left factoring is the rewrite for alternatives that share a common prefix: the common part is pulled out and the remainder becomes a new nonterminal. Left recursion is a different defect with a different rewrite.

Step by step.

  1. (A) has one alternative beginning with a and one that is ε; FIRST(a S b) = {a} and FOLLOW(S) decides the other, so it is already LL(1).
  2. (B) has two alternatives with the common prefix a, so both would occupy M[S, a]. Factoring gives S → a S′, S′ → b | c.
  3. (C) suffers from immediate left recursion, which is removed, not factored.
  4. (D) has alternatives beginning with different terminals and needs nothing.

Final answer. Option (B).

Common misconception. Applying the wrong rewrite. Left factoring cures a shared prefix; left recursion removal cures an alternative that begins with its own head.

CD-U2-Q71NATmedium

Immediate left recursion is removed from every nonterminal of E → E + T | T, T → T * F | F, F → ( E ) | id. How many new nonterminals does the transformation introduce? (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Removing immediate left recursion from a nonterminal that has it always introduces exactly one new nonterminal, whatever the number of left-recursive alternatives.

Step by step.

  1. E → E + T | T is left recursive, so a new nonterminal is created: E → T E′, E′ → + T E′ | ε. That is one.
  2. T → T * F | F is left recursive, so T′ is created: T → F T′, T′ → * F T′ | ε. That is two.
  3. F → ( E ) | id has no left-recursive alternative — neither right side begins with F — so nothing is introduced for F.
  4. Total: E′ and T′, that is 2.

Final answer. 2.

Common misconception. Adding a new nonterminal for every nonterminal, or adding one per left-recursive alternative. One per nonterminal that actually has immediate left recursion.

CD-U2-Q72NATmedium

The grammar S → S a | b c | b d is transformed in two steps: immediate left recursion is removed from S, and the result is then left factored as far as possible. How many productions does the final grammar contain in total? (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The two rewrites solve different problems and are applied in sequence: left recursion removal keeps the language, then left factoring removes the shared prefix that the first step leaves behind.

Step by step.

  1. Left recursion removal on S → S a | b c | b d gives S → b c S′ | b d S′ and S′ → a S′ | ε — four productions.
  2. The two alternatives of S now share the prefix b, so factor: S → b S″, S″ → c S′ | d S′.
  3. S″'s alternatives begin with c and d, so no further factoring is possible.
  4. Count: one production for S, two for S″, two for S′ — 1 + 2 + 2 = 5.

Final answer. 5.

Common misconception. Stopping after the first rewrite and reporting four productions. The grammar is not LL(1) at that point: M[S, b] would hold two entries.

CD-U2-Q73MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about left factoring are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Left factoring leaves the language generated by the grammar unchanged.
  2. Left factoring can turn an unambiguous grammar into an ambiguous one.
  3. A grammar in which two alternatives of one nonterminal begin with the same terminal cannot be LL(1).
  4. Left factoring alone removes the conflict caused by a left-recursive alternative.
  5. Left factoring three alternatives that share a two-symbol prefix may introduce more than one new nonterminal.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Left factoring defers a choice: the shared prefix is matched once and the choice between the remainders is made later. It is a language-preserving rewrite that attacks FIRST/FIRST conflicts, and it does nothing about left recursion.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is true: factoring rewrites A → α β | α γ as A → α A′, A′ → β | γ, and the set of derived strings is identical.
  2. (B) is false. Factoring can only reduce the number of ways to begin an alternative; it cannot create a second parse tree where none existed.
  3. (C) is true: both alternatives would be entered in M[A, a], whatever follows a.
  4. (D) is false. A → A a | b begins one alternative with A itself; only left recursion removal can fix that.
  5. (E) is true: factoring x y z | x y w | x v needs two levels — A → x A′, A′ → y A″ | v, A″ → z | w — so two new nonterminals appear.

Final answer. (A), (C) and (E).

Common misconception. Believing one factoring pass always suffices. Alternatives that share a prefix of length greater than one usually need one new nonterminal per level of the shared prefix.

CD-U2-Q74MCQhard

The left-recursive grammar E → E + T | T is replaced by E → T E′, E′ → + T E′ | ε, which generates the same language. Which one of the following statements is TRUE?

  1. The new grammar generates a different language, because the recursion has been moved.
  2. The new grammar generates the same language and keeps the left-associative meaning without any change to the semantic actions.
  3. The new grammar generates the same language, but its parse trees nest to the right, so the semantic actions must fold each left operand in as the recursion unwinds.
  4. The new grammar is ambiguous on a + b + c.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Eliminating left recursion preserves the language but changes the shape of the parse trees. In the left-recursive grammar the tree for a + b + c groups from the left; in the rewritten grammar E′ recurses on the right, so the tree nests to the right.

Step by step.

  1. Both grammars derive exactly the strings T (+ T)*, so option (A) is false.
  2. In E ⇒ T E′ ⇒ T + T E′ ⇒ T + T + T E′ ⇒ T + T + T, each E′ owns everything to its right. The tree is right-nested.
  3. Left associativity is therefore recovered by the semantic rule, not by the tree: E′.val is computed from the inherited left operand and the value of the T it just read, so (a + b) + c is still what is evaluated.
  4. Option (B) is false because the actions cannot be left untouched; option (D) is false because the rewritten grammar is LL(1) and hence unambiguous.

Final answer. Option (C).

Common misconception. Reading right-nested parse trees as right-associative meaning. Associativity is a property of the semantics you attach, and the rewritten grammar needs an inherited attribute to keep the left-associative one.

CD-U2-Q75MCQhard

Which one of the following statements about left recursion is TRUE?

  1. Left recursion is a property of grammars, not of languages: any context-free language that has a left-recursive grammar also has a grammar with no left recursion.
  2. Left recursion is a property of languages, so a left-recursive language has no left-recursion-free grammar.
  3. Only immediate left recursion can be removed; indirect left recursion can never be removed.
  4. A grammar with indirect left recursion always generates an ambiguous language.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Left recursion is a syntactic property of a particular grammar. The elimination procedure — order the nonterminals, substitute, then remove the immediate recursion — works for any grammar and returns a grammar for the same language.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is the correct reading: the rewrite is language preserving, so the language of a left-recursive grammar always has a left-recursion-free grammar too.
  2. (B) confuses the two levels. Languages are not left recursive; grammars are.
  3. (C) is false: indirect recursion is removed by substituting A's productions into B for each pair A before B in the chosen order, which turns it into immediate recursion that is then removed.
  4. (D) is false: S → A a | b, A → S c | d has indirect left recursion and exactly one parse tree per string it derives.

Final answer. Option (A).

Common misconception. Supposing that some languages are inherently left recursive. Any context-free grammar can be rewritten without left recursion, so no language is.

Review the 50 solved questions from Unit 3 — Syntax Analysis: Bottom-Up Parsing
LR(0) items & parsing · 14
CD-U3-Q01MCQeasy

In the LR(0) item A → X · Y Z, what does the position of the dot record?

  1. Nothing has been read yet; the parser still expects the whole right side X Y Z.
  2. A string derived from X has already been recognised, and a string derived from Y Z is still expected.
  3. All of X Y Z has been recognised, so the production is ready to be reduced.
  4. The dot separates the lookahead symbol from the handle and has no relation to the right side.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. An LR(0) item is one production with a distinguished position in its right side. The dot is that position: everything to its left has been matched against the input already, everything to its right is still expected.

Step by step.

  1. A → · X Y Z — the dot is at the left end, so nothing of the right side has been read.
  2. A → X · Y Z — X has been read; the parser now expects a string derived from Y Z.
  3. A → X Y · Z — two symbols read; one remains.
  4. A → X Y Z · — the dot is at the right end, which makes this a complete (reduce) item.

Final answer. Option (B) — the dot records how much of the right side has been recognised, so X is done and Y Z is still to come.

Common misconception. Reading the dot as a marker of the current input position or of the handle in the input string. The dot is always a position inside one production; the state the parser is in is what says where it stands in the input.

CD-U3-Q02MCQeasy

Which one of the following is NOT an LR(0) item of the production A → X Y Z?

  1. A → · X Y Z
  2. A → X · Y Z
  3. A → X Y Z ·
  4. A → X · Z Y
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A production with n symbols on its right side yields exactly n + 1 LR(0) items, one for each legal dot position. The order of the symbols on the right side is never changed: an item is not a permutation of a production.

Step by step.

  1. n = 3 symbols, so there are 4 items.
  2. They are A → · X Y Z, A → X · Y Z, A → X Y · Z and A → X Y Z ·.
  3. The fourth option reads X · Z Y: Z and Y have been exchanged, which is a different string, not a different dot position.

Final answer. Option (D) — A → X · Z Y is not an item of A → X Y Z.

Common misconception. Treating an item as "the production plus a marker anywhere", which makes any rearrangement look legal. The dot moves through the right side in order; the symbol sequence is fixed.

CD-U3-Q03NATeasy

For the grammar S → A A, A → a A | b, how many LR(0) items does the augmented grammar contain in total? Count every item of every production, including the added production S′ → S. (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A production with n symbols on its right side contributes n + 1 LR(0) items. The augmented grammar adds one production, S′ → S, which contributes its own items.

Step by step.

  1. S′ → S: one symbol on the right, so 2 items.
  2. S → A A: two symbols, so 3 items.
  3. A → a A: two symbols, so 3 items.
  4. A → b: one symbol, so 2 items.
  5. Total 2 + 3 + 3 + 2 = 10.

Final answer. 10 items.

Common misconception. Counting the four productions instead of the items, or forgetting the augmented production S′ → S. The start symbol is always primed and its production always counted.

CD-U3-Q08MCQmedium

For the grammar S → A A, A → a A | b, which set is the closure of the single item {S → A · A}?

  1. {S → A · A, A → · a A, A → · b}
  2. {S → A · A, A → a · A, A → b ·}
  3. {S → A · A}
  4. {S → A · A, A → · a A, A → · b, S → · A A}
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The closure of an item set adds B → · γ for every production of B whenever some item in the set has the dot immediately before B. Nothing else is added.

Step by step.

  1. The item S → A · A has the dot before the nonterminal A.
  2. A has the productions A → a A and A → b, so the items A → · a A and A → · b are added.
  3. Neither new item has a dot before a nonterminal, so the closure stops.
  4. S → · A A is not added: the dot before A adds the productions of A, never productions of S.

Final answer. Option (A).

Common misconception. Adding the productions of the item’s own left side (option D), or moving the dot forward while closing (option B). Closure only looks at the symbol after the dot, and only adds items with the dot at the left end.

CD-U3-Q09NATmedium

For the grammar S → A A, A → a A | b, how many states does the canonical collection of LR(0) items contain? Count the initial state. (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The states are the distinct closed item sets reached from the closure of S′ → · S by repeated GOTO on every grammar symbol.

Step by step.

  1. I0 = {S′ → · S, S → · A A, A → · a A, A → · b}
  2. GOTO(I0, S) = I1 = {S′ → S ·}; GOTO(I0, A) = I2 = {S → A · A, A → · a A, A → · b}
  3. GOTO(I0, a) = I3 = {A → a · A, A → · a A, A → · b}; GOTO(I0, b) = I4 = {A → b ·}
  4. GOTO(I2, A) = I5 = {S → A A ·}; GOTO(I3, A) = I6 = {A → a A ·}
  5. I2 and I3 reuse the states I3, I4 on a and b, so no further state appears. The collection is I0 … I6, seven states.

Final answer. 7 states.

Common misconception. Expecting one state per item or per production. States are shared: the same core {A → · a A, A → · b} appears inside I0, I2 and I3, and its GOTO on a always lands in the same state.

CD-U3-Q16NAThard

For the grammar S → A A, A → a A | b, how many states of the canonical LR(0) collection contain at least one completed item, that is, an item whose dot stands at the right end? (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A completed item is one of the form A → ω ·. Such a state is where a reduction can happen, so the number of them measures how many places in the automaton a reduce action lives.

Step by step.

  1. I1 = {S′ → S ·} — complete (accepting).
  2. I4 = {A → b ·} — complete.
  3. I5 = {S → A A ·} — complete.
  4. I6 = {A → a A ·} — complete.
  5. I0, I2, I3 hold only items with the dot before a symbol, so they are not counted.

Final answer. 4 states.

Common misconception. Counting the accepting state separately from reduce states, or forgetting that I6 is complete. I1 holds a completed item too; it just happens to be the augmented production, whose "reduction" is acceptance.

CD-U3-Q22MSQeasy

Which of the following statements about LR(0) items and the canonical collection are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. If A → α · B β is in a set and B → γ is a production, then B → · γ belongs to the closure of that set.
  2. GOTO(I, X) is the closure of the set of items A → α X · β taken over all A → α · X β in I.
  3. Every state of the canonical collection is the closure of a set of kernel items.
  4. The number of states of the collection equals the number of productions of the augmented grammar.
  5. An item whose dot stands at the left end of a production is called a reduce item.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The collection is built by two operations: CLOSURE, which completes a set of items with the productions of the nonterminal after the dot, and GOTO, which advances the dot over one grammar symbol and then closes.

Step by step.

  1. (A) true: that is the definition of the closure operation.
  2. (B) true: GOTO first moves the dot over X in every item that allows it, then takes the closure.
  3. (C) true: the initial state is the closure of {S′ → · S}, and every other state is the closure of the items produced by a GOTO, which are exactly its kernel.
  4. (D) false: the count depends on the grammar. S → A A, A → a A | b has four productions and seven states.
  5. (E) false: an item with the dot at the left end is an initial item. A reduce item has the dot at the right end.

Final answer. Options (A), (B) and (C).

Common misconception. Expecting a one-to-one correspondence between productions and states. States are sets of items, many states share items, and the same core can be reached by different paths.

CD-U3-S2-Q01MCQeasy

In a state of the canonical LR(0) collection the items are split into kernel items and items added by CLOSURE. Which one of the following is a non-kernel (closure) item by definition?

  1. S′ → · S, the initial item of the collection.
  2. Any item whose dot stands at the left end, other than S′ → · S.
  3. Any item whose dot does not stand at the left end.
  4. Any item whose dot stands at the right end.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The kernel of a state is S′ → · S together with every item whose dot is not at the left end; everything else in the state was added by CLOSURE because the dot stands before a nonterminal.

Step by step.

  1. S′ → · S is the kernel of the initial state, by definition.
  2. An item such as A → X · Y Z was produced by advancing the dot, so it is a kernel item.
  3. An item such as A → · X Y Z enters a state only through the CLOSURE rule, so it is a non-kernel item — unless it is S′ → · S.
  4. An item with the dot at the right end, such as A → X Y Z ·, is a complete (reduce) item, but it is still a kernel item because its dot did not start at the left end.

Answer: Option (B)

Common misconception. Treating complete items as closure items because they are the ones that trigger a reduction. Kernel versus closure is decided by how the dot got there, not by whether the item can be reduced.

CD-U3-S2-Q02MCQeasy

Before the canonical LR(0) collection is built, the grammar is augmented with a new production S′ → S. Why is this augmentation done?

  1. It removes every ε-production from the grammar.
  2. It makes the start symbol appear on the right side of exactly one production, so that a single item S′ → S · marks acceptance and the parser stops in exactly one state.
  3. It makes the grammar unambiguous.
  4. It guarantees that the grammar is LR(0).
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The parser must know when to accept. Acceptance is a property of one distinguished item, and the augmented production supplies it without disturbing the rest of the grammar.

Step by step.

  1. Without augmentation the start symbol may occur on the right side of other productions, so several states could contain a complete item with the start symbol on the left.
  2. Adding S′ → S guarantees that the start symbol occurs on the right side of exactly one production, so exactly one state contains S′ → S ·.
  3. That item alone is turned into the accept action; no other state can ever accept.
  4. Augmentation changes neither ambiguity nor the class of the grammar, so options (C) and (D) are wrong.

Answer: Option (B)

Common misconception. Believing that augmentation simplifies or repairs the grammar. It adds one production and one item; it repairs nothing except the uniqueness of the accept action.

CD-U3-S2-Q03NATeasy

For the expression grammar E → E + T | T, T → T * F | F, F → ( E ) | id, how many LR(0) items does the augmented grammar contain in total? Count every item of every production, including the items of the added production E′ → E. (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A production whose right side has n symbols has exactly n + 1 LR(0) items, one for each position of the dot.

Step by step.

  1. E′ → E has 1 symbol, so 2 items.
  2. E → E + T has 3 symbols, so 4 items; E → T has 1 symbol, so 2 items.
  3. T → T * F gives 4 items; T → F gives 2 items.
  4. F → ( E ) gives 4 items; F → id gives 2 items.
  5. Total: 2 + 4 + 2 + 4 + 2 + 4 + 2 = 20.

Answer: 20

Common misconception. Counting one item per production (7), which forgets the dot positions, or forgetting the augmented production and answering 18.

CD-U3-S2-Q04MCQmedium

For the grammar S → C C, C → c C | d, the initial state is I₀ = CLOSURE({S′ → · S}). Which set of items is GOTO(I₀, C)?

  1. {S → C · C, C → · c C, C → · d}
  2. {S → C · C}
  3. {S′ → S ·}
  4. {C → c · C, C → · c C, C → · d}
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. GOTO(I, X) advances the dot over X in every item of I that has the dot before X, and then closes the resulting set.

Step by step.

  1. The only item of I₀ with the dot before C is S → · C C; advancing the dot gives the kernel S → C · C.
  2. The dot now precedes C, so CLOSURE adds the productions of C with the dot at the left end: C → · c C and C → · d.
  3. Option (B) stops before CLOSURE; option (C) is GOTO(I₀, S); option (D) is GOTO(I₀, c).

Answer: Option (A)

Common misconception. Forgetting the CLOSURE step, which gives the bare kernel, or shifting the wrong symbol and returning the state reached on c.

CD-U3-S2-Q05NATmedium

For the grammar S → C C, C → c C | d, how many states does the canonical collection of LR(0) items contain? Count the initial state. (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The states are the distinct closed item sets reachable from the initial state by repeated GOTO.

Step by step.

  1. I₀ = {S′ → · S, S → · C C, C → · c C, C → · d}.
  2. On S: {S′ → S ·}.
  3. On C: {S → C · C, C → · c C, C → · d}.
  4. On c from I₀ and from that state: {C → c · C, C → · c C, C → · d}, a single shared state.
  5. On d: {C → d ·}; on C from {C → c · C, …}: {C → c C ·}; on C from {S → C · C, …}: {S → C C ·}.
  6. Counting the initial state: 7 states.

Answer: 7

Common misconception. Forgetting that the two occurrences of c lead to the same state, which gives 8, or dropping the accept state and answering 6.

CD-U3-S2-Q06MSQeasy

Which of the following statements about viable prefixes and the LR parser’s stack are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. The stack of a shift-reduce LR parser always holds a viable prefix of the grammar.
  2. Every prefix of a right-sentential form is a viable prefix.
  3. A viable prefix is a prefix of a right-sentential form that does not continue past the right end of the rightmost handle of that sentential form.
  4. The set of viable prefixes of a grammar is a regular language, and the canonical LR(0) automaton is a DFA that recognises it.
  5. The number of states of the canonical LR(0) collection equals the number of distinct viable prefixes.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The correctness of LR parsing rests on one invariant: the symbols on the stack, read as a string, always form a viable prefix, and every viable prefix labels a path from the initial state.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is the invariant of the parser: shift appends one symbol, reduce replaces a handle by its left side, and both moves preserve the viable-prefix property.
  2. (B) is false. A prefix that runs past the right end of the handle is not a viable prefix, which is why the parser must not read past the handle before reducing.
  3. (C) is the standard definition of a viable prefix.
  4. (D) is true: the item sets form a DFA whose states are the equivalence classes of viable prefixes.
  5. (E) is false: there are infinitely many viable prefixes but finitely many states, so several prefixes lead to the same state.

Answer: Options (A), (C) and (D)

Common misconception. Assuming every prefix of a right-sentential form is viable, which would let the parser shift forever without reducing.

CD-U3-S2-Q07MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about CLOSURE, GOTO and the states of the canonical LR(0) collection are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. CLOSURE(I) is the smallest set containing I and closed under the rule: if A → α · B β is in the set and B → γ is a production, then B → · γ is in the set.
  2. An item whose dot stands at the left end is a kernel item only when its left side is the start symbol.
  3. GOTO(I, X) is the CLOSURE of the set of items A → α X · β taken over all A → α · X β in I; when no item of I has the dot before X, GOTO(I, X) is empty.
  4. The initial state of the collection is CLOSURE({S′ → · S}).
  5. Every grammar has exactly one state in its canonical LR(0) collection that contains a completed item.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The collection is built from two operations only: CLOSURE, which adds the productions of a nonterminal that stands after the dot, and GOTO, which advances the dot and closes again.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is the CLOSURE rule, stated exactly as it is used.
  2. (B) is false: a left-end item is a non-kernel item whether or not its left side is the start symbol; S′ → · S is simply the one left-end item that is also a kernel item.
  3. (C) is the definition of GOTO, including the empty-set case.
  4. (D) is the definition of the initial state.
  5. (E) is false: a grammar with several productions that can complete will have several such states — for S → C C, C → c C | d there are four.

Answer: Options (A), (C) and (D)

Common misconception. Reading CLOSURE as "add every production of every nonterminal in the set". CLOSURE looks only at the symbol immediately after the dot.

SLR(1) parsing & conflicts · 12
CD-U3-Q04MCQeasy

An SLR(1) parser is about to reduce by A → ω in a state I. In the ACTION table the reduction is entered in column a only when which condition holds?

  1. a ∈ FIRST(A).
  2. a is the terminal that stands immediately to the right of the handle in the input seen so far.
  3. a ∈ FOLLOW(A).
  4. a is the terminal that was shifted last, whatever it happens to be.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. SLR(1) approximates the lookahead of a reduction by the FOLLOW set of the left side. Every reduction by A → ω is entered in all columns a ∈ FOLLOW(A), and nowhere else.

Step by step.

  1. When the parser has A → ω · on top, A is about to appear in the sentential form, so the next input symbol can only be one that may follow A.
  2. SLR does not look at the individual state; it uses the grammar-wide set FOLLOW(A).
  3. FIRST(A) governs shift decisions and the table entries of productions that begin with A, not the reduction of A → ω.

Final answer. Option (C) — the reduction is entered on the terminals of FOLLOW(A).

Common misconception. Believing SLR(1) inspects the real context of the state. That is exactly what LR(1) does with per-item lookaheads; SLR substitutes the coarser FOLLOW set, which is why an SLR table can conflict where an LR(1) table does not.

CD-U3-Q10MCQmedium

The grammar S → a S b | a is not LR(0). Why is it nevertheless SLR(1)?

  1. Because the grammar is unambiguous, and every unambiguous grammar is SLR(1).
  2. Because the conflict is a reduce/reduce conflict, which SLR(1) removes by production order.
  3. Because FOLLOW(S) is empty, so the reduction never competes with a shift.
  4. Because from the state that holds S → a · the only shift is on a, while FOLLOW(S) = {b, $} contains no a.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. LR(0) requires a state that holds a complete item to reduce in every column, so any state that also has a shift item is in conflict. SLR(1) narrows the reduce action to the columns of FOLLOW.

Step by step.

  1. After reading a from the initial state the parser is in {S → a · S b, S → · a S b, S → · a, S → a ·}.
  2. The complete item S → a · coexists with a shift on a, so the LR(0) table has a shift/reduce conflict there.
  3. FOLLOW(S) = {b, $}: b from S → a S b and $ because S is the start symbol.
  4. a ∉ FOLLOW(S), so the reduce action and the shift action occupy disjoint columns and the SLR(1) table has no conflict.

Final answer. Option (D).

Common misconception. Assuming the hierarchy is a chain in which failing LR(0) means failing SLR(1) too. The classes are nested the other way: every LR(0) grammar is SLR(1), and this grammar shows the inclusion is strict.

CD-U3-Q14MCQhard

In the dangling-else grammar S → i C t S | i C t S e S | a, C → b, the SLR(1) table has exactly one conflict. Which one is it, and how is it conventionally resolved?

  1. A shift/reduce conflict between shifting e and reducing S → i C t S; resolved by shifting, so the e binds to the nearest i.
  2. A reduce/reduce conflict between the two S-productions; resolved by taking the longer production.
  3. A shift/reduce conflict resolved by reducing, so the e binds to the outermost i.
  4. There is no conflict: the grammar is unambiguous, so SLR(1) accepts it as written.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The grammar is ambiguous: the sentence i b t i b t a e a has two parse trees, one attaching the e to each i. SLR(1) exposes that ambiguity as a single shift/reduce conflict.

Step by step.

  1. After i C t S the parser is in a state holding the complete item S → i C t S · and the item S → i C t S · e S.
  2. e ∈ FOLLOW(S), so the reduce action sits in column e — the same column that asks for a shift of e.
  3. Shifting is chosen, which postpones the reduction and attaches the e to the innermost i.
  4. The two actions are exactly the two parse trees of the ambiguous sentence, which is why the conflict is unavoidable without changing the grammar or the rule.

Final answer. Option (A).

Common misconception. Expecting a reduce/reduce conflict, or thinking the resolution is part of the grammar. The conflict is shift/reduce and the "shift wins" rule is a parser-level convention that encodes the usual nearest-i semantics.

CD-U3-Q17MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about conflicts in an SLR(1) table are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. A shift/reduce conflict arises in a state that holds a completed item and an item with the dot before a terminal, when that terminal belongs to the FOLLOW set of the completed item’s left side.
  2. A reduce/reduce conflict arises when two completed items in one state have left sides whose FOLLOW sets share a terminal.
  3. Every SLR(1) grammar is LR(0).
  4. Every LR(1) grammar is SLR(1).
  5. An SLR(1) table can never contain a reduce/reduce conflict.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The SLR(1) table is filled by shifting on every terminal transition and reducing by A → ω in the columns of FOLLOW(A). A conflict is simply a cell that receives two actions.

Step by step.

  1. (A) true: the shift action occupies column a and the reduce action also occupies it, which is the definition of the conflict.
  2. (B) true: both complete items write into every terminal of their own FOLLOW set, so a shared terminal receives two different reductions.
  3. (C) false: S → a S b | a is SLR(1) but not LR(0), so the inclusion is strict.
  4. (D) false: S → L = R | R, L → * R | id, R → L is LALR(1) but has a shift/reduce conflict under SLR(1).
  5. (E) false: S → A a | B a, A → c, B → c produces a reduce/reduce conflict in column a.

Final answer. Options (A) and (B).

Common misconception. Treating "SLR(1) conflict" as synonymous with shift/reduce. Reduce/reduce conflicts are equally possible, and they indicate two distinct handles that the grammar does not separate.

CD-U3-Q18MCQmedium

For the grammar S → A a | B a, A → c, B → c, which conflict does the SLR(1) construction produce?

  1. A shift/reduce conflict in column a.
  2. A reduce/reduce conflict in column a.
  3. A reduce/reduce conflict in column $.
  4. No conflict — the grammar is SLR(1).
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Both productions A → c and B → c are complete after the same terminal c, and FOLLOW(A) = FOLLOW(B) = {a}, so one cell receives two reductions.

Step by step.

  1. FOLLOW(A) comes from S → A a, giving {a}; FOLLOW(B) comes from S → B a, also {a}.
  2. The state reached after shifting c holds A → c · and B → c ·.
  3. Both write a reduce action into column a, so the cell ACTION[that state, a] holds two entries.
  4. No shift action competes there, so the conflict is reduce/reduce, not shift/reduce.

Final answer. Option (B) — reduce/reduce in column a.

Common misconception. Expecting a shift/reduce conflict because the grammar "looks" like an operator grammar. Shift/reduce needs a shift item in the same state; here the state holds only the two complete items.

CD-U3-Q21MCQeasy

In a shift-reduce parser, the handle of a right-sentential form is which of the following?

  1. The leftmost terminal of the form.
  2. The longest prefix of the form that is also a prefix of some right-sentential form.
  3. The next input symbol that will be shifted.
  4. The substring that matches the right side of some production and whose reduction is one step of a rightmost derivation in reverse.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A bottom-up parse is a rightmost derivation written backwards. Each reduction undoes one step of that derivation, so the handle is the substring that the next reduction must replace.

Step by step.

  1. A right-sentential form is what a rightmost derivation produces at some step.
  2. Reversing one step means replacing a right side by its left side — that right side is the handle.
  3. Option (B) describes a viable prefix, the other central notion of LR parsing; it is a stack prefix, not a handle.
  4. Options (A) and (C) describe the input pointer, not a reducible substring.

Final answer. Option (D).

Common misconception. Confusing the handle with the viable prefix. A viable prefix is any prefix of a right-sentential form that does not extend past the end of the handle; the handle itself is the part about to be reduced.

CD-U3-Q25MSQeasy

Which of the following statements about the moves of a shift-reduce parser are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Shift pushes the next input symbol onto the stack and advances the input pointer.
  2. Reduce pops the handle from the top of the stack and pushes the left side of the production that matched it.
  3. Accept is announced as soon as the stack holds the start symbol, whether or not input remains.
  4. An error is detected as soon as the table has no action for the current state and the current lookahead.
  5. Every reduce pops exactly one symbol from the stack.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A shift-reduce parser performs four kinds of move: shift, reduce, accept and error. Accept needs the stack to hold exactly the start symbol and the input to be exhausted.

Step by step.

  1. (A) true: one input symbol moves onto the stack, and the input pointer advances by one.
  2. (B) true: | ω | symbols leave the stack and the left side A takes their place, so the stack can also shrink by more than one symbol.
  3. (C) false: with input still unread, holding the start symbol is not acceptance; the parser must also see $.
  4. (D) true: an empty ACTION entry is the parser’s only way to notice that no legal move exists, and it reports the error immediately.
  5. (E) false: a reduce by A → X Y Z pops three symbols.

Final answer. Options (A), (B) and (D).

Common misconception. Thinking reduce always replaces one symbol by one symbol. It replaces a whole right side, of any length, by a single nonterminal.

CD-U3-S2-Q08MCQeasy

In the parsing table of an SLR(1) parser, which transitions are recorded in the GOTO part rather than in ACTION?

  1. Shifts on terminals.
  2. Transitions on nonterminals.
  3. Reductions by completed items.
  4. The accept action.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The table has two halves: ACTION decides what the parser does when it sees a terminal, GOTO tells it which state to enter after a reduction has pushed a nonterminal.

Step by step.

  1. ACTION is indexed by a state and a terminal, and its entries are shift, reduce, accept or error.
  2. GOTO is indexed by a state and a nonterminal, and its entry is the state to move to.
  3. Shifts, reductions and accept therefore all live in ACTION; only nonterminal transitions live in GOTO.

Answer: Option (B)

Common misconception. Putting nonterminal transitions in ACTION, which breaks the parser after every reduction.

CD-U3-S2-Q09MCQeasy

An LR(0) parser and an SLR(1) parser are built from the same canonical collection. Which statement describes the difference between their ACTION tables?

  1. The LR(0) table enters the reduction by A → ω in every column, whereas SLR(1) enters it only in the columns of the terminals in FOLLOW(A).
  2. The LR(0) table uses two symbols of lookahead, while SLR(1) uses one.
  3. The LR(0) table contains no reduce actions at all.
  4. The SLR(1) table reduces in every column, while the LR(0) table restricts reductions to FOLLOW sets.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. LR(0) has no lookahead, so a completed item forbids every shift in its state. SLR(1) attaches one token of lookahead and reduces only where that token can follow the left side.

Step by step.

  1. In the LR(0) table, a state holding A → ω · reduces on any terminal whatsoever; the table is filled in every column.
  2. That is why LR(0) fails on almost every practical grammar: the reduction collides with any shift in the same state.
  3. SLR(1) narrows the reduction to the columns of FOLLOW(A), which removes most of those collisions.
  4. Options (B), (C) and (D) reverse the roles or invent lookahead that LR(0) does not have.

Answer: Option (A)

Common misconception. Thinking that SLR(1) differs from LR(0) in the automaton it builds. The states are identical; only the placement of the reduce entries changes.

CD-U3-S2-Q10MCQmedium

For the grammar S → L = R | R, L → * R | id, R → L, the SLR(1) table contains exactly one conflict. Which one is it?

  1. A shift/reduce conflict on =, in the state that holds S → L · = R and R → L ·.
  2. A reduce/reduce conflict on =, between R → L and S → R.
  3. A shift/reduce conflict on *, in the initial state.
  4. None — the grammar is SLR(1).
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A shift/reduce conflict appears when one state holds both a completed item and an item with the dot before a terminal, and that terminal lies in the FOLLOW set of the completed item’s left side.

Step by step.

  1. FOLLOW(R) = {$, =} and FOLLOW(L) = {$, =}.
  2. The state reached from the initial state on L holds S → L · = R and R → L ·.
  3. On = the first item asks for a shift and the second, because = ∈ FOLLOW(R), asks for a reduction by R → L.
  4. That is one shift/reduce conflict on =; no other cell conflicts, so the grammar is not SLR(1).

Answer: Option (A)

Common misconception. Assuming that any conflict must be reduce/reduce because two items share a state. Here the two items are one shift item and one reduce item, so the conflict is shift/reduce.

CD-U3-S2-Q11MCQmedium

Which one of the following chains of strict inclusions between grammar classes is correct?

  1. LR(0) ⊂ SLR(1) ⊂ LALR(1) ⊂ LR(1)
  2. LR(0) ⊂ LR(1) ⊂ SLR(1) ⊂ LALR(1)
  3. SLR(1) ⊂ LR(0) ⊂ LALR(1) ⊂ LR(1)
  4. LALR(1) ⊂ SLR(1) ⊂ LR(1) ⊂ LR(0)
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Each class is obtained from the previous one by adding information, never by removing it, so the accepted class of grammars grows strictly at every step.

Step by step.

  1. LR(0) uses no lookahead, so it is the weakest of the four.
  2. SLR(1) adds FOLLOW sets, which strictly enlarges the class.
  3. LALR(1) adds exact lookaheads but keeps the LR(0) cores, which again strictly enlarges the class.
  4. LR(1) keeps full canonical lookaheads and is the largest of the four; a grammar that is LR(1) but not LALR(1) exists, so the last inclusion is strict too.

Answer: Option (A)

Common misconception. Believing that LALR(1) is stronger than LR(1) because its tables are smaller. Size and power go the other way: LR(1) accepts more grammars.

CD-U3-S2-Q12NAThard

For the grammar S → A a | b A c | d c | b d a, A → d, how many cells of the SLR(1) ACTION table contain a conflict? Count each (state, terminal) cell once. (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Count conflicts cell by cell: a conflict exists exactly when one cell receives two different actions.

Step by step.

  1. FOLLOW(A) = {a, c}, because A is followed by a in S → A a and by c in S → b A c.
  2. The state reached on d from the initial state holds A → d · and S → d · c. On c it must shift (for S → d · c) and reduce A → d, since c ∈ FOLLOW(A). That is one conflicting cell.
  3. The state reached on b d holds A → d · and S → b d · a. On a it must shift and reduce, again because a ∈ FOLLOW(A). That is the second conflicting cell.
  4. Both conflicts are shift/reduce; no other cell receives two actions, so the total is 2.

Answer: 2

Common misconception. Finding only the state reached on d and answering 1. The grammar has two such states, one after d and one after b d.

LR(1) and LALR(1) parsers · 13
CD-U3-Q05MCQeasy

In the LR(1) item [A → α · β, a], what does the second component a record?

  1. A terminal that may immediately follow A in some sentential form, so the reduction by this production is taken only on that lookahead.
  2. The terminal that must be shifted next, before any reduction of A can happen.
  3. The symbol that stands immediately to the left of the dot.
  4. The precedence level attached to the production A → αβ.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. An LR(1) item is a pair: an LR(0) item and one lookahead terminal. The lookahead says which terminal may follow the completed left side, and therefore in which columns the reduction belongs.

Step by step.

  1. The dot part A → α · β is exactly an LR(0) item and records progress through the right side.
  2. The second component a is the extra information LR(1) adds: a terminal that can come after A.
  3. When the dot reaches the end, [A → αβ ·, a] licenses the reduction by A → αβ only in column a.

Final answer. Option (A) — a is the lookahead that may follow A.

Common misconception. Confusing the lookahead with the next symbol to be shifted. The lookahead is a condition on a reduction, and the same state can carry different lookaheads for the same core.

CD-U3-Q11MCQmedium

LALR(1) is built by merging every pair of canonical LR(1) states that have the same core. Which conflicts can such a merge create that were not present in the canonical automaton?

  1. Shift/reduce conflicts only.
  2. Reduce/reduce conflicts only.
  3. Both shift/reduce and reduce/reduce conflicts.
  4. None — merging states with equal cores never creates a conflict.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Two LR(1) states with the same core have the same LR(0) items, hence the same shift actions and the same GOTO transitions; they differ only in the lookahead terminals attached to complete items.

Step by step.

  1. Shift actions depend only on the core, so a shift present in one state is present in the other, and after merging no new shift competes with anything.
  2. Reduce actions depend on the lookaheads. Merging unions the lookahead sets.
  3. If two complete items with different left sides are in the merged state, a terminal that is a lookahead of both now asks for two different reductions: a reduce/reduce conflict appears.
  4. Since no new shift arises and no shift loses a column, no shift/reduce conflict can be created.

Final answer. Option (B) — only reduce/reduce conflicts can be introduced.

Common misconception. Believing LALR is simply "LR(1) with fewer states" and therefore equally powerful on every grammar. The grammar S → a A d | b B d | a B e | b A e, A → c, B → c is LR(1) but the merge produces exactly such a reduce/reduce conflict.

CD-U3-Q12NATmedium

For the grammar S → L = R | R, L → * R | id, R → L, how many states does the LALR(1) automaton have? Count the initial state; states are obtained by merging canonical LR(1) states that share a core. (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The LALR(1) automaton has the states of the LR(0) collection with LR(1) lookaheads attached, so its state count equals the LR(0) state count and is at most the canonical LR(1) count.

Step by step.

  1. Counting the LR(0) states of this grammar: the initial state, the state after S, after L, after R, after *, after id, after =, after * R, after L =, and the state after L = R. That is 10 states.
  2. The canonical LR(1) collection keeps two copies of the L and R states, one reached through S → · L = R and one through S → · R, giving 14 states.
  3. Merging each pair with a common core reduces 14 to 10 states; the merged automaton has no conflict, which is why this grammar is LALR(1) although it is not SLR(1).

Final answer. 10 states.

Common misconception. Answering 14 by counting canonical LR(1) states. LALR merges states with identical cores, so the count must equal the LR(0) count, not the LR(1) count.

CD-U3-Q15MCQhard

Which one of the following grammars is LR(1) but NOT LALR(1)?

  1. S → A A, A → a A | b
  2. S → a S b | a
  3. S → a A d | b B d | a B e | b A e, A → c, B → c
  4. S → L = R | R, L → * R | id, R → L
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. LALR(1) merges canonical LR(1) states that share a core, unioning their lookaheads. A grammar that is LR(1) fails LALR exactly when that union puts two different reductions in one cell.

Step by step.

  1. Option (A): S → A A, A → a A | b is LR(0), hence also SLR(1) and LALR(1).
  2. Option (B): S → a S b | a is not LR(0) but is SLR(1), so again LALR(1).
  3. Option (D): S → L = R | R is the standard grammar that is LALR(1) but not SLR(1).
  4. Option (C): the canonical LR(1) collection has 14 states and no conflict. The states reached through a and through b carry A → c · with lookahead d and B → c · with lookahead e, and the mirror images carry the same two items with the lookaheads exchanged.
  5. Merging each pair leaves 13 states in which both complete items carry {d, e}, so column d (and e) asks for two different reductions: a reduce/reduce conflict.

Final answer. Option (C).

Common misconception. Assuming LALR(1) accepts every LR(1) grammar. It accepts every LR(1) language, but individual grammars can be lost in the merge.

CD-U3-Q19MSQhard

Which of the following statements about LR(1) and LALR(1) are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. For the same grammar the canonical LR(1) collection can contain more states than the LALR(1) collection.
  2. LALR(1) grammars generate exactly the same class of languages as LR(1) grammars.
  3. Merging states with the same core can change the language the parser accepts.
  4. Every SLR(1) grammar is LALR(1).
  5. In the canonical LR(1) construction each item carries a set of lookahead terminals rather than one.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The four classes are nested: LR(0) ⊂ SLR(1) ⊂ LALR(1) ⊂ LR(1) as classes of grammars, while LALR(1) and LR(1) accept the same class of languages.

Step by step.

  1. (A) true: S → L = R | R, L → * R | id, R → L has 14 canonical LR(1) states and only 10 LALR(1) states.
  2. (B) true: for every LR(1) grammar there is an equivalent LALR(1) grammar, so the two classes of languages coincide.
  3. (C) false: a merge only changes when a reduction is allowed, never which strings are derivable; if the merge produces a conflict the grammar is simply not LALR(1), and its language is unchanged.
  4. (D) true: SLR(1) lookaheads are a special case of LALR(1) lookaheads, so no SLR(1) grammar is lost.
  5. (E) false: in the canonical construction each item is a pair with a single lookahead terminal. Sets of lookaheads appear only after states are merged.

Final answer. Options (A), (B) and (D).

Common misconception. Confusing "same languages" with "same grammars". LALR(1) accepts the same languages as LR(1) while rejecting some individual LR(1) grammars, exactly the ones whose cores conflict when merged.

CD-U3-Q23MCQmedium

For the expression grammar E → E + T | T, T → T * F | F, F → ( E ) | id, how many states does the LALR(1) automaton have?

  1. 12
  2. 10
  3. 22
  4. 24
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. This grammar has no pair of LR(1) states with a common core that carry different lookahead sets needing separate copies, so the LALR(1) automaton is the LR(0) automaton with lookaheads attached and has the same number of states.

Step by step.

  1. Start: I0 = {E′ → · E, E → · E + T, E → · T, T → · T * F, T → · F, F → · ( E ), F → · id}.
  2. The GOTO transitions follow the nesting E, T, F: states for E′ → E ·, E → E · + T, E → T ·/T → T · * F, T → F ·, F → id ·, F → ( · E ), E → E + · T, T → T * · F, F → ( E · ), E → E + T ·, T → T * F · and F → ( E ) ·.
  3. Counting them gives 12 states, which is also the LR(0) count and the LALR(1) count.
  4. The canonical LR(1) automaton, by contrast, keeps separate copies of several of these states and has 22.

Final answer. Option (A) — 12 states.

Common misconception. Quoting the canonical LR(1) count for an LALR question. Whenever no two states share a core with different lookaheads, LALR and LR(0) have the same state count.

CD-U3-S2-Q13MCQeasy

For the same grammar, how do the canonical LR(1) collection and the LALR(1) collection compare in size?

  1. LALR(1) never has more states than canonical LR(1); its states are obtained by merging LR(1) states that share the same kernel.
  2. LALR(1) always has strictly more states than canonical LR(1).
  3. The two collections always have exactly the same number of states.
  4. The LALR(1) collection has as many states as the grammar has productions.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. LALR(1) is defined as a quotient of the canonical LR(1) collection: states with equal kernels are merged, so the merged collection can only shrink.

Step by step.

  1. Merging partitions the LR(1) states, so the number of LALR(1) states is at most the number of LR(1) states.
  2. For the grammar S → C C, C → c C | d the canonical LR(1) collection has 10 states while the LALR(1) collection has 7, so the inequality is often strict.
  3. The LALR(1) states have exactly the LR(0) kernels, which is why LALR(1) and SLR(1) tables for the same grammar always have the same number of states.

Answer: Option (A)

Common misconception. Expecting LALR(1) to be smaller but stronger. It is smaller and slightly weaker: the merge can introduce reduce/reduce conflicts that canonical LR(1) does not have.

CD-U3-S2-Q14MCQeasy

In the closure of the LR(1) item [A → α · B β, a], which lookaheads are attached to the added items B → · γ?

  1. Every terminal in FIRST(β a).
  2. Only the single terminal a.
  3. Every terminal in FOLLOW(B).
  4. Only the end marker $.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The lookahead of a new item records what can follow B at this point of the parse: anything derived from β, or a itself when β can vanish.

Step by step.

  1. After B is reduced, the parser still has to match β and then the lookahead a.
  2. So the terminals that can legally follow B here are exactly those in FIRST(β a).
  3. When β derives ε, the whole of FIRST(β a) collapses to {a}, which is why option (B) is the special case and not the rule.
  4. FOLLOW(B) is the SLR(1) approximation: it unions the lookaheads over all contexts and is therefore weaker.

Answer: Option (A)

Common misconception. Using FOLLOW(B) inside the LR(1) construction. That turns the canonical LR(1) automaton into an SLR(1) one and loses exactly the precision the extra states are paying for.

CD-U3-S2-Q15NATmedium

For the grammar S → C C, C → c C | d, how many states does the canonical LR(1) collection contain? Count the initial state. (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The canonical LR(1) collection separates states that have the same kernel but different lookaheads, so it can be larger than the LR(0) collection of the same grammar.

Step by step.

  1. The LR(0) collection of this grammar has 7 states, and those 7 kernels are exactly the cores that appear in the LR(1) collection.
  2. Three of them split, because the same kernel carries different lookaheads in different contexts: {C → c · C, C → · c C, C → · d} splits into a copy with lookaheads {c, d} and a copy with lookahead {$}.
  3. {C → d ·} splits the same way, into {c, d} and {$}.
  4. {C → c C ·} splits likewise into {c, d} and {$}.
  5. Total: 7 + 3 = 10 states. Merging the three pairs gives back the 7-state LALR(1) automaton, which is why this grammar is LALR(1).

Answer: 10

Common misconception. Answering 7 by assuming LR(1) and LR(0) collections always have the same number of states, or 13 by splitting every state.

CD-U3-S2-Q16MCQmedium

Why is the canonical LR(1) collection usually much larger than the LALR(1) collection for the same grammar?

  1. Because each LR(1) item carries a specific lookahead, so two states with identical kernels but different lookaheads are kept apart.
  2. Because an LR(1) item carries a set of lookaheads while an LALR(1) item carries only one.
  3. Because the LR(1) construction adds an extra production to the grammar.
  4. Because an LR(1) parser uses two symbols of lookahead.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The identity of an LR(1) state is its set of items together with their lookaheads; the identity of an LALR(1) state is only its kernel.

Step by step.

  1. Two contexts can reach the same kernel of items while permitting different lookaheads.
  2. In the canonical construction those are two distinct states, so the collection grows.
  3. LALR(1) deliberately merges them, keeping one state per kernel and unioning the lookaheads.
  4. Option (B) reverses the two definitions — the LR(1) item holds a single lookahead, the merged LALR(1) item ends up with a set. Option (D) is wrong: LR(1) means one symbol of lookahead.

Answer: Option (A)

Common misconception. Thinking that more states mean more lookahead. Both parsers read one symbol ahead; the difference is whether lookahead information is allowed to split a state.

CD-U3-S2-Q17MSQhard

Which of the following statements about the construction of an LALR(1) table are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Merging two states that have the same kernel keeps every shift action, because shifts depend only on the dot positions and not on the lookaheads.
  2. In a merged state, a reduction is entered in exactly those columns given by the union of the lookaheads of the completed items that were merged.
  3. For any grammar, the LALR(1) collection has the same number of states as the SLR(1) collection of that grammar.
  4. An LALR(1) parser never performs a reduction earlier than the point at which a canonical LR(1) parser would have detected an error.
  5. Every grammar that is LALR(1) becomes SLR(1) once it has been left-factored.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. LALR(1) merges LR(1) states with equal kernels. Merging preserves the shift structure and only widens the lookaheads attached to reductions.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is true: an item A → α · X β asks for a shift on X whatever its lookahead is, and merging cannot change the dot positions.
  2. (B) is true and is the definition of the merged reduce entry.
  3. (C) is true: both collections have exactly the LR(0) kernels as their states, so their state counts are equal — 7 for S → C C, C → c C | d, for example.
  4. (D) is false: because a merged state may reduce on a lookahead that a canonical state would have used to detect an error, LALR(1) can delay error detection.
  5. (E) is false: left-factoring changes the grammar, but S → A a | b A c | d c | b d a, A → d is LALR(1) and not SLR(1) and needs no factoring at all — the conflict comes from an imprecise FOLLOW set, not from a common prefix.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C)

Common misconception. Assuming the merge is harmless. It never creates a shift/reduce conflict, but it can create a reduce/reduce conflict, and it is exactly that possibility that separates LALR(1) from LR(1).

CD-U3-S2-Q18MCQhard

Which one of the following grammars is LALR(1) but NOT SLR(1)?

  1. S → C C, C → c C | d
  2. S → a A d | b B d | a B e | b A e, A → c, B → c
  3. S → A a | b A c | d c | b d a, A → d
  4. S → a S | b S | a
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A grammar is LALR(1) but not SLR(1) when the FOLLOW sets are too coarse but the exact LALR lookaheads are not: the SLR table conflicts while the LALR table does not.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is SLR(1): its SLR table has no conflict at all.
  2. (B) is LR(1) but not LALR(1): merging the states with the same kernel unions the lookaheads of A → c · and B → c · and produces reduce/reduce conflicts on d and on e. It fails at the LALR level, not the SLR level.
  3. (C) has FOLLOW(A) = {a, c}, so the SLR table reduces A → d on both a and c and collides with a shift in two cells. In the LALR automaton the lookahead of A → d is {a} in one context and {c} in the other, so both conflicts disappear. It is LALR(1) and not SLR(1).
  4. (D) is SLR(1): its SLR table has no conflict.

Answer: Option (C)

Common misconception. Picking (B), which is the more famous example but fails one level too low: it is not LALR(1) at all.

CD-U3-S2-Q19MSQhard

For the grammar S → a A d | b B d | a B e | b A e, A → c, B → c, which of the following statements are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. It is LR(1).
  2. It is LALR(1).
  3. Merging the two states that share a kernel unions the lookaheads of A → c · and B → c ·, which creates reduce/reduce conflicts on d and on e.
  4. The canonical LR(1) collection has the same number of states as the LALR(1) collection.
  5. Every SLR(1) grammar is LR(1).
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. This is the standard witness that the inclusion LALR(1) ⊂ LR(1) is strict: two contexts reach the same kernel with disjoint lookaheads, and the merge destroys that distinction.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is true: the canonical LR(1) collection has 14 states and no conflict in any cell.
  2. (B) is false — see (C).
  3. (C) is true: after a c the item A → c · has lookahead d, and after b c it has lookahead e; the same holds for B → c · with the roles exchanged. Merging the two kernels unions the lookaheads, so both A → c and B → c become reducible on d and on e, giving two reduce/reduce conflicts.
  4. (D) is false: the LR(1) collection has 14 states, the LALR(1) collection 13.
  5. (E) is true: SLR(1) ⊂ LALR(1) ⊂ LR(1), so every SLR(1) grammar is LR(1).

Answer: Options (A), (C) and (E)

Common misconception. Concluding that the grammar is ambiguous because the LALR table conflicts. It is unambiguous and LR(1); only the merged table fails.

Operator-precedence parsing · 11
CD-U3-Q06MCQeasy

For the grammar E → E + E | E * E | ( E ) | id, which precedence relation holds between the terminals ( and )?

  1. ( <· )
  2. ( ≌ )
  3. ( ·> )
  4. No precedence relation holds between them.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The three relations are read off the productions: a ≌ b when a right side contains a b or a B b; a <· b when a is followed by a nonterminal whose FIRST set contains b; a ·> b when a is in the LAST set of a nonterminal that is followed by b.

Step by step.

  1. The production E → ( E ) has the shape terminal, nonterminal, terminal: (, E, ).
  2. That is precisely the pattern a B b, so ( ≌ ).
  3. The relation is symmetric in form only: ) never satisfies ) ≌ (, because the production is written in one direction.

Final answer. Option (B) — ( ≌ ).

Common misconception. Reading ≌ as "the two operators have equal precedence". It is a parsing relation meaning the parser is inside a bracketed construct and must keep shifting until the matching closing symbol appears.

CD-U3-Q07MSQeasy

Which of the following statements about operator-precedence parsing are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. An operator grammar has no ε-production and no production whose right side contains two adjacent nonterminals.
  2. The relations are computed from the productions alone; no LR item set, closure or state machine is built.
  3. Every context-free grammar becomes an operator grammar once its ε-productions are deleted.
  4. The parser reduces when the relation between the topmost terminal on the stack and the next input symbol is ·>.
  5. The parser must compute the FOLLOW set of every nonterminal before it can run.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Operator-precedence parsing is a shift-reduce method that decides every move from a table of three relations between pairs of terminals, built directly from the productions of an operator grammar.

Step by step.

  1. (A) true: those two restrictions are the definition of an operator grammar, and they guarantee that the handle of every right-sentential form is bordered by terminals.
  2. (B) true: FIRST and LAST of the nonterminals, plus a scan of each right side, are all that is needed.
  3. (C) false: deleting ε-productions does not remove a right side such as A B, and adjacent nonterminals are forbidden in an operator grammar.
  4. (D) true: shift while <· or ≌ holds, reduce while ·> holds, and report an error when no relation holds.
  5. (E) false: FOLLOW sets belong to SLR(1); the precedence relations replace them entirely.

Final answer. Options (A), (B) and (D).

Common misconception. Believing that any CFG can be handled after a quick rewrite. The class of operator grammars is strictly smaller, and grammars with ε-productions or adjacent nonterminals need a real transformation, not a deletion.

CD-U3-Q13MCQmedium

For the grammar E → E + E | E * E | ( E ) | id, which precedence relation holds between the terminals id and +?

  1. id <· +
  2. id ≌ +
  3. id ·> +
  4. No precedence relation holds between them.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. a ·> b holds when some production has a nonterminal A immediately followed by the terminal b, and a belongs to LAST(A). LAST(E) = {id, )} here, because E → E + E and E → ( E ) end in id and ).

Step by step.

  1. E → E + E has E immediately before +.
  2. Every terminal in LAST(E) therefore satisfies ·> +: id ·> + and ) ·> +.
  3. Symmetrically FIRST(E) = {id, (} and E → E + E gives + <· id and + <· (.
  4. The two relations go in opposite directions, so id <· + is false.

Final answer. Option (C) — id ·> +.

Common misconception. Reading the relations as operator precedence levels. They are local facts about where a handle can end: id ·> + says that a handle may end just before the +, which is what lets the parser reduce id to E.

CD-U3-Q20NAThard

For the grammar E → E + E | E * E | ( E ) | id, using the standard definition of the precedence relations, how many ordered pairs (a, b) of terminals satisfy a ·> b? Count each ordered pair once. (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. a ·> b holds when some production has a nonterminal A immediately followed by the terminal b and a ∈ LAST(A). Here LAST(E) = {id, )}: every right side that ends in a terminal ends in id or ).

Step by step.

  1. E → E + E has E followed by +, so every a ∈ LAST(E) gives a ·> +: id ·> + and ) ·> + — two pairs.
  2. E → E * E likewise gives id ·> * and ) ·> * — two more.
  3. E → ( E ) has E followed by ), giving id ·> ) and ) ·> ) — two more.
  4. E → E + E also has E followed by E, but the symbol after the nonterminal must be a terminal for the relation, so that occurrence adds nothing.
  5. Total 2 + 2 + 2 = 6.

Final answer. 6 ordered pairs: (id, +), (), +), (id, *), (), *), (id, )), (), )).

Common misconception. Assuming ·> only relates operator pairs, which would give the four pairs of {+, *, id} alone and miss the two pairs involving ). LAST(E) decides the left member, and it contains id and ), not the operators.

CD-U3-Q24MCQmedium

Which one of the following is a genuine limitation of operator-precedence parsing?

  1. It cannot parse grammars that contain left recursion.
  2. It cannot parse expressions that use more than one operator.
  3. It applies only to operator grammars, so ε-productions and adjacent nonterminals must be removed first.
  4. It needs two tokens of lookahead where LR(1) needs one.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The method needs every handle to be bordered by terminals, which is exactly what an operator grammar guarantees: no ε-production and no right side with two adjacent nonterminals.

Step by step.

  1. Left recursion is harmless: the classic expression grammar E → E + E | E * E is left recursive and is parsed by this method.
  2. Several operators are the normal case; the relations handle them pair by pair.
  3. One token of lookahead is enough: the decision uses only the topmost stack terminal and the next input symbol.
  4. The real restriction is grammatical: a production such as A → B C or A → ε leaves a handle that is not bordered by terminals, so the relation table cannot decide.

Final answer. Option (C).

Common misconception. Believing the method is limited by recursion or by the number of operators. The limitation is the operator-grammar restriction, which excludes whole families of grammars regardless of how simple their operators are.

CD-U3-S2-Q20MCQeasy

Which one of the following grammars is an operator grammar?

  1. S → S S | a
  2. S → a S b | ε
  3. E → E + E | E * E | ( E ) | id
  4. S → A B, A → a, B → b
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. An operator grammar has no ε-production and no production whose right side contains two adjacent nonterminals, so that any two adjacent terminals can be compared directly.

Step by step.

  1. (A) has S S, two adjacent nonterminals.
  2. (B) has the ε-production S → ε.
  3. (C) satisfies both conditions: no production is empty and no right side has two adjacent nonterminals.
  4. (D) has A B, two adjacent nonterminals.

Answer: Option (C)

Common misconception. Thinking that any expression grammar qualifies. The conditions are purely syntactic, on the right sides of the productions, and a grammar with S → S S fails even though it has no ε.

CD-U3-S2-Q21MCQeasy

An operator-precedence parser keeps both terminals and nonterminals on its stack. Which comparison does it make at each step?

  1. It compares the topmost terminal on the stack with the next input symbol, ignoring the nonterminals above that terminal.
  2. It compares the symbol on top of the stack, whatever kind it is, with the next input symbol.
  3. It compares the top two symbols of the stack with each other.
  4. It compares the leftmost terminal on the stack with the last symbol of the input.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution
