Concept. The precedence relations are defined between terminals only, so the parser must look past the nonterminals sitting above the topmost terminal.

Step by step.

  1. After a reduction the stack top is a nonterminal, and no relation is defined between a nonterminal and a terminal.
  2. The parser therefore scans down to the first terminal, which is always the terminal that borders the handle it is about to recognise.
  3. It then compares that terminal with the next input symbol to choose shift or reduce.

Answer: Option (A)

Common misconception. Comparing the raw stack top. That works only until the first reduction, after which the top is a nonterminal and the comparison is undefined.

CD-U3-S2-Q22MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about operator-precedence parsing are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. The precedence relations are computed from FIRST and LAST of the nonterminals of the grammar.
  2. When the relation between the topmost stack terminal and the next input symbol is ≌, the parser shifts.
  3. An operator grammar may contain a production whose right side is ε.
  4. If no precedence relation holds between the topmost stack terminal and the next input symbol, the parser reports an error, because the input is not a sentence of the grammar.
  5. The precedence relations form a partial order on the terminals: they are transitive and antisymmetric.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The relations come from FIRST and LAST alone, and each of the three relations maps to one parser action: shift on <·, shift on ≌, reduce on ·>.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is the construction: a <· b when b starts a string derivable from a nonterminal that follows a, and a ·> b when a ends one that precedes b.
  2. (B) is true: ≌ means the two terminals are adjacent inside one handle, so both are shifted before that handle is reduced.
  3. (C) is false: an operator grammar has no ε-production by definition.
  4. (D) is true: a missing relation means the pair can never occur in a sentential form, so the input is not derivable.
  5. (E) is false: the relations are neither transitive nor antisymmetric in general; + ·> + and + <· + can both hold in different tables.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D)

Common misconception. Reading ≌ as "reduce". It means the handle has been completed on both sides of the pair, so the parser shifts first and reduces afterwards.

CD-U3-S2-Q23MCQmedium

For the grammar E → E + E | E * E | ( E ) | id, which pair of terminals has no precedence relation in the table derived from FIRST and LAST?

  1. id and +
  2. + and id
  3. ( and id
  4. ) and (
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. FIRST(E) = {(, id} and LAST(E) = {), id}. A relation between a and b exists only when the grammar can put b immediately after a in a sentential form.

Step by step.

  1. id ·> +, because in E → E + E the left E can end with id and is followed by +. So option (A) is not the answer.
  2. + <· id, because the right E of the same production can begin with id. So option (B) is not the answer.
  3. ( <· id, from E → ( E ). So option (C) is not the answer.
  4. No production puts ( immediately after ), and ) is in LAST, never in FIRST, so ) and ( have no relation at all.

Answer: Option (D)

Common misconception. Assuming that every pair of terminals must carry some relation. Of the 25 ordered pairs of these five terminals only 13 do: 6 satisfy <·, 6 satisfy ·> and 1 satisfies ≌.

CD-U3-S2-Q24NATmedium

For the grammar E → E + E | E * E | ( E ) | id, using the relations derived from FIRST and LAST, how many ordered pairs (a, b) of the five terminals +, *, (, ), id satisfy a <· b? Count each ordered pair once. (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. a <· b holds when a is a terminal and b can begin the string that follows it, that is when b ∈ FIRST of the symbol after a.

Step by step.

  1. FIRST(E) = {(, id}.
  2. From E → E + E: + is followed by E, giving + <· id and + <· ( — 2 pairs.
  3. From E → E * E: * <· id and * <· ( — 2 more.
  4. From E → ( E ): ( is followed by E, giving ( <· id and ( <· ( — 2 more.
  5. ) and id are followed by nothing inside any right side, so no pair starts with them. Total 2 + 2 + 2 = 6.

Answer: 6

Common misconception. Counting the pairs of the form ·> as well, which also number 6 and would give 12, or including the end marker $, which adds two more.

CD-U3-S2-Q25MCQhard

In the table derived from FIRST and LAST for E → E + E | E * E | ( E ) | id, no relation holds between + and +. What does this tell us?

  1. The grammar cannot be parsed by an operator-precedence parser built only from FIRST and LAST: reducing E + E before a second + needs an associativity convention that the relations do not supply.
  2. The grammar is not context-free.
  3. The string id + id + id is not in the language of the grammar.
  4. The table is complete: on the second + the parser shifts and the parse succeeds.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The FIRST/LAST relations record where terminals can stand relative to each other. They cannot decide associativity, because that is a property of how the grammar is used, not of its shape.

Step by step.

  1. + is followed by E in E → E + E, so the only <· pairs for + are + <· id and + <· (.
  2. + is preceded by E, and LAST(E) = {), id}, so the only ·> pairs for + are id ·> + and ) ·> +.
  3. Hence (+, +) carries no relation, yet the string id + id + id requires the parser to compare + with + at the second plus.
  4. A practical parser therefore imposes precedence levels and left associativity, which adds + ·> + and * ·> + by hand. The FIRST/LAST derivation alone is incomplete for an ambiguous grammar.

Answer: Option (A)

Common misconception. Believing that a complete FIRST/LAST derivation always yields a usable table. For an ambiguous grammar it leaves gaps, and those gaps are exactly where associativity has to be supplied.

Review the 50 solved questions from Unit 4 — Syntax-Directed Translation & Intermediate Code
S-attributed vs L-attributed definitions · 13
CD-U4-Q01MCQeasy

A syntax-directed definition (SDD) is called S-attributed when:

  1. every attribute it uses is a synthesized attribute
  2. every attribute it uses is an inherited attribute
  3. inherited attributes are used only at the leaves of the parse tree
  4. its semantic rules are evaluated before the parse tree is built
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. An SDD attaches a set of semantic rules to each production. If every attribute it defines is a synthesized attribute, the definition is S-attributed; such a definition can be evaluated by one bottom-up, postorder pass over the parse tree.

Step by step.

  1. The definition of S-attributed turns on one question only: are all attributes synthesized? Option (A) states exactly that.
  2. Option (B) describes the opposite extreme, where every attribute is inherited; no definition with an inherited attribute can be S-attributed.
  3. Option (C) is a restriction on where inherited attributes may appear, not the definition of S-attributed; and a definition with an inherited attribute is not S-attributed wherever the attribute appears.
  4. Option (D) confuses evaluation with construction: semantic rules are applied to nodes of a parse tree, which must therefore exist first. An S-attributed definition is evaluated during a bottom-up parse as each reduction completes, not before the tree exists.

Final answer. Option (A).

Common misconception. Reading the “S” as “synthesized at the root” or as a statement about where attributes are stored. The letter marks the attribute kind, and the test is that no inherited attribute occurs anywhere in the definition.

CD-U4-Q02MCQeasy

Which one of the following statements about S-attributed and L-attributed definitions is TRUE?

  1. Every L-attributed definition is also S-attributed.
  2. Every S-attributed definition is L-attributed, but an L-attributed definition need not be S-attributed.
  3. The class of S-attributed definitions and the class of L-attributed definitions coincide.
  4. Neither class is contained in the other.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. An L-attributed definition allows an inherited attribute of a node to depend only on attributes of its parent, of its left siblings and of itself, together with synthesized attributes of its own children. An S-attributed definition uses no inherited attributes at all, so every one of its dependencies is automatically of the permitted kind.

Step by step.

  1. Take any S-attributed definition. Its rules compute synthesized attributes from the attributes of children, which are themselves synthesized. No rule reads a right sibling, a parent or an inherited attribute, so every rule satisfies the L-attributed restriction.
  2. Hence S-attributed ⊆ L-attributed.
  3. The inclusion is strict. The standard example is the definition for a list of identifiers, D → T L with L.inh = T.type: it carries an inherited attribute and is L-attributed, but it is not S-attributed.
  4. Options (A) and (C) deny the strict inclusion, and option (D) denies the inclusion altogether.

Final answer. Option (B).

Common misconception. Assuming the two classes are the same because both can be evaluated in a single pass. The passes differ: an S-attributed definition is evaluated bottom-up, an L-attributed one left-to-right depth-first, and only the latter can carry inherited information downwards.

CD-U4-Q03MSQeasy

Which of the following are permitted in an L-attributed definition? (Select all that apply.)

  1. An inherited attribute of a node may be computed from an inherited attribute of its parent.
  2. An inherited attribute of a node may be computed from an attribute of a sibling to its left.
  3. An inherited attribute of a node may be computed from an attribute of a sibling to its right.
  4. A synthesized attribute of a node may be computed from an inherited attribute of the same node.
  5. An L-attributed definition can always be evaluated in one left-to-right, depth-first pass over the parse tree.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. L-attributed means “left to right”: every inherited attribute of a node depends only on information that is already available when the traversal reaches that node from the left. Concretely, an inherited attribute of X may depend on inherited attributes of X’s parent, on attributes of X’s left siblings, and on inherited attributes of X itself; a synthesized attribute of X may depend on anything at X or below it.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is permitted: the parent’s inherited attributes are computed before the traversal descends to the child.
  2. (B) is permitted: a left sibling is fully visited before the traversal reaches X.
  3. (C) is not permitted: a right sibling has not been visited yet, so its attributes are unknown at the moment X is visited. This single case is what separates L-attributed from the general class.
  4. (D) is permitted: a synthesized attribute may read the node’s own inherited attributes, which are known before the node is processed.
  5. (E) follows from the definition: because every inherited attribute is available on entry and every synthesized attribute is available on return, one left-to-right depth-first pass computes all of them.

Final answer. Options (A), (B), (D) and (E).

Common misconception. Believing that an inherited attribute may read a right sibling because “the whole tree is there anyway”. The tree exists, but a single pass has not yet computed the right sibling’s values, so such a definition is not L-attributed.

CD-U4-Q04MCQmedium

Consider the SDD: D → T L with the rule L.inh = T.type; T → int with the rule T.type = integer; L → L₁ , id with the rules L₁.inh = L.inh and L.type = L₁.type; L → id with the rule L.type = L.inh. This definition is:

  1. S-attributed.
  2. neither S-attributed nor L-attributed.
  3. L-attributed but not S-attributed.
  4. both S-attributed and L-attributed.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Decide the two questions separately. Is any attribute inherited? If yes, the definition is not S-attributed. Is every inherited attribute computed only from the parent’s inherited attributes, from left siblings and from the node itself? If yes, it is L-attributed.

Step by step.

  1. L.inh is an inherited attribute — it is defined by rules attached to the parent production and flows downwards. So the definition is not S-attributed, which rules out (A) and (D).
  2. Check each inherited rule. L.inh = T.type reads a left sibling of L, which is allowed.
  3. L₁.inh = L.inh reads the parent’s inherited attribute, which is allowed.
  4. The synthesized rules L.type = L₁.type and L.type = L.inh read a child and the node’s own inherited attribute, both allowed.
  5. No rule reads a right sibling, so the definition is L-attributed.

Final answer. Option (C) — L-attributed but not S-attributed.

Common misconception. Classifying a definition as S-attributed because every attribute is eventually passed upwards or because the grammar is not left-recursive. The classification depends on the direction each attribute flows, not on the shape of the grammar.

CD-U4-Q05MCQmedium

Which one of the following semantic rules, added to an otherwise L-attributed definition, would make it neither S-attributed nor L-attributed?

  1. X.syn = Y.syn + Z.syn, where Y and Z are the two children of X.
  2. Y.inh = X.inh, where Y is a child of X.
  3. X.inh = Z.syn, where Z is the sibling immediately to the right of X.
  4. X.syn = X.inh, where X.inh is an inherited attribute of X.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A rule breaks S-attributedness as soon as it mentions an inherited attribute, and it breaks L-attributedness when an inherited attribute depends on something not yet available in a left-to-right depth-first traversal — a right sibling is the standard case.

Step by step.

  1. (A) uses only synthesized attributes of children: perfectly S-attributed, hence also L-attributed.
  2. (B) is the archetypal L-attributed rule — information flowing from parent to child. It does break S-attributedness, but not L-attributedness, so it does not give “neither”.
  3. (C) makes an inherited attribute of X depend on a right sibling. When the traversal reaches X, that sibling has not been visited and its synthesized attribute has no value, so the rule cannot be evaluated in one pass. It also uses an inherited attribute, so it is not S-attributed. The definition is neither.
  4. (D) reads the node’s own inherited attribute, which is available on entry: L-attributed, though not S-attributed.

Final answer. Option (C).

Common misconception. Thinking that a rule involving an inherited attribute automatically destroys L-attributedness. Inherited attributes are the whole point of the class; only their source is restricted.

CD-U4-Q06MSQhard

Which of the following statements about the evaluation of attributes are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. A parse tree for an S-attributed definition can be annotated by one bottom-up, postorder traversal.
  2. Every syntax-directed definition can be evaluated during a single pass over its parse tree.
  3. A parse tree for an L-attributed definition can be annotated by one left-to-right, depth-first traversal.
  4. Dependencies among inherited attributes can form a cycle, so that no evaluation order exists for that parse tree.
  5. If the dependency graph of a particular parse tree is acyclic, a topological order of that graph gives a valid evaluation order.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Attributes and semantic rules induce a directed graph on the annotated parse tree: an edge runs from every attribute used on the right of a rule to the attribute defined on the left. Any topological order of that graph is a legal evaluation order, and a single pass is possible only when a pass order happens to respect every edge.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is the standard result: synthesized attributes of a node depend only on its children, so finishing the children first, in postorder, computes every value exactly once.
  2. (B) is false. A definition whose rules need information from a right sibling, or whose dependency graph is circular, cannot be evaluated in one pass; it needs several passes or a separate dependency analysis.
  3. (C) is the definition of L-attributed restated as an evaluation strategy: inherited attributes are available on the way down, synthesized ones on the way up.
  4. (D) is true. With A.syn = B.syn in one production and B.inh = A.inh plus A.inh = B.syn arranged around a cycle of nodes, the graph can close on itself; such a definition has no evaluation order at all.
  5. (E) is true and is the general algorithm: build the graph for the given tree and sort it. A single pass is then a special case in which the pass order is already topological.

Final answer. Options (A), (C), (D) and (E).

Common misconception. Assuming that because a parse tree is finite, some evaluation order must exist. Finiteness does not prevent a cycle: the dependency graph can be circular even on a tree with three nodes.

CD-U4-Q07NATmedium

For the grammar E → E + T | T, T → T * F | F, F → ( E ) | id, let every E, T and F node carry one synthesized attribute val. For the input x + y * z, how many instances of val are there in the annotated parse tree? (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. In an annotated parse tree, each attribute of each node is one instance. Counting instances is therefore counting the nodes that carry the attribute — here every E, T and F node, but not the id leaves, which carry lexval instead.

Step by step.

  1. The grammar forces the shape of the tree. x + y * z parses as E → E + T at the root, because + is the outermost operator; the right operand y * z is a T.
  2. Root: E₀ → E₁ + T₀. That is two E nodes and one T node so far.
  3. E₁ → T₁ → F₁ → id x: one T and one F.
  4. T₀ → T₂ * F₂: two T-side nodes, one T₂ and one F₂.
  5. T₂ → F₃ → id y: one more F. F₂ → id z.
  6. Total: E nodes 2, T nodes 3, F nodes 3 → 2 + 3 + 3 = 8.

Final answer. 8 instances of val.

Common misconception. Counting the three id leaves as well and answering 11, or forgetting that E₁ → T contributes a node of its own and answering 7.

CD-U4-Q26MCQeasy

Consider the class of L-attributed definitions. Which one of the following statements is TRUE?

  1. Every attribute of every symbol must be a synthesized attribute.
  2. An inherited attribute of a symbol on the right side may be computed from attributes of the symbols to its left and from the inherited attributes of the head of the production.
  3. An inherited attribute of a symbol on the right side may be computed from attributes of the symbols to its right.
  4. Such a definition can be evaluated only by a bottom-up parser.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A definition is L-attributed when every inherited attribute of a right-side symbol depends only on information already available when the parse tree is traversed left to right: the inherited attributes of the head, the attributes of the symbols to its left, and its own inherited attributes. Synthesized attributes may depend on anything at the node or below it.

Step by step.

  1. Option (A) describes S-attributed, the special case in which there are no inherited attributes at all; it is not what L-attributed means.
  2. Option (B) states the L-condition exactly: left siblings and the head are both visited before the current symbol, so their values are ready.
  3. Option (C) is precisely what L-attributed forbids — a right sibling has not been visited yet when the current node is reached.
  4. Option (D) is false: the standard use of an L-attributed definition is a single left-to-right depth-first pass, which is exactly what a top-down parser performs.

Final answer. Option (B).

Common misconception. Treating L-attributed and S-attributed as opposites. S-attributed is a proper subset of L-attributed; the L-condition says nothing that an all-synthesized definition would violate.

CD-U4-Q27MCQmedium

An SDD has one production, S → A B, with the rules A.i = S.i, B.i = A.s and S.s = B.s. Which of the following is a valid order for evaluating all six attributes of one node of the parse tree?

  1. S.i, A.i, A.s, B.i, B.s, S.s
  2. S.i, A.i, B.i, A.s, B.s, S.s
  3. A.s, A.i, S.i, B.i, B.s, S.s
  4. S.s, S.i, A.i, A.s, B.i, B.s
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A valid evaluation order is any topological order of the dependency graph induced by the semantic rules: a rule can be applied only after every attribute it reads has been computed.

Step by step.

  1. A.i reads S.i, so S.i must come first.
  2. B.i reads A.s, so A.s must precede B.i.
  3. S.s reads B.s, so S.s must come last.
  4. Option (A) respects all three constraints: S.i, then A.i, then A.s, then B.i, then B.s, then S.s.
  5. Option (B) evaluates B.i before A.s, which the rule forbids. Option (C) evaluates A.i before S.i. Option (D) evaluates S.s first.

Final answer. Option (A).

Common misconception. Reading the rules as if they were assignment statements executed in the order written. Only the data dependencies matter, and any order consistent with them is valid.

CD-U4-Q28MSQmedium

Which of the following claims about the relationship between S-attributed and L-attributed definitions hold? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Every S-attributed definition is also L-attributed.
  2. Every L-attributed definition is also S-attributed.
  3. An L-attributed definition can be evaluated in a single left-to-right, depth-first pass over the parse tree.
  4. An S-attributed definition needs a separate stack for inherited attributes during bottom-up evaluation.
  5. A definition in which one attribute of a node is computed from another attribute of the same node cannot be L-attributed.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The L-condition restricts only inherited attributes of right-side symbols. A definition with no inherited attributes satisfies it vacuously.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is true. S-attributed means all attributes are synthesized; the L-condition is then never triggered, so the definition is L-attributed as well.
  2. (B) is false. L.type = L.inh in the standard list grammar uses an inherited attribute, so that definition is L-attributed but not S-attributed.
  3. (C) is true. That single pass is the defining motivation for the class: values flow down the left spine and up on return.
  4. (D) is false. An S-attributed definition defines no inherited attributes at all, so no stack for them is needed; values travel on the parser's own value stack.
  5. (E) is false. Rules such as L.type = L.inh and A.s = A.i + 1 combine two attributes of the same node and are perfectly legal in an L-attributed definition.

Final answer. Options (A) and (C).

Common misconception. Assuming the two classes coincide. The proper inclusion S-attributed ⊂ L-attributed is the single most examined fact about them.

CD-U4-Q29NATmedium

An SDD contains the productions S → A B with rules A.i = 1, B.i = A.s and S.s = B.s; A → id with rule A.s = A.i + 1; and B → id with rule B.s = B.i × 2. What is the value of S.s at the root? (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Evaluate the attributes in dependency order: inherited attributes flow down from the root, synthesized attributes flow up from the leaves.

Step by step.

  1. A.i = 1 is set by the rule on S → A B.
  2. A.s = A.i + 1 = 1 + 1 = 2.
  3. B.i = A.s = 2 — a left-sibling dependency, which is what makes this definition L-attributed rather than S-attributed.
  4. B.s = B.i × 2 = 4.
  5. S.s = B.s = 4.

Final answer. 4.

Common misconception. Substituting A.s as 1 by ignoring the +1 rule, which gives 2 at the root instead of 4.

CD-U4-Q30MCQhard

During bottom-up (LR) parsing of an S-attributed definition, where are synthesized attribute values kept?

  1. In a separate attribute stack indexed by parse-tree node, allocated when each node is created.
  2. On the parser's value stack beside each grammar symbol, so that a reduction finds the values of the right-side symbols at the top of the stack.
  3. In a hash table keyed by parser state, filled in when each terminal is shifted.
  4. Nowhere — a synthesized attribute cannot be computed during bottom-up parsing, so a separate pass over the parse tree is required.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Bottom-up parsing reduces a handle after all of its right-side symbols have been shifted or reduced, so their synthesized values are already on top of the stack when the reduction fires.

Step by step.

  1. A reduction pops k states and symbols; the corresponding k attribute values are the top k entries of the value stack.
  2. The semantic action computes the head's attribute from those values and pushes the single result together with the new state.
  3. No node-indexed structure is needed, because a node's value is consumed exactly once, at the reduction that creates it.
  4. Option (D) is the misconception that semantic analysis must follow parsing; the standard construction is a parser with an added value stack.

Final answer. Option (B).

Common misconception. Believing an S-attributed definition is evaluated after parsing. It is evaluated during parsing, in lock-step with the reductions.

CD-U4-Q31MCQeasy

Which one of the following attributes must be an inherited attribute?

  1. The value of an expression node, computed from the values of its children.
  2. The data type of an identifier, passed down from its declaration to a use of that identifier.
  3. The number of nodes in the subtree rooted at a node.
  4. The lexical value of an identifier, returned by the scanner.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A synthesized attribute is computed from the children of a node; an inherited attribute is computed from the parent, the siblings or the node itself.

Step by step.

  1. Option (A) reads only the children, so it is synthesized.
  2. Option (B) flows from the enclosing declaration downward to a use — information that is not available from the use itself, so it must be inherited.
  3. Option (C) reads only the subtree, so it is synthesized.
  4. Option (D) is supplied by the lexer at the leaf, so it is synthesized (a lexical attribute).

Final answer. Option (B).

Common misconception. Deciding the direction from where the information "lives" in the program text rather than from the direction in which it travels in the tree.

Three-address code, quadruples & triples · 14
CD-U4-Q08MCQeasy

Which one of the following is NOT a valid three-address instruction?

  1. x = y + z
  2. x[i] = y
  3. if x < y goto L
  4. x = y * z * w
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A three-address instruction names at most three addresses: normally two operands and one result, plus the operator. A binary operator is therefore the most an instruction may contain.

Step by step.

  1. x = y + z has result x and operands y, z: valid, the canonical form.
  2. x[i] = y is the indexed copy instruction with result x, index i and source y: valid.
  3. if x < y goto L is the conditional jump with operands x, y and target L: valid.
  4. x = y * z * w contains two operators and four addresses, so it must be broken into two instructions, for example t1 = y * z followed by x = t1 * w.

Final answer. Option (D).

Common misconception. Reading “three-address” as “three variables may appear” and therefore accepting a longer expression. The limit is on operators per instruction, which is what keeps each instruction cheap to translate and to reorder.

CD-U4-Q09MCQeasy

In the quadruple representation of three-address code, the four fields are, in order:

  1. operator, arg1, arg2, result
  2. operator, result, arg1, arg2
  3. result, arg1, arg2, operator
  4. arg1, arg2, operator, result
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A quadruple records one instruction as four slots: the operator and its two arguments, followed by the name of the result. A temporary appears explicitly in the result field.

Step by step.

  1. Take t1 = b * c. The operator is *, arg1 is b, arg2 is c and the result is t1.
  2. So the fields read operator, arg1, arg2, result — option (A).
  3. Instructions that do not need both arguments leave a slot empty: for t1 = -c the operator is unary minus, arg1 is c, arg2 is unused, and for a plain copy x = t1 the operator is = with arg1 t1.

Final answer. Option (A).

Common misconception. Expecting the result first because it is written first in the source statement. In the quadruple table the destination sits in the fourth column, which is why a reader has to scan to the end of the row to see where a value lands.

CD-U4-Q10MCQmedium

Why are indirect triples often preferred to plain triples?

  1. They store the result of every instruction in an explicitly named temporary.
  2. They keep a separate array of pointers to the triples, so the instructions can be reordered without rewriting the positional references inside them.
  3. They need fewer instructions than quadruples for the same expression.
  4. They remove the need for temporaries altogether.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. In a triple, an operand that holds the value of an earlier computation is written as that triple’s index, for example (3). This makes the position of a triple part of the meaning of other triples.

Step by step.

  1. Because positions are cited by number, any reordering of a plain triple list invalidates every reference into it. Optimisations such as moving code out of a loop do exactly that.
  2. Indirect triples add one level of indirection: a separate array holds pointers (or indices) to the triples, and the program order is the order of that pointer array.
  3. Reordering now means permuting the pointer array only; the triples keep their indices and their internal references stay correct.
  4. Options (A) and (D) describe quadruples and temporaries wrongly, and (C) is false: all three representations describe the same instructions, only the bookkeeping differs.

Final answer. Option (B).

Common misconception. Believing indirect triples save instructions or storage. They add a pointer array; what they buy is the freedom to reorder code cheaply, which is why optimising compilers prefer them.

CD-U4-Q11NATmedium

For the assignment a = b * -c + d, using one three-address instruction per operator and one for the final assignment, and never reusing a temporary, how many three-address instructions are needed? (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Translating an expression to three-address code emits one instruction per operator, plus one copy instruction for the assignment to the left-hand-side variable. Temporaries carry the intermediate values.

Step by step.

  1. Unary minus is an operator: t1 = -c  (1)
  2. The multiplication: t2 = b * t1  (2)
  3. The addition: t3 = t2 + d  (3)
  4. The assignment to a: a = t3  (4)

Final answer. 4 instructions.

Common misconception. Dropping the unary minus as if b * -c were a single operator and answering 3. In three-address code every operator, unary or binary, becomes its own instruction.

CD-U4-Q12MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about the representations of three-address code are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. In a quadruple the result field names the temporary explicitly.
  2. In a triple a temporary is referred to by the index of the triple that computes it.
  3. In indirect triples the list of pointers can be reordered without touching the triples themselves.
  4. A three-address instruction has at most three addresses, one of which may be the result.
  5. In static single-assignment form the same variable may be defined more than once in a program.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. All three representations describe the same instructions and differ only in bookkeeping: quadruples name results, triples identify results by position, and indirect triples add a pointer array so that position and program order can differ.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is the definition of the fourth field of a quadruple.
  2. (B) is the definition of a triple reference such as (3).
  3. (C) is the purpose of the extra array: program order becomes the order of the pointers, not the order of the triples.
  4. (D) is the instruction format itself: two operands and a result at most.
  5. (E) is false. Static single-assignment form renames each definition so that every variable is assigned exactly once; later definitions become new names such as x₁ and x₂.

Final answer. Options (A), (B), (C) and (D).

Common misconception. Confusing SSA with ordinary three-address code. Ordinary code may assign the same variable many times; SSA forbids it precisely so that each name has a single definition site, which is what makes data-flow facts easy to state.

CD-U4-Q13MCQhard

a and b are arrays of 4-byte integers with base addresses a0 and b0; i and j are integers and c is an integer. Which sequence correctly translates a[i] = b[j] + c?

  1. t1 = j * 4; t2 = b[t1]; t3 = t2 + c; a[i] = t3
  2. t1 = b[j]; t2 = t1 + c; a0[i] = t2
  3. t1 = j * 4; t2 = b[t1]; t3 = t2 + c; t4 = i * 4; a[t4] = t3
  4. t1 = i * 4; a[t1] = b[j] + c
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. An array access with element size w and base base is translated by computing the byte offset index * w and addressing base[offset]. Every indexed access — on either side of the assignment — needs its own offset computation, and the right-hand side is evaluated before the left-hand side is stored into.

Step by step.

  1. Right-hand side, inner access: t1 = j * 4 scales the subscript of b; t2 = b[t1] fetches the element.
  2. Right-hand side, addition: t3 = t2 + c.
  3. Left-hand side: t4 = i * 4 scales the subscript of a; then a[t4] = t3 stores the value.
  4. (A) never scales i, so it stores into the wrong element. (B) treats b[j] as a single address and scales neither subscript. (D) folds the addition into one instruction, which is not three-address code, and leaves b[j] unscaled as well.

Final answer. Option (C).

Common misconception. Assuming that the left-hand side needs no offset computation because “the destination is just a place”. Both sides index an array, so both need index * 4, and the store must come after the value has been computed.

CD-U4-Q14NAThard

For a = b * c + d * e + f * g, using one instruction per operator, no common subexpression elimination and no reuse of temporaries, how many temporaries does the three-address code need? (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. With no reuse, each computed value occupies a fresh temporary; the final assignment writes into the named variable rather than into a temporary.

Step by step.

  1. t1 = b * c
  2. t2 = d * e
  3. t3 = t1 + t2 — the left addition, left to right
  4. t4 = f * g
  5. t5 = t3 + t4
  6. a = t5 — the assignment targets a, not a temporary

Final answer. 5 temporaries (in 6 instructions).

Common misconception. Answering 4 by counting only the four arithmetic results and forgetting that the two additions also need temporaries, or answering 6 by counting a as a temporary.

CD-U4-Q32MCQeasy

In the triple representation of three-address code, how does a later instruction refer to a value produced by an earlier instruction?

  1. By a named temporary variable.
  2. By the position number of the triple that produced it.
  3. By the address of the result operand.
  4. By a register number assigned during code generation.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A triple stores only three fields — operator, operand, operand — and there is no result field. The result of a triple is the triple itself, identified by its index.

Step by step.

  1. The triples for a = b * c + d are (0) * b c, (1) + (0) d, (2) = a (1).
  2. Triple 1 names triple 0 by the parenthesised index (0); no temporary name is introduced.
  3. Option (A) describes quadruples, which do carry a named result. Options (C) and (D) belong to later phases.

Final answer. Option (B).

Common misconception. Expecting a result field in a triple. The absence of that field is exactly why triples are compact and why moving a triple renumbers its uses.

CD-U4-Q33MCQmedium

Which sequence correctly translates the short-circuit test if (x < y && y < z) goto L?

  1. if x < y goto L1; goto L2; L1: if y < z goto L; L2:
  2. if x < y goto L; if y < z goto L
  3. if x < y goto L; goto L
  4. if y < z goto L; if x < y goto L
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Short-circuit && evaluates the second operand only when the first is true. Control flow therefore falls through past the whole test as soon as one conjunct fails.

Step by step.

  1. If x < y holds, the decision depends on y < z, so control goes to the second test at L1.
  2. If x < y fails, the conjunction is false and y < z must not be evaluated; control jumps past the test to L2.
  3. At L1 the truth of y < z sends control to L; failure simply falls through to L2.
  4. Option (B) evaluates y < z even when x < y is false, which is the non-short-circuit semantics.

Final answer. Option (A).

Common misconception. Writing the two tests back to back with the same target. That implements or-style fall-through, not and.

CD-U4-Q34MSQeasy

In extended three-address code (the form that also allows indexed addresses), which of the following are valid instructions? (Select all that apply.)

  1. x = y + z
  2. x = y * z + w
  3. x = y[z]
  4. goto L
  5. x = y = z
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Three-address code allows at most one operator on the right of an assignment, together with an assignment or a jump. Each instruction therefore has at most three addresses.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is the canonical form: one operator, three addresses, valid.
  2. (B) has two operators and needs a temporary for the product, so it is not a three-address instruction.
  3. (C) is an indexed copy, a standard extension used for arrays; it has three addresses and is valid.
  4. (D) is a jump, which is one of the permitted instruction forms.
  5. (E) is a double assignment, which requires two separate instructions.

Final answer. Options (A), (C) and (D).

Common misconception. Rejecting the indexed copy because it "looks like an array expression". The restriction is on the number of operators, not on the shape of an address.

CD-U4-Q35NATeasy

In the quadruple form (op, arg1, arg2, result) a unary operation leaves arg2 empty: t1 = -b becomes (uminus, b, -, t1). For the code t1 = -b followed by t2 = a * t1, how many quadruples have an empty arg2 field? (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A quadruple always has four fields; a field that an instruction does not use is filled with a placeholder. Unary operators use only arg1.

Step by step.

  1. Quadruple 1 is (uminus, b, -, t1): arg2 is empty.
  2. Quadruple 2 is (*, a, t1, t2): both operand fields are used.
  3. Exactly one quadruple in the sequence has an empty arg2.

Final answer. 1.

Common misconception. Expecting a third operand for a unary minus, or counting the placeholder in the result field of a jump as an empty arg2 when no jump is present.

CD-U4-Q36MCQmedium

For a = b * c + d * e, using one instruction per operator and one for the final assignment, what is the minimum number of distinct temporaries required if a temporary may be reused as soon as its value is no longer needed?

  1. 1
  2. 2
  3. 3
  4. 4
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The number of temporaries needed at any point is the maximum number of values simultaneously live; temporaries can be recycled after their last use.

Step by step.

  1. t1 = b * c — one temporary live.
  2. t2 = d * e — two live, since t1 is still needed.
  3. t1 = t1 + t2 — t2 dies here and its slot is free, but only two were ever live at once.
  4. a = t1 — the final assignment consumes the last temporary.
  5. The peak liveness is 2, so two temporaries suffice; one is impossible because both products must exist before they are added.

Final answer. Option (B) — 2.

Common misconception. Counting temporaries by counting operators. Four operators do not imply four temporaries; only simultaneous liveness matters.

CD-U4-Q37NAThard

An array is declared int a[1..10][1..20], each element occupying 4 bytes, stored in row-major order, with base address 1000. In the three-address code for a[i][j], what byte offset is added to the base address when i = 4 and j = 7? (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. In row-major order the offset of a[i][j] in an array declared a[lo₁..hi₁][lo₂..hi₂] is ((i − lo₁)·n₂ + (j − lo₂))·w, where n₂ = hi₂ − lo₂ + 1 is the number of columns and w is the element width.

Step by step.

  1. Columns per row: n₂ = 20 − 1 + 1 = 20.
  2. Rows skipped: i − lo₁ = 4 − 1 = 3, contributing 3 × 20 = 60 elements.
  3. Columns skipped within the row: j − lo₂ = 7 − 1 = 6.
  4. Total elements before a[4][7]: 60 + 6 = 66.
  5. Byte offset: 66 × 4 = 264, so the address is 1000 + 264 = 1264.

Final answer. 264 (the offset; the full address is 1264).

Common misconception. Using i·n₂ + j and answering (80 + 7) × 4 = 348. The lower bounds are 1, not 0, and the question asks for the offset from the base, not the absolute address.

CD-U4-Q38MCQhard

Consider while (a < b) do S, translated with the loop test at the top, a jump that leaves the loop when the test fails, and a jump at the end of the body back to the test. How many jump instructions appear in the generated three-address code?

  1. 1
  2. 2
  3. 3
  4. 4
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A while loop has one conditional exit jump at the test and one unconditional jump back from the end of the body; the exit path needs a jump over the body only when the body is placed before the test.

Step by step.

  1. L1: if a < b goto L2 — the conditional jump that enters the body.
  2. goto L3 — leaves the loop when the test fails.
  3. L2: code for S.
  4. goto L1 — the unconditional jump back to the test.
  5. L3: the statement after the loop. That is three jumps in total.

Final answer. Option (C) — 3.

Common misconception. Answering 2 by counting only the test and the back edge. The exit jump over the body is a separate instruction in this scheme, and a compiler that inverts the test (if a ≥ b goto L3) would emit only two — which is why the stem fixes the scheme.

Syntax trees & DAGs · 12
CD-U4-Q15MCQeasy

How does the DAG of an expression differ from its syntax tree?

  1. Common subexpressions are shared, so a node may have more than one parent.
  2. Leaves are shared but interior nodes never are.
  3. It always has more nodes than the syntax tree.
  4. It can represent only straight-line code without assignments.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A syntax tree gives every subexpression its own node. A DAG merges nodes that denote the same value, so a shared node has several parents — which is exactly why it is a directed acyclic graph rather than a tree.

Step by step.

  1. For (a + b) * (a + b) the syntax tree contains two separate + nodes, one for each occurrence.
  2. The DAG contains one + node with two parents, the two operands of *.
  3. Sharing applies to interior nodes and leaves alike, so (B) is wrong.
  4. Merging can only reduce the node count, so (C) is wrong, and DAGs represent whole basic blocks including assignments, so (D) is wrong.

Final answer. Option (A).

Common misconception. Believing the DAG is only a drawing convention. Sharing is the data structure that lets the compiler see that the second occurrence needs no new computation.

CD-U4-Q16MCQeasy

Which optimisation is exposed directly by building a DAG for a basic block?

  1. loop unrolling
  2. register allocation
  3. strength reduction
  4. common subexpression elimination
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Two occurrences of the same expression map to the same node in the DAG. Every node with more than one parent is therefore a common subexpression, computed once and reused.

Step by step.

  1. Build the DAG for the block, creating one node per distinct value.
  2. Read the DAG back out: a node with several parents is emitted once, and its later uses refer to the earlier result.
  3. Dead-code elimination is also available from the DAG — nodes with no live value are dropped — but the optimisation the construction exposes directly is common subexpression elimination.
  4. Loop unrolling, register allocation and strength reduction are loop-level or machine-level transformations; a single basic block contains no loop to unroll.

Final answer. Option (D).

Common misconception. Expecting the DAG to reorder code. It only merges equal values; the instruction order still has to be recovered from the graph when the block is emitted.

CD-U4-Q17NAThard

For the expression a + a * (b - c) + (b - c) * d, a DAG is built in which two nodes are merged exactly when they have the same operator and the same children, and a leaf is created once per distinct variable. How many nodes does the DAG contain in total, counting leaves and interior nodes once each? (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Build the DAG bottom-up, creating a leaf once per distinct variable and merging any two interior nodes with the same operator and the same children. The two subtractions b - c are identical, so they share one node.

Step by step.

  1. Leaves: a, a, b, c, b, c, d collapse to the four distinct leaves a, b, c, d.
  2. n1 = b - c, created once and reused for both occurrences.
  3. n2 = a * n1.
  4. n3 = a + n2.
  5. n4 = n1 * d.
  6. n5 = n3 + n4, the root.
  7. Interior nodes n1 to n5 = 5; leaves = 4; total = 9.

Final answer. 9 nodes.

Common misconception. Building a syntax tree instead and answering 12 (four leaves plus eight operator occurrences), or merging the two multiplications because they “look alike” — they have different children, so they stay distinct.

CD-U4-Q18MCQmedium

In a DAG built for a basic block, an interior node labelled + with two children represents:

  1. two variables that are added somewhere in the block.
  2. the value obtained by applying + to the values of its children, a node shared by every use of that value.
  3. an instruction that must be executed twice.
  4. an operand whose value is not known at compile time.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Each node of the DAG denotes a value. A leaf denotes the value of a variable; an interior node denotes the value produced by applying its operator to the values of its children.

Step by step.

  1. The label names the operator, the children name the operands, and the node names the result.
  2. Because nodes are merged on identical operator and identical children, a node with several parents is a value computed once and used several times.
  3. Option (C) confuses the value with the instruction: a shared node means the opposite of executing twice.
  4. Option (D) confuses a node with an unknown: every node in the DAG of a basic block is a value the block computes.

Final answer. Option (B).

Common misconception. Treating the DAG as a picture of the statements. It is a picture of the values the statements compute, which is why the statement count and the node count differ.

CD-U4-Q19MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about DAGs for basic blocks are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. A DAG exposes the common subexpressions of a basic block.
  2. A node whose value is never used can be removed from the DAG, which is dead-code elimination.
  3. Every syntax tree is a DAG.
  4. A DAG never has more nodes than the syntax tree of the same expression.
  5. A DAG for an expression can always be converted back into a unique syntax tree.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The DAG is obtained from the syntax tree by merging equal subexpressions. Merging never creates a node and never changes a value, so the DAG is a quotient of the tree.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is the point of the construction: a node with several parents is a common subexpression.
  2. (B) is true. A node with no parent outside the DAG and no live variable attached computes a value nobody reads; deleting it, and then its orphaned children, is dead-code elimination.
  3. (C) is true: a tree is an acyclic directed graph in which every node has at most one parent.
  4. (D) is true because the DAG is built by merging nodes of the tree; the node count can only fall.
  5. (E) is false. After merging, the information about which occurrence came first is gone: (a + b) * (a + b) and a block computing a + b once and using it twice give the same DAG, and a shared node can be expanded in several different tree shapes.

Final answer. Options (A), (B), (C) and (D).

Common misconception. Believing the DAG is a lossless replacement for the syntax tree. It preserves values, not occurrences, so a unique tree cannot be recovered from it.

CD-U4-Q20MCQeasy

Local value numbering finds common subexpressions in a basic block by:

  1. sorting the instructions of the block by operator.
  2. counting how many times each variable is assigned.
  3. giving the same number to two expressions that have the same operator and the same numbered operands.
  4. building the loop-nesting structure of the block.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Value numbering walks the block in order, assigning a number to each value. A leaf variable gets a number; an expression gets a number determined by its operator and the numbers of its operands. Two expressions with the same key are the same value.

Step by step.

  1. Read the block left to right, keeping a table from keys to numbers.
  2. For t1 = b * c the key is (*, #b, #c); it is new, so it receives a fresh number.
  3. When a later instruction has the same key, it receives the same number, so it is a redundant computation and can be replaced by a copy of the earlier result.
  4. Sorting instructions, counting assignments and analysing loop nesting are different analyses and none of them identifies equal expressions.

Final answer. Option (C).

Common misconception. Comparing expressions as text. Value numbering compares the values of operands, so it also catches t2 = x * y when x was previously copied from b and y from c.

CD-U4-Q39MCQeasy

When a parse tree is condensed into a syntax tree, which one of the following is the result?

  1. Every grammar symbol is kept as an interior node, including those introduced by single-production chains.
  2. Nodes introduced by single-production chains and the parentheses are dropped, so operators become interior nodes and operands become leaves.
  3. Every operator node is duplicated, so the syntax tree has strictly more nodes than the parse tree.
  4. Only the leaves survive, and operator precedence is recovered from the order of the tokens.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A syntax tree is a condensed parse tree: nodes whose productions have a single symbol on the right, and punctuation such as parentheses, are removed because they carry no value.

Step by step.

  1. For id + id * id the parse tree contains chains such as E → T → F → id.
  2. The syntax tree collapses each such chain to one node per operator, with the operands as leaves.
  3. Precedence is preserved because the tree shape is unchanged: the multiplication subtree is still a child of the addition.
  4. Hence the syntax tree never has more nodes than the parse tree, and it does represent precedence.

Final answer. Option (B).

Common misconception. Believing the syntax tree loses information. It loses only nodes that carry no operator and no operand.

CD-U4-Q40MCQmedium

A DAG is built for the expression ((a + b) × (a + b)) − (a + b), merging two nodes exactly when they carry the same operator and the same children, and creating one leaf per distinct variable. How many nodes does the DAG contain in total?

  1. 4
  2. 5
  3. 6
  4. 7
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A DAG shares every subexpression that denotes the same value, so a repeated subexpression is stored once and reached by several edges.

Step by step.

  1. Leaves: a and b — 2 nodes.
  2. Interior node n1 = +(a, b), built once and shared by all three occurrences.
  3. Interior node n2 = ×(n1, n1).
  4. Interior node n3 = −(n2, n1).
  5. Total: 2 leaves + 3 interior nodes = 5.

Final answer. Option (B) — 5.

Common misconception. Counting three separate + nodes and answering 7. Sharing is the whole point of the DAG.

CD-U4-Q41NATeasy

For the expression a × b + a × b, identical operator nodes are shared and each distinct variable contributes one leaf. How many nodes does the resulting DAG contain in total? (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Identical subexpressions collapse to one node in a DAG, which is how common subexpressions become visible.

Step by step.

  1. Leaves a and b: 2 nodes.
  2. n1 = ×(a, b): the two products are the same node.
  3. n2 = +(n1, n1): 1 node.
  4. Total 2 + 1 + 1 = 4.

Final answer. 4.

Common misconception. Answering 6 by keeping both product nodes, which is the syntax tree, not the DAG.

CD-U4-Q42MSQhard

A DAG for a basic block is built statement by statement. Which of the following statements about that construction are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Two interior nodes are merged exactly when they carry the same operator and the same ordered list of children.
  2. A leaf labelled with a variable may have more than one parent.
  3. Every interior node of the DAG has exactly one parent.
  4. The DAG can be built incrementally, one statement at a time, reusing an existing node whenever an identical one is already present.
  5. Building the DAG requires the whole basic block to be parsed into a syntax tree before any node is created.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The DAG is built by a value-numbering style pass: each variable and each operator application is given a number, and a repeated number means the node already exists.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is true and is the merge rule; the order of children matters, since subtraction and division are not commutative.
  2. (B) is true — a variable used in several statements has one leaf with several parents, which is exactly the sharing the DAG records.
  3. (C) is false. An interior node may also have several parents, for example the shared +(a, b) in ((a + b) × (a + b)).
  4. (D) is true; that single incremental pass is the standard construction and is equivalent to local value numbering.
  5. (E) is false. No separate syntax tree is needed; nodes are created as each statement is processed.

Final answer. Options (A), (B) and (D).

Common misconception. Assuming a DAG is still a tree apart from its leaves. Any node, leaf or interior, may have several parents.

CD-U4-Q43MCQmedium

Which statement relating the syntax tree and the DAG of the same expression is TRUE?

  1. The DAG always has strictly fewer nodes than the syntax tree.
  2. The DAG never has more nodes than the syntax tree, and has fewer exactly when some subexpression occurs more than once.
  3. The DAG and the syntax tree always have the same number of nodes.
  4. The DAG may have more nodes, because it records sharing with extra nodes.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A DAG is obtained from a syntax tree by merging nodes that denote the same value, so it is at most as large as the tree.

Step by step.

  1. Every node of the DAG corresponds to at least one node of the syntax tree, so the DAG has no more nodes.
  2. If no subexpression repeats, nothing can be merged and the two have the same size — which rules out option (A).
  3. Sharing is recorded by extra edges, never by extra nodes, which rules out option (D).

Final answer. Option (B).

Common misconception. Thinking a DAG needs a new node to point at a shared value. A second edge from the existing node is enough.

CD-U4-Q44MCQhard

A DAG is built for the basic block d = a + b; e = a + b; f = d + e. How many interior nodes does the DAG contain?

  1. 1
  2. 2
  3. 3
  4. 4
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Each statement adds at most one interior node, and an identical node is reused rather than created.

Step by step.

  1. d = a + b creates n1 = +(a, b); d is attached to n1.
  2. e = a + b finds the identical node n1 already present, so no node is created; e is attached to the same n1.
  3. f = d + e becomes +(n1, n1), a new node n2, because its children are node numbers, not the operands a and b.
  4. Interior nodes: n1 and n2 — two. Adding the three leaves a, b and the variable labels gives the full node count, but only two are interior.

Final answer. Option (B) — 2.

Common misconception. Merging f's node with n1 because both are additions over the same values. Merging compares the child nodes; +(a, b) and +(n1, n1) are different nodes even though they compute the same value.

Type checking · 11
CD-U4-Q21MCQeasy

Which one of the following is a static semantic check that a compiler performs at compile time?

  1. that the operands of + have compatible types
  2. that an array subscript is within bounds at run time
  3. that a division by zero never occurs
  4. that a pointer is non-null when it is dereferenced
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Static checks are decided from the program text alone, before execution; dynamic checks need the run-time values and are therefore inserted as code or left to the run-time system.

Step by step.

  1. Type compatibility of + depends only on the declared types of the operands, all of which the compiler knows. It is decidable statically.
  2. Bounds of a subscript depend on the value of the index at run time; in general this cannot be decided by inspection.
  3. Division by zero depends on the divisor’s run-time value, and deciding it in general is undecidable.
  4. Whether a pointer is null depends on the execution path, so it is a run-time property.

Final answer. Option (A).

Common misconception. Assuming a compiler checks everything a language forbids. Many forbidden states — out-of-range subscripts, null dereference — are caught by run-time checks or not at all.

CD-U4-Q22MCQmedium

In type-expression notation, the type of a function that takes an integer and returns a pointer to a character is:

  1. integer × char
  2. char → integer
  3. pointer(integer) → char
  4. integer → pointer(char)
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Type expressions are built with constructors: array, pointer, × for a product and → for a function. A function type is written s → t, where s is the domain and t the range, so the arrow points from argument to result.

Step by step.

  1. Domain: one argument of type integer.
  2. Range: pointer(char), a pointer to a character.
  3. Compose: integer → pointer(char).
  4. (A) is a product of two types, not a function. (B) reverses domain and range. (C) puts the pointer on the argument.

Final answer. Option (D).

Common misconception. Reading → as “returns” from the wrong end, or treating the arrow as symmetric. It is not: integer → char and char → integer are different types.

CD-U4-Q23MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about type checking are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Type checking can be done statically, before the program runs, or dynamically, while it runs.
  2. A sound type system guarantees that a well-typed program cannot suffer a run-time type error.
  3. A complete type checker accepts every program that cannot suffer a run-time type error.
  4. A coercion is an implicit conversion inserted by the compiler to make the types of two operands agree.
  5. A static type checker rejects every program that could suffer a run-time type error.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Soundness and completeness are the two directions of correctness. Sound: nothing accepted is wrong. Complete: nothing right is rejected. They are independent properties, and a static checker that is both sound and complete cannot exist for a Turing-complete language.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is true: a compiler checks types before execution, and a language may also check them at run time, as Lisp and Python do.
  2. (B) is the definition of soundness.
  3. (C) is the definition of completeness.
  4. (D) is the standard meaning of coercion, as when an integer is widened to a real in i + f.
  5. (E) confuses soundness with completeness. A sound checker may reject a program that would in fact never fail, so it need not reject exactly the unsafe ones — and it cannot: deciding whether an arbitrary program raises a type error is undecidable.

Final answer. Options (A), (B), (C) and (D).

Common misconception. Using “sound” and “complete” interchangeably. A sound checker errs on the safe side by rejecting too much; a complete one errs by accepting everything that is actually safe. Real static checkers are sound but deliberately incomplete.

CD-U4-Q24NATeasy

In type-expression notation a two-dimensional array is written array(2, array(3, integer)). How many type constructors — occurrences of array, →, × or pointer — appear in the type expression array(2, array(3, integer → integer))? (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A type expression is a tree whose leaves are basic types (integer, char, boolean, real) and whose interior nodes are type constructors. Counting constructors is counting interior nodes.

Step by step.

  1. Outermost: array(2, …) — one constructor.
  2. Inside it: array(3, …) — second constructor.
  3. Innermost: integer → integer — third constructor.
  4. The two occurrences of integer are basic types, not constructors.

Final answer. 3 type constructors.

Common misconception. Counting the basic types as well and answering 5, or counting the array bound 2 or 3 as part of the type. The bounds are attributes of the constructor node, not constructors themselves.

CD-U4-Q25MCQhard

A sound static type checker:

  1. accepts every program that cannot raise a run-time type error.
  2. accepts exactly those programs that cannot raise a run-time type error.
  3. rejects exactly those programs that can raise a run-time type error.
  4. never accepts a program that can raise a run-time type error, but may reject some programs that cannot.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Soundness is a one-sided guarantee: whatever the checker accepts is safe. Completeness is the other side: whatever is safe is accepted. A sound checker may be conservative and reject safe programs; it may not accept unsafe ones.

Step by step.

  1. (A) states completeness, not soundness.
  2. (B) and (C) each state both directions at once, that is soundness and completeness. For a language with arbitrary computation such a checker cannot exist, since deciding whether a program raises a type error reduces to the halting problem.
  3. (D) states exactly soundness, with the incompleteness made explicit. A typical witness: if (false) x = 1 / "a"; is rejected by a sound checker although it never executes.

Final answer. Option (D).

Common misconception. Expecting a good type checker to accept everything that would actually run safely. Real checkers are sound but incomplete on purpose: rejecting a safe program is a nuisance, accepting an unsafe one breaks the guarantee the type system exists to give.

CD-U4-Q45MCQeasy

Which one of the following checks must be performed at run time rather than at compile time?

  1. That both operands of + have compatible types.
  2. That every variable is declared before it is used.
  3. That an array subscript lies within the declared bounds.
  4. That a function is called with the number of arguments given in its declaration.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A static check is decidable from the program text alone; a dynamic check depends on values that exist only while the program runs.

Step by step.

  1. Operand types are known from the declarations, so compatibility is a static check.
  2. Declaration before use is a scope rule enforced on the symbol table, again static.
  3. A subscript is usually a computed value; whether it lies within bounds is not known until execution, so the check is dynamic — unless the language forces static bounds.
  4. Arity is fixed by the declaration and is static.

Final answer. Option (C).

Common misconception. Calling every semantic check static. Type checking is static only when types are declared or inferable before execution.

CD-U4-Q46MCQmedium

In type-expression notation, the type of an array of ten pointers to integers is:

  1. array(10, pointer(integer))
  2. pointer(array(10, integer))
  3. array(10, integer) → pointer(integer)
  4. pointer(integer) × 10
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A type expression is built with constructors: array(n, T) for n consecutive objects of type T, pointer(T) for a pointer to T, T₁ × T₂ for a product and T₁ → T₂ for a function.

Step by step.

  1. The outermost object is the array of ten elements, so array(10, ·) is the outer constructor.
  2. Each element is a pointer to an integer, so the inner type is pointer(integer).
  3. Composing gives array(10, pointer(integer)).
  4. Option (B) is the type of a single pointer to an array of ten integers — the constructors are swapped.

Final answer. Option (A).

Common misconception. Reading "array of pointers" and "pointer to array" as the same type. Their type expressions differ in the order of the constructors, and so does their size.

CD-U4-Q47MSQmedium

Soundness, completeness and coercion decide what a type checker can promise. Which of the following statements are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. A sound type checker rejects every ill-typed program.
  2. A complete type checker accepts every well-typed program.
  3. A type checker that is sound is necessarily also complete.
  4. A coercion is an implicit conversion inserted so that an operation which would otherwise be ill-typed becomes well-typed.
  5. A language with no static types must still have its type errors detected at compile time.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Soundness means "accepts only well-typed programs"; completeness means "accepts all well-typed programs". They are independent properties, and real checkers usually trade completeness for decidability.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is the definition of soundness.
  2. (B) is the definition of completeness.
  3. (C) is false. A checker that rejects every program is sound but hopelessly incomplete.
  4. (D) is true; a coercion such as widening an integer to a real keeps the program well-typed without an explicit cast.
  5. (E) is false. Without static types, type errors can only be detected dynamically, at run time.

Final answer. Options (A), (B) and (D).

Common misconception. Using "sound" and "complete" interchangeably. A compiler that accepts everything is complete but unsound; one that rejects everything is sound but incomplete.

CD-U4-Q48NATmedium

In type-expression notation, how many type constructors — occurrences of array, pointer, × or → — appear in the type of a function that takes an integer and returns a pointer to a function from an integer to an integer, written integer → pointer(integer → integer)? (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A type constructor is a symbol that builds a type from other types; a basic type such as integer is not a constructor.

Step by step.

  1. The outermost → makes the whole thing a function type: 1.
  2. pointer(·) applied to the result type: 2.
  3. The inner → inside the pointer, for the function from integer to integer: 3.
  4. The three occurrences of integer are basic types, not constructors, so they are not counted.

Final answer. 3.

Common misconception. Counting the basic types as well and answering 6, or missing the inner arrow because it sits inside a pointer.

CD-U4-Q49MCQeasy

Resolving an overloaded operator or function name to a particular definition is an example of:

  1. lexical analysis.
  2. syntax analysis.
  3. static semantic analysis.
  4. machine-independent code optimization.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Overload resolution needs the types of the operands, which are recorded in the symbol table during semantic analysis; it is therefore not a syntactic decision.

Step by step.

  1. The scanner returns the same token for + whatever its operand types, so this is not lexical.
  2. The parser accepts the same phrase structure for both interpretations, so this is not syntactic either.
  3. Semantic analysis looks up the declared types and picks the definition, then annotates the tree with it.
  4. Optimization works on the already-typed intermediate code.

Final answer. Option (C).

Common misconception. Treating overloading as a parsing problem because it looks like an ambiguity. It is resolved by type information, not by grammar.

CD-U4-Q50MSQeasy

Which of the following are checks a compiler can make at compile time? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Every variable is declared before it is used.
  2. No division by zero is ever performed.
  3. The type of the left side of an assignment is compatible with the type of the right side.
  4. A function is called with the number of arguments its declaration specifies.
  5. An array subscript is always within the declared bounds.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A static check must be decidable from the source text; anything that depends on run-time values is a dynamic check.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is a scope rule, decided from the symbol table.
  2. (B) depends on values of divisors, so it is undecidable in general and, at best, approximated by data-flow analysis.
  3. (C) is ordinary type checking over declared types.
  4. (D) is fixed by the declaration, so arity is checked statically.
  5. (E) depends on the computed subscript; it is a run-time check unless the language restricts subscripts to constants.

Final answer. Options (A), (C) and (D).

Common misconception. Assuming every semantic error can be caught statically. Division by zero and out-of-bounds subscripts are the standard examples that cannot.

Review the 50 solved questions from Unit 5 — Runtime Environments & Code Optimization
Activation records & the call stack · 12
CD-U5-Q01MCQeasy

Which one of the following is not normally stored in the activation record of a procedure?

  1. The actual parameters passed to the procedure.
  2. The procedure's local variables and compiler-generated temporaries.
  3. The return address and the saved machine state of the caller.
  4. The symbol table of the entire program.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. An activation record holds exactly the information needed for one activation of one procedure: parameters, links, saved state, locals and temporaries.

Step by step.

  1. The activation record is created when the procedure is entered and destroyed when it returns.
  2. It must hold the incoming parameters, the links to other records, the saved registers and return address, the locals and the temporaries.
  3. The symbol table belongs to the compilation, not to a run-time activation; it is consulted by the compiler and is not part of any single frame.
  4. Hence the whole-program symbol table is the item that is not stored per activation.

Final answer. Option (D)

Common misconception. Confusing the compile-time symbol table with the run-time activation record. Only the entries the compiler decides are needed at run time end up in the frame.

CD-U5-Q02MCQmedium

In a language that allows nested procedures, the access link (static link) of the activation record of procedure P points to

  1. the activation record of whoever called P
  2. the activation record of the most recent activation of the procedure that lexically encloses P
  3. the activation record of the main program, always
  4. the previous activation record of P itself
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The access link implements lexical (static) scoping: it chains a record to the record of its enclosing procedure, not to its caller.

Step by step.

  1. A non-local name inside P refers to a declaration in the procedure that textually encloses P.
  2. That declaration lives in the most recent activation of the enclosing procedure, which is still on the stack.
  3. The access link therefore points at that record, and a chain of access links reaches every lexically enclosing scope.
  4. The caller's record is reached by the control link, which is a different link with a different purpose.

Final answer. Option (B)

Common misconception. Reading the access link as the caller link. The caller link is the control (dynamic) link; the two coincide only when the call is to a procedure nested directly in the caller.

CD-U5-Q03MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about activation records and the run-time stack are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. The control link (dynamic link) of an activation record points to the activation record of the caller.
  2. The access link and the control link always point to the same record.
  3. When a procedure returns, its activation record is popped and the caller's record becomes current again.
  4. A procedure that is never called recursively does not need an activation record at all.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The control link implements the dynamic call chain; the access link implements lexical nesting; the stack discipline follows the LIFO order of calls.

Step by step.

  1. The control link is defined as the pointer to the caller's record, so option (A) is true.
  2. The access link points to the lexically enclosing procedure's record, which is generally not the caller, so option (B) is false.
  3. Calls nest, so records are pushed and popped in LIFO order; on return the caller's record is current again. Option (C) is true.
  4. Every call — recursive or not — needs a frame to hold parameters, saved state and locals. Option (D) is false.

Final answer. Options (A) and (C)

Common misconception. Assuming a non-recursive procedure can keep its locals in static storage. Only when the compiler can prove a single live activation, and the language forbids recursion or re-entrancy, is that possible.

CD-U5-Q04NATmedium

A procedure's activation record holds 8 bytes for the return address, 16 bytes of incoming parameters, 40 bytes of local scalars, 24 bytes of saved registers and 8 bytes of compiler temporaries. How many bytes does one activation record occupy? (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The frame size is the sum of every field the compiler places in the activation record.

Step by step.

  1. Return address: 8 bytes.
  2. Incoming parameters: 16 bytes.
  3. Local scalars: 40 bytes.
  4. Saved registers: 24 bytes.
  5. Temporaries: 8 bytes.
  6. 8 + 16 + 40 + 24 + 8 = 96 bytes.

Final answer. 96 bytes

Common misconception. Forgetting the saved-register area, or leaving out the temporaries because they do not appear in the source. Both occupy real frame space.

CD-U5-Q05MCQhard

Each activation record of a recursive procedure is 96 bytes and the run-time system reserves a further 64 bytes of fixed overhead at the base of the stack. If the deepest recursion reached during a run is 12, what is the total stack space used?

  1. 1152 bytes
  2. 1216 bytes
  3. 1280 bytes
  4. 1088 bytes
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Stack usage for a recursion depth d is d × frame size plus any fixed base overhead.

Step by step.

  1. Twelve simultaneous activations each occupy 96 bytes: 12 × 96 = 1152 bytes.
  2. The fixed 64-byte base overhead is charged once, not per activation.
  3. Total = 1152 + 64 = 1216 bytes.
  4. 1216 is the only option that adds the overhead exactly once.

Final answer. Option (B) — 1216 bytes

Common misconception. Charging the 64-byte overhead on every frame (giving 12 × 160 = 1920) or forgetting it entirely (1152).

CD-U5-Q06MCQeasy

Why can two simultaneous activations of the same recursive procedure each keep a private copy of a local variable?

  1. Because each activation has its own activation record on the run-time stack.
  2. Because local variables are always allocated in static memory.
  3. Because the compiler gives each call site a different name for the variable.
  4. Because local variables are always kept in registers.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Recursion works because each activation gets a fresh frame, so a local name maps to a different storage location in each activation.

Step by step.

  1. A local variable is an offset within the current activation record.
  2. Each call pushes a new record, so the same offset denotes different storage in different activations.
  3. Static allocation would give one location for all activations and destroy the older value on each recursive call.
  4. Registers cannot hold every local of every activation, and the compiler cannot rename a variable at run time.

Final answer. Option (A)

Common misconception. Thinking the compiler 'renames' variables per call. Renaming is a compile-time idea; what actually separates the copies is the frame pointer.

CD-U5-Q26MCQeasy

In the run-time environment of a language that allows nested procedures, the display is

  1. the set of registers reserved for passing actual parameters
  2. the table of free blocks maintained by the heap manager
  3. a vector whose d-th entry points to the most recent activation of the procedure at nesting depth d
  4. the stack of return addresses saved by the call instruction
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The display is an array indexed by lexical nesting depth. Entry d holds a pointer to the activation record of the most recent activation of the procedure declared at depth d, so a reference to a nonlocal variable costs one array access instead of a walk along the access-link chain.

Step by step.

  1. A display is indexed by nesting depth, not by call order and not by parameter position.
  2. Looking up depth d gives the frame of the current activation of the procedure at that depth, which is exactly the frame that lexically encloses the reference.
  3. Options (A), (B) and (D) describe parameter registers, the heap free list and the return-address stack; none of them is indexed by nesting depth.

Final answer. Option (C) — a depth-indexed vector of frame pointers.

Common misconception. Reading “display” as the hardware frame buffer or as the parameter area. In compiler terminology it is a run-time data structure for nonlocal access.

Takeaway. Access links are per frame; a display is per depth. Both reach the same frames, and the display buys O(1) access at the cost of save and restore on every call.

CD-U5-Q27MCQmedium

Procedure P sits at lexical nesting depth 3 and refers to a variable declared in the procedure at lexical nesting depth 1. With access (static) links and no display, how many links must the code follow from the activation record of P to reach the record that holds that variable?

  1. one link
  2. two links
  3. three links
  4. none: the compiler turns it into a static address
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution
