Concept. An access link points to the activation record of the procedure that lexically encloses the current one. Following one access link moves outward by exactly one lexical level, so the number of links to follow is the difference in nesting depth.

Step by step.

  1. Nesting depth of P = 3; depth of the declaring procedure = 1.
  2. Difference = 3 − 1 = 2, so two links are followed: depth 3 → depth 2 → depth 1.
  3. One link would land on the depth-2 frame, and three links would leave the lexical chain altogether.

Final answer. Option (B) — two links.

Common misconception. Following the control link instead, which walks the call chain and may arrive at a frame that does not lexically enclose P at all.

Takeaway. Access link = one lexical level. Nesting-depth difference = number of hops.

CD-U5-Q28MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about the calling sequence of a procedure are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. The caller evaluates the actual parameters and places them where the callee can find them.
  2. The return address that is saved is the address of the instruction that follows the call in the caller.
  3. The callee saves the registers it is about to overwrite and restores them before it returns.
  4. The caller must decrement the stack pointer by exactly the size of the callee’s activation record before transferring control.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The calling sequence is a division of labour: the caller makes the arguments available and records where to resume, and the callee saves whatever it will clobber, allocates its own frame and initialises its own data.

Step by step.

  1. (A) True. Arguments are evaluated in the caller’s context, because only the caller can see them.
  2. (B) True. Execution must resume at the instruction after the call, so that address is the one saved.
  3. (C) True. The callee knows which registers it will overwrite; saving and restoring them is its job.
  4. (D) False. The caller may only save its own state and push arguments; the callee allocates the rest of its frame, which is why the frame size need not be fixed before the call.

Final answer. Options (A), (B) and (C).

Common misconception. Assuming the caller must know the full frame size. That is why the frame layout is fixed by the compiler, not computed by the caller at run time.

Takeaway. Sort every step of a call by who performs it: caller supplies arguments and return point, callee saves registers, allocates and initialises.

CD-U5-Q29NATeasy

A recursive procedure uses 48 bytes per activation record. During a run the deepest recursion reaches 20 simultaneous activations, and the run-time system reserves 512 bytes of fixed overhead at the base of the stack. The run-time stack is 16384 bytes. How many bytes of the stack remain unused at the deepest point? (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Stack usage is the fixed base overhead plus one frame per live activation; the unused space is the stack size minus that total.

Step by step.

  1. Frames in use at the deepest point = 20, so frame space = 20 × 48 = 960 bytes.
  2. Total in use = 512 + 960 = 1472 bytes.
  3. Unused = 16384 − 1472 = 14912 bytes.
  4. Check: 14912 + 1472 = 16384, which is the whole stack.

Final answer. 14912 bytes.

Common misconception. Counting 19 frames by assuming the outermost call does not need one, or forgetting the 512-byte base overhead. Both errors inflate the answer.

Takeaway. Stack cost = fixed overhead + depth × frame size. Compute the frames in use first, then subtract from the stack size.

CD-U5-Q30MCQeasy

Compilers usually keep a separate frame pointer (FP) as well as a stack pointer (SP). The reason is that

  1. SP moves whenever temporaries are pushed or popped, whereas FP stays fixed for the whole activation, so parameters and locals keep stable offsets
  2. FP can be read faster than SP by the processor
  3. FP holds the base address of the heap segment
  4. the call instruction saves the frame pointer but not the stack pointer
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Locals and parameters are addressed as fixed offsets from a base register. If that base were SP, every push and pop of a temporary would shift every offset, so the compiler would have to track the depth of the expression stack.

Step by step.

  1. A frame pointer is set once, on entry, to a fixed point in the frame and restored on return.
  2. SP is free to move as temporaries are evaluated, and the offsets from FP never change.
  3. That stability is what lets a single offset serve a variable for the whole activation, including inside expressions.

Final answer. Option (A) — FP gives stable offsets while SP moves.

Common misconception. Thinking FP exists for speed. It exists for addressing stability, and an optimising compiler often omits it when no variable-length frame is needed.

Takeaway. FP = fixed base for offsets; SP = moving top of the expression stack.

CD-U5-Q31MCQhard

A nested procedure creates a closure that captures a local variable of the enclosing procedure by reference, and the closure is called after the enclosing procedure has returned. What must the compiler do?

  1. leave the variable in its stack frame and simply never reuse that part of the stack
  2. copy the variable into the closure’s own activation record when the closure is created
  3. keep the variable in a callee-saved register that the closure also uses
  4. allocate the captured variable in the heap, or heap-allocate the whole frame, so that it outlives the call
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A stack frame dies when its procedure returns, so any object that must outlive the call has to live somewhere else. Capturing by reference means the closure and the enclosing code must share one storage location, so the location itself must survive the return.

Step by step.

  1. The lifetime required of the captured variable exceeds the lifetime of the frame, which is the defining reason for heap allocation.
  2. Copying it into the closure (option B) gives the closure a private copy: later writes by the enclosing code, or by a second closure, no longer reach it, so by-reference semantics break.
  3. A register (option C) cannot be shared safely either; the register is reused by later calls.
  4. Leaving the frame unreclaimed (option A) leaks stack space without bound and is not what an implementation can guarantee.

Final answer. Option (D) — the captured variable must live in the heap (or the frame must be heap-allocated).

Common misconception. Treating a closure capture as a copy. Copying is correct for capture by value and wrong for capture by reference; the two semantics are not interchangeable.

Takeaway. Lifetime, not scope, decides storage. Anything that outlives its activation escapes the stack.

Basic blocks & flow graphs · 13
CD-U5-Q07MCQeasy

A basic block is

  1. a maximal sequence of consecutive three-address instructions in which control enters only at the first instruction and leaves only at the last
  2. any sequence of instructions that contains no jump instruction
  3. the body of a loop, together with its test
  4. a single three-address instruction
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A basic block is a maximal straight-line run: one entry point at the top, one exit at the bottom.

Step by step.

  1. Control must not be able to enter the middle of the sequence, so no instruction except the first may be a jump target.
  2. Control must not be able to leave early, so no instruction except the last may be a jump.
  3. The sequence must be maximal: extending it in either direction would break one of those two conditions.
  4. A block may therefore contain many instructions, and it may well contain a jump as its final instruction.

Final answer. Option (A)

Common misconception. Defining a basic block as 'a sequence with no jump'. A block may end with a jump; what it may not have is a jump anywhere but at the end, or a label anywhere but at the start.

CD-U5-Q08MCQeasy

When three-address code is partitioned into basic blocks, which of the following is not a leader?

  1. The first instruction of the code.
  2. Any instruction that is the target of a conditional or unconditional jump.
  3. Any instruction that immediately follows a conditional or unconditional jump.
  4. Any instruction that uses a variable defined earlier in the same block.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Leaders are found by three structural rules only: first instruction, jump targets, and instructions following a jump.

Step by step.

  1. The first instruction is a leader by definition, since control enters the procedure there.
  2. A jump target may receive control from elsewhere, so it must start a block.
  3. The instruction after a jump may be reached by fall-through only, and the jump ends the previous block, so it starts a new one.
  4. Using a variable defined earlier in the same block is ordinary data dependence; it does not let control enter from outside, so it does not create a leader.

Final answer. Option (D)

Common misconception. Confusing data dependences with control-flow boundaries. Only jumps and jump targets split blocks.

CD-U5-Q09NATmedium

Consider the three-address code below, where L1, L2 and L3 are labels.
i = 1
if i > n goto L3
L1: t = 4 * i
a[t] = 0
i = i + 1
if i <= n goto L1
L3: x = 0
goto L5
L5: y = x + 1
How many basic blocks does this code contain? (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Apply the three leader rules, then each block runs from one leader up to the instruction before the next.

Step by step.

  1. Leaders: the first instruction i = 1; the target L3; the target L1; the instruction after goto L5, namely y = x + 1 at L5.
  2. Block 1: i = 1, if i > n goto L3.
  3. Block 2 (L1): t = 4 * i, a[t] = 0, i = i + 1, if i <= n goto L1.
  4. Block 3 (L3): x = 0, goto L5.
  5. Block 4 (L5): y = x + 1. Total 4.

Final answer. 4 basic blocks

Common misconception. Missing the block that begins after goto L5. Any instruction following a jump is a leader even when nothing jumps to it.

CD-U5-Q10MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about the flow graph of a procedure are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Each node of the flow graph is a basic block.
  2. There is an edge from block B1 to block B2 if control can pass from the last instruction of B1 to the first instruction of B2.
  3. The initial node is the block that contains the first instruction of the procedure.
  4. Every block in a flow graph has exactly one predecessor.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The flow graph records the possible transfers of control between basic blocks.

Step by step.

  1. Nodes are basic blocks, by construction. Option (A) is true.
  2. An edge exists exactly when a jump or a fall-through can carry control from one block to the other. Option (B) is true.
  3. Execution begins at the procedure's first instruction, which lies in the initial node. Option (C) is true.
  4. A block that is the target of several jumps has several predecessors, and the initial node has none. Option (D) is false.

Final answer. Options (A), (B) and (C)

Common misconception. Assuming a single predecessor because the source reads top to bottom. Any block with two incoming jumps breaks that assumption.

CD-U5-Q11MCQmedium

An edge (B2 → B1) in a flow graph whose head B1 dominates its tail B2 is called a back edge. What does a back edge identify?

  1. A basic block that is never executed.
  2. A natural loop, with B1 as its header.
  3. A procedure call that has not been inlined.
  4. A block that has two or more predecessors.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Back edges are the structural signature of loops: a back edge plus the nodes that can reach its tail without passing through its head form a natural loop.

Step by step.

  1. B1 dominates B2 means every path from the initial node to B2 passes through B1.
  2. The edge B2 → B1 therefore closes a cycle, and the set of nodes that can reach B2 without going through B1, together with B1, is the natural loop.
  3. B1 is called the header: it dominates every node of the loop and is the only entry point.
  4. Irreducible flow can contain cycles without a back edge in this sense, which is why the definition is stated in terms of dominance.

Final answer. Option (B)

Common misconception. Treating every cycle as a natural loop. Only cycles with a single entry — equivalently, closed by a back edge — are natural loops.

CD-U5-Q12NAThard

Consider the three-address code below, where L1, L3 and L4 are labels.
x = 1
y = 2
if x < y goto L1
x = x + 1
goto L3
L1: y = y * 2
L3: if y > 10 goto L4
z = y
L4: w = z + 1
How many edges does the flow graph of this code contain? (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Partition into basic blocks with the leader rules, then count one edge per possible transfer of control.

Step by step.

  1. Leaders: x = 1; L1; x = x + 1 (follows the conditional jump); L3; z = y (follows the conditional jump at L3); L4.
  2. Blocks: B1 = [x = 1, y = 2, if x < y goto L1]; B2 = [x = x + 1, goto L3]; B3 = [y = y * 2] (L1); B4 = [if y > 10 goto L4] (L3); B5 = [z = y]; B6 = [w = z + 1] (L4).
  3. Edges: B1→B3 (jump taken), B1→B2 (fall-through), B2→B4 (goto L3), B3→B4 (fall-through), B4→B6 (jump taken), B4→B5 (fall-through), B5→B6 (fall-through).
  4. B6 has no successor. Total 7 edges.

Final answer. 7 edges

Common misconception. Forgetting the fall-through edge out of a conditional jump, or merging z = y into B4. An instruction after a jump always starts a new block.

CD-U5-Q13MCQhard

In the flow graph of a while loop, which node dominates every node of the loop body?

  1. The node that contains the loop test — the loop header.
  2. The node that contains the exit from the loop.
  3. The node with the largest number of successors.
  4. Every node of the body dominates the header, not the other way round.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The header of a natural loop dominates every node in the loop, because control can reach the body only through the test.

Step by step.

  1. Control reaches the loop body only after passing the loop test, so every path to a body node goes through the header.
  2. That is exactly the definition of domination, so the header dominates every body node.
  3. The exit node is reached from inside the loop, so it cannot dominate the body.
  4. The reverse claim fails: a body node can be bypassed by leaving the loop early.

Final answer. Option (A)

Common misconception. Choosing the exit block because it is 'after' the loop. Domination is about all paths from entry, and the exit is not on the path into the body.

CD-U5-Q32MCQeasy

In a flow graph with entry node s, node B1 dominates node B2 if

  1. every path from s to B2 passes through B1
  2. at least one path from s to B2 passes through B1
  3. B1 and B2 lie on a common cycle
  4. B1 appears before B2 in the source text
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Dominance is a universal statement about paths: a node is unavoidable on the way to another node.

Step by step.

  1. Dominance asks whether control can reach B2 while avoiding B1. If it cannot, B1 dominates.
  2. “Every path” is therefore the definition; “some path” (option B) is far too weak.
  3. Sharing a cycle (option C) or appearing earlier in the text (option D) says nothing about unavoidability; a branch can bypass either one.

Final answer. Option (A) — every path from the entry to B2 passes through B1.

Common misconception. Confusing dominance with reachability or with textual order. Dominance is about all paths, not one.

Takeaway. Dominance is the foundation of loop detection, of the SSA φ-placement rule and of every “this must have executed” argument.

CD-U5-Q33MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about basic blocks and flow graphs are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Two instructions in the same basic block may be interchanged only if neither uses a value that the other defines.
  2. Control can enter a basic block only at its first instruction.
  3. Every basic block must end with a conditional or unconditional jump.
  4. If a block has two predecessors, then the last instruction of each predecessor must be a jump.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A basic block is a maximal straight-line sequence: one entry, one exit. That structure makes local optimisation possible, but it does not make the instructions freely reorderable, and it does not force every block to end in a jump.

Step by step.

  1. (A) True. Reordering two instructions that carry a dependence changes the values computed; independence is the precondition for any reordering.
  2. (B) True. That is the definition of a block: a leader is the only entry point.
  3. (C) False. A block may end with an ordinary instruction and fall through to the next block, which is a leader because it follows a jump elsewhere.
  4. (D) False. One of the two predecessors may reach the block by falling through, with no jump at all.

Final answer. Options (A) and (B).

Common misconception. Believing that every edge of a flow graph is an explicit jump. A fall-through edge is an edge with no jump instruction behind it.

Takeaway. Straight-line entry and exit, not universal jumps, is what defines a basic block.

CD-U5-Q34MCQmedium

Which of the following statements about reducible flow graphs is TRUE?

  1. Every flow graph produced by a structured program is irreducible.
  2. A flow graph is reducible exactly when it can be reduced to a single node by the T1 and T2 transformations, and then every retreating edge in a depth-first ordering is a back edge.
  3. A flow graph that contains a loop cannot be reducible.
  4. Reducibility guarantees that every block has at most one predecessor.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Reducibility is the property that all loops in the graph are entered through a single header. It has three equivalent characterisations: reducibility by the T1 and T2 transformations, every retreating edge being a back edge, and every cycle having a node that dominates the rest of the cycle.

Step by step.

  1. T1 removes a self-loop and T2 collapses a node with a single predecessor; a graph is reducible when repeated application reduces it to one node.
  2. Under that condition every retreating edge in a depth-first ordering turns out to be a back edge, which is the second equivalent form.
  3. Structured code (if, while, for) always produces reducible graphs, so option (A) reverses the truth, and having a loop (option C) is exactly what reducibility talks about.
  4. Reducibility says nothing about the in-degree of a block, so option (D) is unrelated.

Final answer. Option (B).

Common misconception. Reading “reducible” as “can be simplified at all”. Irreducible graphs exist — two entries into one loop body — and they are what makes loop analysis hard.

Takeaway. One header per loop is the practical content of reducibility; goto-based spaghetti is what breaks it.

CD-U5-Q35NATmedium

Consider the three-address code below, where L1, L2, L3 and L4 are labels.
i = 0
L1: if i >= 10 goto L4
t = a[i]
if t == 0 goto L3
s = s + t
goto L2
L2: i = i + 1
goto L1
L3: c = c + 1
L4: d = s + c
How many basic blocks does this code contain? (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A basic block starts at a leader. The leaders are the first instruction, every label that is the target of a jump, and every instruction that immediately follows a jump. Everything between one leader and the next belongs to the same block.

Step by step.

  1. Leader 1: i = 0, the first instruction.
  2. Leader 2: L1: if i >= 10 goto L4, a jump target.
  3. Leader 3: t = a[i], which immediately follows the conditional jump at L1.
  4. The line if t == 0 goto L3 follows an ordinary assignment, not a jump, so it is not a leader: it stays inside the block that began at t = a[i].
  5. Leader 4: s = s + t, which immediately follows the conditional jump.
  6. Leader 5: L2: i = i + 1, a jump target and also the line after goto L2.
  7. Leader 6: L3: c = c + 1, a jump target.
  8. Leader 7: L4: d = s + c, a jump target.
  9. The blocks are therefore [i = 0], [L1: if i >= 10 goto L4], [t = a[i]; if t == 0 goto L3], [s = s + t; goto L2], [L2: i = i + 1; goto L1], [L3: c = c + 1] and [L4: d = s + c] — 7 blocks.

Final answer. 7 basic blocks.

Common misconception. Counting the conditional test if t == 0 goto L3 as the start of a block, which gives 8. A conditional jump ends a block; only the instruction after it begins one.

Takeaway. Collect leaders first, then split. Every leader starts a block, and a jump ends a block.

CD-U5-Q36MCQmedium

The immediate dominator of a node n (other than the entry node) is

  1. the predecessor of n that has the largest number of successors
  2. the node that n dominates directly
  3. the dominator of n that every other dominator of n dominates
  4. the header of the outermost loop that contains n
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The dominators of a node form a chain ordered by dominance, so exactly one member of that chain is the closest to the node. That one is the immediate dominator.

Step by step.

  1. If d dominates n, every dominator of d also dominates n, so the dominators of n are totally ordered by dominance.
  2. The last element of that chain, dominated by all the others, is the immediate dominator.
  3. Option (B) reverses the relation: it is n that dominates its own successors, not its dominator.
  4. Options (A) and (D) describe a predecessor and a loop header, neither of which is defined by the dominance chain.

Final answer. Option (C).

Common misconception. Confusing “dominates” with “is dominated by”. The immediate dominator is above n in the chain, not below it.

Takeaway. Dominators form a tree; the immediate dominator is the parent. Every control-flow question about “the nearest unavoidable block” is a question about that tree.

CD-U5-Q37MCQhard

A while loop whose body contains a break statement is compiled to a flow graph. Compared with the flow graph of the same loop without the break,

  1. one extra edge is added from the block containing the break to the block that follows the loop, and that edge is not a back edge
  2. the loop header acquires a second back edge
  3. the flow graph becomes irreducible
  4. the block containing the break becomes the new loop header
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A break is an additional exit from the middle of the loop body. In the flow graph it is one more edge from the break block to the loop’s successor; the loop still has exactly one entry, so the graph stays reducible and the header is unchanged.

Step by step.

  1. The loop header remains the block with the test, and the only back edge is still the one from the end of the body to the header.
  2. The break block gains an edge straight to the block after the loop, which is a forward edge, not a back edge.
  3. One entry and two exits is still reducible; irreducibility needs two entries into one loop, which a break does not create.
  4. Nothing promotes the break block to header: the test block still dominates the whole body.

Final answer. Option (A).

Common misconception. Assuming any second exit from a loop body makes the graph irreducible. Irreducibility is about entries, not exits.

Takeaway. When you add a construct, ask which node gains an edge and in which direction; that answer settles most flow-graph questions.

Local & loop optimizations · 13
CD-U5-Q14MCQeasy

Detecting that t1 = b + c and t2 = b + c inside one basic block compute the same value, and rewriting the second to use t1, is called

  1. common subexpression elimination
  2. dead code elimination
  3. loop unrolling
  4. constant folding
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Local common subexpression elimination reuses a value already computed earlier in the same block.

Step by step.

  1. The two right-hand sides are identical and neither operand has been redefined in between.
  2. The second computation is therefore redundant and can be replaced by a copy from the first result.
  3. This is common subexpression elimination, performed locally because both occurrences lie in one block.
  4. The other three transformations remove unused code, replicate a loop body, or evaluate constants — none applies here.

Final answer. Option (A)

Common misconception. Calling it copy propagation. Copy propagation moves an assignment x = y into later uses; here a whole recomputation is removed.

CD-U5-Q15MCQeasy

An instruction whose result is never used and which has no side effect is removed by

  1. dead code elimination
  2. strength reduction
  3. code motion
  4. induction variable elimination
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Dead code elimination deletes computations whose results cannot affect the program's output.

Step by step.

  1. A definition is dead when the variable it defines is not live after it.
  2. With no side effect, removing it cannot change any observable behaviour.
  3. That is precisely dead code elimination.
  4. Strength reduction, code motion and induction variable elimination all keep the computation but change its form or position.

Final answer. Option (A)

Common misconception. Deleting a call or a store because 'the result is unused'. Calls and stores have side effects, so they are not dead merely because a value is discarded.

CD-U5-Q16NATeasy

After constant propagation and constant folding, what integer value is assigned by x = 3 * (4 + 5) - 2 * 6? (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Constant folding evaluates at compile time any expression whose operands are all constants.

Step by step.

  1. Fold the parenthesised sum: 4 + 5 = 9.
  2. Fold the first product: 3 × 9 = 27.
  3. Fold the second product: 2 × 6 = 12.
  4. Subtract: 27 − 12 = 15.

Final answer. 15

Common misconception. Applying the multiplication before the parentheses (giving 3×4+5 = 17). Parentheses bind first; only then do × and ÷.

CD-U5-Q17MCQmedium

Which one of the following transformations replaces an expensive operation inside a loop by a cheaper equivalent one?

  1. constant folding
  2. strength reduction
  3. unreachable code elimination
  4. common subexpression elimination
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Strength reduction substitutes a cheaper operation — typically addition for multiplication, or a shift for a power-of-two multiply — while preserving the value.

Step by step.

  1. Constant folding evaluates expressions whose operands are known at compile time; nothing expensive is being replaced at run time.
  2. Strength reduction keeps the same value but computes it with a cheaper operation, for example maintaining t = t + 8 instead of recomputing 8 * i each iteration.
  3. Unreachable code elimination removes code that can never execute; it is not a replacement.
  4. Common subexpression elimination removes a redundant recomputation rather than making an operation cheaper.

Final answer. Option (B)

Common misconception. Confusing strength reduction with constant folding. Folding needs compile-time constants; strength reduction works on run-time values.

CD-U5-Q18MSQmedium

Which of the following are loop optimizations? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Loop-invariant code motion.
  2. Induction variable elimination.
  3. Loop unrolling.
  4. Register allocation by graph colouring.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Loop optimizations exploit the repeated execution of a loop body: hoisting invariants, simplifying induction variables, or trading code size for fewer branch and index operations.

Step by step.

  1. Loop-invariant code motion moves a computation that does not change inside the loop to just before it. Option (A) is a loop optimization.
  2. Induction variable elimination replaces a variable that steps by a constant with a simpler recurrence or removes it entirely. Option (B) is a loop optimization.
  3. Loop unrolling replicates the body to cut the loop-control overhead. Option (C) is a loop optimization.
  4. Graph colouring is a global register-allocation technique; it applies to the whole procedure and is not specific to loops. Option (D) is not.

Final answer. Options (A), (B) and (C)

Common misconception. Treating every optimization that happens to help a loop as a loop optimization. Graph colouring and dead code elimination are global or local, not loop-specific.

CD-U5-Q19MCQhard

In the loop while (i < n) { s = s + a * b; i = i + 1; }, where a and b are not modified anywhere in the loop, which statement is TRUE?

  1. a * b is loop-invariant and may be computed once before the loop, provided the body is known to execute at least once or the computation has no side effect.
  2. a * b cannot be moved out of the loop, because it appears inside a loop.
  3. Hoisting a * b changes the number of times s is updated.
  4. i = i + 1 is loop-invariant and can be hoisted out of the loop.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Loop-invariant code motion hoists a computation whose operands do not change in the loop, subject to the usual safety conditions.

Step by step.

  1. a and b are not assigned in the loop, so a * b has the same value on every iteration — it is loop-invariant.
  2. Computing it once before the loop removes repeated work and cannot change the value of s.
  3. The one caveat is that hoisting executes the computation even when the loop body runs zero times; that is safe for a pure multiplication but not for an operation that may trap or has side effects.
  4. i = i + 1 changes every iteration, so it is the opposite of invariant.

Final answer. Option (A)

Common misconception. Hoisting an invariant division or a dereference without checking. Hoisting can introduce a fault on a path that never executed the body.

CD-U5-Q38MCQeasy

After an assignment x = y, replacing later uses of y by x inside the same basic block is called

  1. constant folding
  2. dead code elimination
  3. loop unrolling
  4. copy propagation
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Copy propagation takes an assignment of the form x = y and rewrites the uses of y that follow it into uses of x, so the copy itself can often be deleted.

Step by step.

  1. The assignment establishes that x and y hold the same value from that point on.
  2. Every later use of y in the block can therefore read x instead, provided neither variable is redefined in between.
  3. Once no use of y remains that needs the copy, the assignment itself becomes dead and is removed.
  4. Constant folding, dead code elimination and loop unrolling are different transformations with different preconditions.

Final answer. Option (D) — copy propagation.

Common misconception. Calling it common subexpression elimination. CSE removes a repeated computation; copy propagation removes a repeated value transfer.

Takeaway. Copy propagation usually works with dead code elimination: propagate first, then delete the copy that nothing reads.

CD-U5-Q39MCQeasy

Replicating the body of a loop k times inside one iteration, with the loop control adjusted to run n/k iterations, is called

  1. loop fusion
  2. loop unrolling
  3. loop distribution
  4. loop inversion
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Loop unrolling replicates the body so that one iteration performs k copies of the work and the branch and induction-variable update are amortised over those copies.

Step by step.

  1. The body is copied k times and the loop bound is divided by k.
  2. Branch overhead per unit of work falls, and the scheduler gets a larger straight-line window.
  3. Fusion merges two loops, distribution splits one loop into several, and inversion turns a while-loop into a do-while with a guard — none of them replicates a body.

Final answer. Option (B) — loop unrolling.

Common misconception. Confusing unrolling with fusion. Unrolling copies the body of one loop; fusion joins two separate loops.

Takeaway. Unrolling trades code size for fewer branches and a bigger scheduling window.

CD-U5-Q40NATeasy

In the loop
for (i = 0; i < 100; i = i + 1) a[4 * i] = 0;
strength reduction replaces the multiplication by an induction variable j that starts at 0 and is increased by 4 on every iteration. What is the value of j in the last iteration in which the loop body executes? (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Strength reduction turns the multiplication into an addition by carrying the value 4·i in its own induction variable, which starts at the value for i = 0 and is incremented by the same amount the multiplication would have produced each time.

Step by step.

  1. The body executes for i = 0, 1, 2, …, 99, so there are 100 iterations.
  2. j takes the values 0, 4, 8, …, one per iteration, so on the k-th iteration (counting from 0) it holds 4k.
  3. The last iteration is k = 99, giving 4 × 99 = 396.
  4. Check: 4 × 99 = 396 and 396 + 4 = 400 = 4 × 100, which is the value the loop would have computed if it had run one more time.

Final answer. 396.

Common misconception. Reporting 400 by using the loop bound 100 instead of the last index 99. The body never executes for i = 100.

Takeaway. An induction variable after strength reduction holds exactly what the original expression held; read its final value off the last iteration, not off the bound.

CD-U5-Q41MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about local and loop optimizations are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Constant folding replaces an expression whose operands are all known at compile time by its value.
  2. Two identical expressions in different basic blocks can always be merged into one computation.
  3. An assignment x = e can be deleted when x is not live after the assignment and evaluating e has no side effect.
  4. A loop-invariant expression that may raise an exception can be moved out of a loop whose body may execute zero times.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Every transformation needs a precondition. Constant folding needs compile-time constants; dead-code elimination needs the target not to be live and the right-hand side to be free of side effects; code motion needs the moved computation to be safe wherever it lands.

Step by step.

  1. (A) True. If all operands are constants, the value is fixed and can be computed once by the compiler.
  2. (B) False. A redefinition of an operand between the two blocks destroys the equality; only available expressions justify the merge.
  3. (C) True. If the value is never used again and evaluating the right-hand side cannot trap or change state, the assignment has no observable effect.
  4. (D) False. Hoisting a potentially trapping computation out of a loop that might execute zero times can introduce an exception the original program never raised, which changes behaviour.

Final answer. Options (A) and (C).

Common misconception. Assuming that an unused assignment is always removable. The right-hand side can still divide by zero, call a function or write to memory.

Takeaway. State the precondition with the transformation. Unconditional versions of these rules are the classic source of “optimisations” that change program behaviour.

CD-U5-Q42MCQmedium

To eliminate, in block B3, a computation of b + c that was already performed on every path reaching B3 from block B1, the compiler needs the data-flow information called

  1. live variables
  2. very busy expressions
  3. available expressions
  4. reaching definitions
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Global common subexpression elimination asks whether an expression is available at a point: whether it has been computed on every path to that point and no operand has been redefined since.

Step by step.

  1. The key words are “already performed on every path”, which is a must condition along all paths — exactly the meet of an available-expressions analysis.
  2. Available expressions is a forward, must analysis whose universe is the set of expressions in the procedure.
  3. Live variables is backward and about values still needed; very busy expressions is backward and about computations that will be needed; reaching definitions tracks definitions, which is a may analysis and too weak for this “every path” guarantee.

Final answer. Option (C) — available expressions.

Common misconception. Choosing reaching definitions because it also mentions “definitions”. Reaching definitions is a may analysis, so a single path is enough to satisfy it, and that is not what CSE needs.

Takeaway. Every “on every path” phrase is a must analysis; every “on some path” phrase is a may analysis.

CD-U5-Q43MCQhard

A loop with a large body is unrolled by a factor of 8. Which of the following is TRUE?

  1. It always reduces the running time, because fewer branches are executed.
  2. It removes the loop-carried dependences in the body.
  3. It is legal only when the trip count is an exact multiple of 8.
  4. It reduces loop overhead but increases code size and register pressure, so it can make the program slower through instruction-cache misses and spills.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Unrolling is a space-for-time transformation. It removes branches and induction-variable updates per unit of work, but the code grows by the unroll factor and the scheduler needs more registers to keep the widened body busy.

Step by step.

  1. Per-iteration overhead (branch, compare, increment) is divided by 8, which is the gain.
  2. The code footprint is multiplied by 8 in that loop, which competes for instruction cache and may exceed it, so the gain can be more than cancelled.
  3. A larger straight-line body also raises register pressure; the compiler may spill, adding memory traffic.
  4. Dependences are a property of the computation, not of how many copies of it are written out, and a trip count that is not a multiple of 8 is handled by a residual loop, not by rejecting the transformation.

Final answer. Option (D).

Common misconception. Assuming fewer branches must mean a faster loop. Branch count is one cost among several, and code size is one of the others.

Takeaway. Every loop transformation is a trade: name the resource it spends and the resource it saves.

CD-U5-Q44MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about constant propagation are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. It replaces a use of a variable by a known constant when no intervening definition changes the variable.
  2. Finding those constants across basic blocks needs a forward, must data-flow analysis.
  3. It can turn a conditional branch whose test has become constant into an unconditional jump.
  4. It may be applied across a procedure call even when the call might assign to the variable.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Constant propagation carries the fact “this variable has this constant value here” through the flow graph. The fact is true on every path or it is not usable, which is why the analysis is forward and must.

Step by step.

  1. (A) True. That is the transformation itself: substitute the known value at the use.
  2. (B) True. The constant must hold on every path reaching the point, so the meet is intersection and the direction is forward along the flow graph.
  3. (C) True. Once the test is constant, one arm is unreachable and the branch becomes a jump; this is where constant propagation feeds unreachable-code elimination.
  4. (D) False. A call may assign to a global or to a variable it can reach through a pointer, so the constant is no longer guaranteed after the call.

Final answer. Options (A), (B) and (C).

Common misconception. Assuming a variable keeps its constant across a call. Calls are the standard kill point for propagated facts.

Takeaway. Constant propagation and constant folding are partners: propagation exposes constants at the use, folding computes with them.

Data-flow analysis & liveness · 12
CD-U5-Q20MCQeasy

A variable is live at a point in a program if

  1. it appears somewhere in the program text
  2. its value may be used before being redefined along some path starting at that point
  3. it is declared at the top of the procedure
  4. it is currently held in a register
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Liveness is about future use: a variable is live where its current value may still be read before being overwritten.

Step by step.

  1. The definition quantifies over paths that start at the point in question.
  2. Along at least one such path the current value reaches a use before any redefinition, so the value must be preserved.
  3. If every path redefines the variable before any use, the current value is dead there.
  4. Mentioning a name in the source, declaring it, or holding it in a register says nothing about whether the current value will be read.

Final answer. Option (B)

Common misconception. Defining liveness as 'appears in the program'. A variable can appear many times and still be dead at a given point because it is overwritten first.

CD-U5-Q21MCQeasy

Live variable analysis is a ____ data-flow problem.

  1. forward, must
  2. forward, may
  3. backward, may
  4. backward, must
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Liveness flows information from later points to earlier ones, and a variable counts as live if it is live along any one path.

Step by step.

  1. Whether a variable is live at a point depends on what happens after that point, so the equations are solved backwards from the exit.
  2. The transfer is live_in = use ∪ (live_out − def), and the meet over predecessors' successors is set union.
  3. Union is a may-operator: live on some path is enough.
  4. Hence backward, may.

Final answer. Option (C)

Common misconception. Calling it must because it is backward. Reaching definitions is forward/may and available expressions is forward/must; liveness is backward/may.

CD-U5-Q22NATeasy

Consider the block below. Assume that z is live on exit from the block and that no other variable is.
1: x = 1
2: y = x + 2
3: x = 3
4: z = y
How many variables are live immediately before instruction 3? (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Work backwards: a variable is live before a statement if it is used by that statement, or live after it and not defined by it.

Step by step.

  1. z is live on exit and is defined at instruction 4, so before instruction 4 the live set is {y} — y is used there.
  2. Instruction 3 defines x. The value of x from instruction 1 is not used at instruction 3 or 4, so x is not live after instruction 2 either.
  3. Before instruction 3 the live set is therefore {y}: y will be read at instruction 4, and nothing kills it at instruction 3.
  4. x is not in the set, because instruction 3 overwrites it before any use. Count = 1.

Final answer. 1

Common misconception. Answering 2 by keeping x live. A variable whose next action on every path is a redefinition is dead, whatever its earlier uses were.

CD-U5-Q23MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about classical data-flow analyses are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Reaching definitions is a forward, may analysis.
  2. Live variable analysis is a backward, may analysis.
  3. Available expressions is a forward, must analysis.
  4. Very busy expressions is a forward, must analysis.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Each classical analysis is fixed by two choices: the direction information flows, and whether the meet is union (may) or intersection (must).

Step by step.

  1. Reaching definitions asks which definitions may reach a point, propagating forwards and taking union. Option (A) is true.
  2. Liveness propagates backwards and takes union. Option (B) is true.
  3. Available expressions asks which expressions are computed on every path, propagating forwards and intersecting. Option (C) is true.
  4. Very busy expressions asks which expressions will be computed on every future path — it propagates backwards, and the meet is intersection. Option (D) has the wrong direction.

Final answer. Options (A), (B) and (C)

Common misconception. Assuming 'must' always pairs with 'forward'. Very busy expressions is backward and must; the two choices are independent.

CD-U5-Q24MSQmedium

For which of the following is liveness information used? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Register allocation, since variables that are simultaneously live cannot share a register.
  2. Dead code elimination, since an assignment to a variable that is not live afterwards can be removed.
  3. Directly identifying the loop-invariant computations of a loop.
  4. Propagating information backwards through the flow graph, since liveness on exit from a block is needed to compute liveness on entry.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Liveness answers 'is this value still needed?', which drives both register pressure and the removal of useless definitions.

Step by step.

  1. Two variables live at the same point interfere, so they need different registers; this is the basis of the interference graph. Option (A) is a use.
  2. An assignment whose target is not live afterwards computes a value nobody reads, so it is dead. Option (B) is a use.
  3. Loop-invariance is a property of the operands' definitions relative to the loop, not of liveness. Option (C) is not a use.
  4. Liveness is itself defined by a backward system of equations in which a block's live-out feeds its live-in. Option (D) is a use.

Final answer. Options (A), (B) and (D)

Common misconception. Expecting liveness to find loop invariants. Invariance is decided by asking whether a definition lies outside the loop; liveness says nothing about where a definition is.

CD-U5-Q25MCQhard

Assume that only t is live on exit from the block below.
1: p = q * r
2: p = p + 1
3: t = p
4: p = 0
Which of the following is TRUE?

  1. Line 4 is dead and can be deleted; lines 1, 2 and 3 must all be kept.
  2. Lines 2 and 4 are dead and can be deleted.
  3. Line 1 is dead, because p is redefined at line 2.
  4. No statement is dead, because every line assigns something.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A definition is dead when the variable it defines is not live afterwards; here only t is live on exit.

Step by step.

  1. p is not live on exit and nothing after line 4 reads it, so line 4 defines a value nobody uses. It is dead.
  2. Line 1 defines p, which line 2 reads on its right-hand side, so line 1 is live.
  3. Line 2 produces the p that line 3 reads, so line 2 is live; line 3 produces t, which is live on exit.
  4. Deleting line 1 would change the value of t, so option (C) is wrong despite the redefinition at line 2.

Final answer. Option (A)

Common misconception. Deleting line 1 because p is reassigned at line 2. The reassignment *reads* p, so the earlier value is used.

CD-U5-Q45MCQeasy

In live-variable analysis, the set of variables live on exit from a basic block is

  1. the intersection of the variables live on entry to its successors
  2. the union of the variables live on entry to its successors
  3. the variables both used and defined in the block
  4. the variables that the block defines and never uses again
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Liveness is a may property: a variable is live if some continuation of the execution uses it before redefining it. Because a block can be followed by any one of its successors, the exit set is the union over those successors.

Step by step.

  1. The block can transfer control to any successor, so a variable that is live on entry to at least one successor is live on exit from this block.
  2. “At least one” is union, and it is what makes liveness a may analysis.
  3. An intersection would demand the variable be live on every successor, which is the must version and is not what liveness asks.

Final answer. Option (B) — the union over successors.

Common misconception. Using intersection because liveness feels like a “must” property. It is existential: one path that uses the value is enough.

Takeaway. Backward + may = union over successors. Write the direction and the meet operator down before touching the equations.

CD-U5-Q46NATeasy

Assume that only z is live on exit from the block below.
1: x = a + b
2: y = c * d
3: x = y + 1
4: z = x * 2
5: w = y
How many of the five assignments are dead, that is, their result is never used? (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. An assignment is dead when the variable it defines is not live immediately after it. Liveness is computed backwards from the exit set, so the block is scanned from line 5 up to line 1.

Step by step.

  1. Live after line 5 = {z}. Line 5 defines w, which is not live after it, so line 5 is dead.
  2. Live after line 4 = {z}; line 4 defines z, which is live, so line 4 stays.
  3. Live before line 4 = {x, z}; line 3 defines x, which is live, so line 3 stays.
  4. Live before line 3 = {y}; line 2 defines y, which is live, so line 2 stays.
  5. Live before line 2 = {c, d}; line 1 defines x, which is not live after line 1 (line 3 redefines x before any use), so line 1 is dead.
  6. Dead assignments: lines 1 and 5, that is 2.

Final answer. 2 dead assignments (lines 1 and 5).

Common misconception. Keeping line 1 because x is used at line 3. The use at line 3 reads the value defined at line 3’s own right-hand side, not the value from line 1.

Takeaway. Walk backwards and keep the live set in hand. An assignment dies the moment its target is not in the live set just after it.

CD-U5-Q47MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about the iterative data-flow framework are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. The meet operator of a must analysis is set intersection.
  2. Monotone transfer functions are what guarantee that the iterative algorithm reaches a fixed point.
  3. A forward analysis converges fastest when the blocks are visited in reverse postorder.
  4. A taller lattice cannot change the number of iterations the algorithm needs.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The iterative framework rests on three facts: a lattice with a meet operator, monotone transfer functions, and an iteration order. Monotonicity gives termination; the order only affects speed.

Step by step.

  1. (A) True. A must property holds only if it holds on every path, and every path means intersection.
  2. (B) True. Monotonicity ensures the values only move one way along the lattice, so the process cannot oscillate and must stop at a fixed point.
  3. (C) True. Reverse postorder visits a block after most of its predecessors, so information propagates far in one pass and fewer passes are needed.
  4. (D) False. The height of the lattice bounds the number of times a value can change, so a taller lattice can require more iterations.

Final answer. Options (A), (B) and (C).

Common misconception. Believing the iteration order affects the answer. It affects only the number of passes; the fixed point reached is the same.

Takeaway. Monotonicity gives correctness and termination; the visiting order gives speed. Keep the two arguments separate.

CD-U5-Q48MCQeasy

For live-variable analysis, the gen and kill sets of a basic block are

  1. gen = the variables used in the block before any redefinition in it; kill = the variables defined in the block
  2. gen = the variables defined in the block; kill = the variables used in the block
  3. gen = every variable that appears in the block; kill = the empty set
  4. gen = the empty set; kill = every variable that appears in the block
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The transfer function of a block for liveness is live_in = use ∪ (live_out − def). The use set — uses reached before any definition in the block — is what the block generates, and the definitions are what it kills.

Step by step.

  1. A variable used before it is redefined in the block is live on entry whatever the exit set says, so it is generated.
  2. A variable defined in the block is killed: an earlier value of it cannot be needed beyond the block, because the definition overwrites it.
  3. Options (B), (C) and (D) get the two roles backwards or throw the distinction away.

Final answer. Option (A).

Common misconception. Generating every variable that appears in the block. A use that comes after a definition in the same block reads the new value, so it does not make the old value live.

Takeaway. gen = used before defined; kill = defined. That pair is the whole transfer function for liveness.

CD-U5-Q49NAThard

Consider the code below, where L1 and L4 are labels.
1: i = 0
2: s = 0
L1: 3: if i >= n goto L4
4: s = s + i
5: u = s * 2
6: i = i + 1
7: u = 0
8: goto L1
L4: 9: r = s
How many variables are live on entry to the block that begins at label L1? (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A variable is live on entry to the loop header if some execution starting there uses its current value before redefining it. Redefinitions inside the loop remove variables from the set; uses keep them in.

Step by step.

  1. The block at L1 tests i and n, so both are live on entry.
  2. s is read at line 4 (s = s + i) and again at line 9 (r = s), so its incoming value is used and it is live.
  3. u is written at line 5 but overwritten at line 7 before any use, so the value written at line 5 is never read; u is not live on entry to the loop.
  4. r is defined at line 9 and never read, so it is not live either.
  5. The live set is {i, n, s}, which has 3 variables.

Final answer. 3 variables (i, n and s).

Common misconception. Counting u because it appears twice in the loop. A definition that is overwritten before any use generates no liveness at the loop header.

Takeaway. Ask of each variable: is the value it holds on entry to the loop actually read before being overwritten? Only those count.

CD-U5-Q50MCQhard

A variable x is live at a point p. Which of the following statements is TRUE?

  1. On every path from p the variable x is used before it is redefined.
  2. On every path from p the variable x is redefined.
  3. There is at least one path from p to a use of x on which x is not redefined first.
  4. The variable x is used at the point p itself.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Liveness is existential over paths. A variable is live at a point when some continuation uses its current value before overwriting it; it does not have to be used on every continuation, and it need not be used at that point at all.

Step by step.

  1. “Live” is defined as: there exists a path from the point to a use, with no definition of the variable in between. That is option (C) verbatim.
  2. Option (A) strengthens “some path” to “every path”, which would make liveness a must property and would discard most of the information register allocation relies on.
  3. Option (B) is not implied at all; a variable can be live forever and never redefined.
  4. Option (D) is too strong: liveness is about a future use, not a use at the point itself.

Final answer. Option (C).

Common misconception. Reading liveness as “used on all paths”. That universal reading is a different and much rarer property, and it would make almost no variable live after a branch.

Takeaway. For every data-flow fact, write down its quantifier: some path (may, union) or every path (must, intersection).

What this test contains

25 questions on Unit 3 (Syntax Analysis: Bottom-Up Parsing), Set 1 of 2 of the second pass over this unit. All four subtopics are covered: LR(0) items and the canonical collection (7) — the GOTO and CLOSURE definitions, why the parser stack carries states, counting states and completed items, and which grammars survive with no lookahead at all; SLR(1) parsing and conflicts (6) — FOLLOW-based reductions, the two kinds of conflict and how a table resolves them, and why FOLLOW is too coarse a lookahead; LR(1) and LALR(1) parsers (6) — lookahead propagation, merging by core, why a merge can only create reduce/reduce conflicts, and a grammar that is LR(1) but not LALR(1); and operator-precedence parsing (6) — the three relations, the moves they trigger, a full parse trace, precedence functions, and the unary-minus failure. Composition: 15 MCQ, 5 MSQ, 5 NAT; difficulty 10 easy, 10 medium, 5 hard; 60 minutes, 35 marks. MCQ carries 1 mark, MSQ and NAT carry 2 marks, there is no negative marking, and an MSQ scores only when the exact set of correct options is selected. This is a practice marking scheme, not the official GATE scheme.

Concept map used to build the questions

  • LR(0) items and the canonical collection. An item records how much of a right side has been recognised; CLOSURE adds the productions of a nonterminal the dot is about to enter, and GOTO moves the dot over one symbol from a whole state. A completed item licenses a reduce, so a grammar is LR(0) exactly when no state is asked to both shift and reduce or to reduce by two productions. The states of the collection are the viable prefixes of the grammar.
  • SLR(1) parsing and conflicts. SLR(1) adds one global set per nonterminal: a reduce by A → α is entered in the columns FOLLOW(A). A shift/reduce conflict is a genuine choice between two parses or a precedence question; a reduce/reduce conflict is a symptom of an ambiguous or non-LALR(1) grammar. Because FOLLOW is computed once for the whole grammar, SLR(1) can report conflicts on grammars that a lookahead-carrying construction handles.
  • LR(1) and LALR(1) parsers. An LR(1) item carries the terminal that may follow the reduced string, so the same production can be reduced with different lookaheads in different states. Merging states with the same core unions those lookaheads, which keeps the table the size of the LR(0) table but can create a reduce/reduce conflict — never a shift/reduce one, because the cores, and therefore the shifts, are identical.
  • Operator-precedence parsing. With no ε-production and no two adjacent nonterminals, every decision can be taken between the topmost terminal on the stack and the current input terminal, using three relations read off the right sides. It is a shift-reduce parser with the states removed, so it is small and fast but cannot express grammars in which one terminal plays two roles.
  • Connections. Unit 2 built the FIRST and FOLLOW sets that SLR(1) reuses; the lookahead discipline of LR(1) is the same idea carried one step further. The handle notion reappears in Unit 4 as syntax-directed translation, where a reduction is the moment a semantic action fires.

Source categories

Each question carries a data-source tag. STANDARD_TEXTBOOK marks questions built on the standard treatment of bottom-up parsing (Aho–Lam–Sethi–Ullman, Compilers: Principles, Techniques and Tools), written in original wording. ORIGINAL_SYNTHESIS marks independently designed GATE-style practice. No question here is presented as an official GATE question: no bottom-up-parsing previous-year question has been verified against an official paper and answer key for this unit, so none is claimed.

Only a question carrying a verified year, paper and official answer key is ever called an official GATE question.

Provenance: no question here is presented as an official GATE question unless its year, paper and official answer key are recorded in the Question Bank tab. All other items are practice questions written for this series.
